Judges Scientific · JDG-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Judges Scientific memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Judges Scientific was written: 53,314 words, against 11,966 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 26,486 words · written 23 October 2026, 11:09 to 11:29 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components

Reading notes for the Consumer (data checks that change how the files should be used)

  1. The latest Earnings Review can be relied on. It covers the six months to 30 June 2026: a trading update on 23 July 2026 and interim results on 23 September 2026. That is the same period as FactSet's last reported period (06/2026). The trading update and the interims give slightly different figures (H1 revenue £55.5m against £55.7m; adjusted EPS "about 40p" against 39.0p). Use the interim figures: revenue £55.7m, adjusted operating profit £4.8m, an adjusted operating margin of 8.6%, adjusted EPS 39.0p.
  2. Bases. The company's "adjusted operating profit" is the same basis as the brokers' EBIT. For FY25, adjusted operating profit of £28.0m plus about £4.7m of depreciation gives the brokers'-basis EBITDA of £32.7m. FactSet's history rows labelled "EBIT (adjusted)" and "gross profit" are amortisation-burdened, standardised lines (for example 8.8% in FY25). They are not the company's adjusted margin and must not be used as operating margin history. The correct adjusted operating margin series is 26.6% (FY22), 25.6% (FY23), 20.9% (FY24), 19.2% (FY25), 20.4% (H1 25), 18.1% (H2 25, derived) and 8.6% (H1 26).
  3. FactSet's history "free cash flow" line appears to be stated after dividends. It reconciles exactly to operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex less dividends paid: FY25 26.4 − 5.4 − 7.1 = 13.9; FY24 25.3 − 5.0 − 6.5 = 13.8. So the peer table's "FCF/net income" of 2.5x for FY25 is not a conversion measure. On my reconciliation, FY25 pre-dividend free cash flow was about £21m, against adjusted net income of about £18m.
  4. Anchor numbers for the model. On the company's adjusted basis: - Revenue: FY23 £136.1m, FY24 £133.6m, FY25 £145.8m (H1 £70.2m, H2 £75.6m), H1 26 £55.7m. - Adjusted EPS: 374.6p, 283.4p, 275.3p (H1 141.4p, H2 about 134p), H1 26 39.0p. - FactSet consensus (median, only three brokers): FY26 revenue £121m, EBIT £20.3m, EPS 199p; FY27 £128m, £22.0m, 219p; FY28 £138m, £23.8m, 248p. - Implied H2 2026: revenue about £65m (14% below H2 25), EBIT about £15.5m (a margin of about 24%), EPS about 160p.
  5. Price and valuation. The price is 3,425p to 3,450p (21 to 22 October 2026). The market capitalisation is about £230m and enterprise value about £274m. On FY26 consensus that is about 11.1x EV/EBITDA, 13.5x EV/EBIT and 14.5x to 14.7x P/E. The 24-month-forward P/E sits at the 0th percentile of five years (range 13.8x to 35.7x, median 21.8x) and the 9th percentile of ten years.
  6. A method warning on the Risks report. Its competing-hypotheses matrix names FALSE FRIEND the winner, and the July memo treated that as its single most consequential finding. FALSE FRIEND also wins the same test in the Risks reports for Halma, Renishaw and Jenoptik, and is the main alternative at Vaisala. The Wisdom Library entry "The Vaguest Explanation Wins A Fewest-Contradictions Contest" describes exactly this bias. The verdict is a method artefact, not evidence peculiar to Judges. The specific mechanisms it names are real but individually modest, and I weigh them on their own merits below: - shipments deferred by customers sitting inside the order book; - cash conversion flattered by Geotek advance payments and by stretched creditors; - capitalisation of development costs.

External change forces

❌ D3610 questions
C-0130C-0238C-0345C-0428C-0545C-0658C-0732C-0848C-0940C-1035

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external environment has been the worst in Judges' 20-year listed history. It was political in origin (United States research funding, the Chinese tax-exemption procedure), compounded by Geotek's deferral and offshore-wind postponements, and set against a sector whose winners are riding semiconductor and AI capex that Judges barely touches.

The trend has turned from deteriorating to stabilising: orders fell 18% in Q1 2026 and rose about 15% in Q3. But none of the forces has turned into a tailwind. China's catch-up is timing, the United States is in another stopgap year at best, and macro is hostile to small, levered UK shares.

For the model, the external forces justify:

  • treating FY26 as the trough;
  • a mechanical, base-effect recovery in FY27 (organic high single digit) rather than a structural one;
  • no Geotek coring before FY28;
  • a modest wage-led cost headwind and a material, lagged currency sensitivity.

The skew over 12 months is positive relative to the depressed base, because several independent headwinds could ease at once. The cone remains wide, because the dominant driver is political and has no lead indicator better than the order book.

C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility?

❌ D30

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. Demand is at or just past a policy-driven cyclical trough. It is not in structural collapse, and visibility is poor: there is nothing close to two years of it.

The last 18 months saw the worst organic demand run in the group's history:

  • organic US sales fell 22% in FY25, and US orders fell 23% in 2025;
  • group order intake fell 12% in H1 2026 (Q1 −18%, Q2 roughly flat);
  • China and Hong Kong orders fell 47%, Europe 20% and the United States 7%;
  • the UK rose 5% and the Rest of World 12%.

The last six months show a turn in orders but not yet in revenue. Year-to-date intake recovered from −12% at June to −1% by late September, which implies Q3 intake about 15% above the prior year. The order book was 17.3 weeks at 30 June, against 15.7 weeks at December and 17.4 weeks a year earlier. Revenue in H1 was still down 21% (about 9% to 12% excluding the absent Geotek coring). Customers have ordered and then asked for shipment to be delayed.

The demand mix splits into three different kinds of force.

  1. United States academic demand (about half of the roughly 25% of revenue normally sold in the US; academia and research institutes are about two-thirds of group revenue on management's own September 2025 split). This is political and cyclical, with a slow structural drift underneath it: federal R&D has fallen from 1.86% to 0.63% of GDP. The National Institutes of Health spending plan was approved only in March 2026. Q3 2026 grants were down 8% year on year (up 3% on a trailing twelve-month basis). The FY2027 budget request cuts the National Institutes of Health by about 10% and the National Science Foundation by 54%. The courts blocked the 15% indirect-cost cap. The base case is another stopgap year, so this leg is bottoming but not recovering.
  2. China. This is mainly administrative timing: a once-in-four-years change to research and development tax-exemption procedures, also seen at JEOL, which management says is now resolving. Underneath sits slower Chinese science spending (up 4% on a trailing twelve-month basis, against 8% in 2023) and structural localisation, so the catch-up is a refill, not new demand.
  3. Commercial and industrial demand (semiconductors, batteries, Geotek services). This is resilient and positive, but it carried only eight of 25 businesses into growth in H1.

Offshore wind (geotechnical subsidiaries) deteriorated further in H1. Its timing is unknowable.

Visibility amounts to a 17-week order book plus a Q4 that is unusually heavily weighted. The next real evidence is the January 2027 trading update.

For the model. Treat 2026 as the trough year. Treat 2027 organic growth as recovery from a depressed base, partly mechanical: H1 2026 comparisons are −21%, and China orders deferred from 2026 will ship. Do not assume the United States returns to the 2023 run-rate within the forecast window. Do not model a multi-year cyclical upswing.

C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

❌ D38

Judgement. Competitive intensity is rising slowly against Judges on three fronts. In most of its 25 niches the change is incremental rather than acute, and it bites in specific businesses rather than across the portfolio.

  1. Product competition from Chinese suppliers. Chinese rivals are moving up the quality curve. They price 20% to 30% below Western brands (seen at Renishaw), are helped by state subsidies of about 3% of revenue, and benefit from grant categories that exclude foreign instruments in China. Judges' UK-made export model has no "Made in China" answer.
  2. Scale and software competition from large consolidators. Halma, Oxford Instruments, Waters and Danaher are building integrated workflow, software and service platforms. Zeus says market concentration is increasing. Halma's EBIT margin is 22.7% and rising while Judges' is falling.
  3. Competition for acquisitions. Private equity is active for targets above £1m of EBITDA. The chief executive confirmed in 2026 that Chinese buyers are acquiring UK scientific businesses for their intellectual property. SDI Group's chief executive notes that off-market sourcing gets harder as platforms scale.

Where it has already bitten:

  • Armfield's share of its market fell from 40% to about 15% since 2016, losing to GUNT, TecQuipment and Edibon;
  • Fire Testing Technology's margin fell from 30% to about 15% as cheaper rivals and a software lag hurt it;
  • Scientifica discontinued its multiphoton line.

The counterweight. In the core vacuum and materials niches (UHV Design, Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal, EWB, Korvus, Geotek core logging) shares rarely move more than 1% a year, competition is on specification rather than price, and there is no evidence of price wars. Academic customers defer purchases rather than force price cuts.

For the model. No group-wide price erosion. A persistent drag of perhaps 1 to 2 percentage points a year on organic growth in the three or four weakest businesses. A slow upward drift in the acquisition multiples Judges must pay, which weakens the inorganic leg of EPS growth.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

❌ D45

Judgement. Laboratory automation, artificial intelligence and software-led workflow platforms are strengthening disruption forces. Their effect on Judges is mildly negative over three to five years and close to neutral over the next 18 months.

The threat. Danaher (with NVIDIA), Waters (AI Link) and BICO are building closed loops of instruments, robotics and software. In that model the vendor that controls the data and software layer captures the customer relationship and the pricing power, and stand-alone hardware is left as a component. Judges has no group-wide software, connectivity or recurring-software revenue disclosed. Fire Testing Technology shows the cost of falling behind on software: it needed remedial R&D. Longer instrument lives (the Agilent expert) and AI that reduces exploratory measurements can slow replacement cycles.

The opposing evidence. Physical validation remains indispensable. Many of Judges' instruments rest on physics-based measurement: vacuum manipulation, cryo-cooling, coring and core logging, sample preparation. Automation increases measurement intensity in semiconductors, where gate-all-around nodes need 20% to 30% more metrology steps. Geotek and Rockwash digitise geological cores into machine-learning-ready data, which is a genuine opportunity. No source identifies a substitute technology for Judges' core categories, and the risk is application-specific, not portfolio-wide.

For the model. No explicit technology haircut in 2026 to 2028. Treat software and AI as a factor that widens the gap between Judges' better businesses and its worse ones, and as a reason the R&D intensity of 7.0% of revenue, below peers at 8% to 10%, cannot be cut to rescue margins.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

❌ D28

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is the single most important external force on Judges. Over the last 18 months it was decisively negative. Over the next six months it is mixed, with the worst administrative effects fading but no positive policy catalyst.

Negative:

  • United States research funding. The proposed FY26 budget implied a 42% cut to the combined National Institutes of Health and National Science Foundation budget. Congress rejected it, but grant obligation was frozen and delayed: the NIH fell $1.5bn behind in 2025 and its FY26 spend plan was approved only on 17 March 2026. The FY2027 request again proposes cuts (NIH about −10%, NSF −54%).
  • China. The research and development tax-exemption procedure caused the 47% fall in H1 orders. Separately, structural domestic-substitution rules in grant categories persist.
  • United Kingdom tax. Higher corporation tax and employer National Insurance cost about £3m a year, which management puts at about 10% of operating profit.
  • Export controls, rare-earth licensing, product-safety rules (RoHS, the draft PFAS restriction). These are small compliance costs.

Positive or easing:

  • the China procedure is resolving (management and JEOL both expect a catch-up);
  • the US indirect-cost cap was blocked by injunction (affirmed 5 January 2026) and rescinded at the Department of Energy;
  • Horizon Europe gained an extra €500m for 2026 to 2027 (€14.8bn), and the UK has rejoined Horizon;
  • the UK Patent Box cut the H1 2026 adjusted tax rate to 18%;
  • US tariffs on China could make UK exporters relatively more competitive.

Structural risk. The EU's 2028 to 2034 framework programme earmarks about 4% of €154.9bn for defence, and sovereign deficits threaten research austerity.

For the model. The political variable sets the slope of academic demand, so it is the main swing factor in 2027 revenue. Model a slow US academic recovery from FY27, a one-off China catch-up in Q4 2026 to H1 2027 that is not a run-rate increase, and a tax rate of about 18% to 21% that depends on Patent Box.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

❌ D45

Judgement. The net effect is a modest headwind, dominated by wages and taxes rather than materials. Supply availability is no longer a constraint.

Costs. Staff costs are £48.5m (FY25, about 33% of revenue, up from £43.3m, about 12%), almost entirely in sterling and in a tight market for specialist engineers. Employer National Insurance and corporation tax rises add about £3m a year. Raw materials and consumables are £44.8m (about 31% of revenue): steel, copper, Hastelloy, vacuum and optoelectronic components. These are monitored and largely passed through; subsidiaries pass on cost increases in 90% to 95% of cases. The Iran conflict had no material effect on input costs or logistics up to September 2026.

Supply. Lead times have normalised and inventories have been cut (by £3.0m to £3.3m in FY25). Armfield is still running a multi-year insourcing project after its suppliers exited post-Covid. Some subsidiaries face single-source specialist component suppliers. Chinese rare-earth export licensing is a watch item. Small subsidiaries have little buying power.

For the model. Assume wage inflation of 4% to 5% on the staff base, about £2m a year, which needs price increases of about 1.5% to 2% of revenue just to stand still on margin. Assume materials are neutral. Price pass-through is weaker in academic channels while budgets are frozen. This, not materials, is why margins compressed in FY25 even before volume fell.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C58

Judgement. The order cycle turned in Q3 2026. The revenue cycle troughed in H1 2026 and should turn in Q4 2026 to H1 2027. The earnings cycle troughs in FY26, with the risk concentrated in Q4 delivery.

Evidence for the turn:

  • Q1 2026 orders −18%, Q2 flat, Q3 about +15% (implied by year-to-date going from −12% to −1%);
  • the order book back to 17.3 weeks, level with a year ago;
  • China's administrative bottleneck resolving;
  • comparisons in 2027 that are very easy (H1 2026 revenue −21%, EPS −72%).

Evidence against a durable turn. Year-to-date intake is still slightly negative, not growing. The Q3 recovery is partly deferred orders being released (the "refill" pattern in the Wisdom Library entry "Use, Refill or Price") rather than budget-backed new demand. US flows remain uneven. Europe was −20% in H1. The Geotek coring that normally lifts the cycle has moved to 2028 or later.

For the model. Book-to-bill is above 1.0 in 2026 because revenue lags intake. If FY26 intake ends near FY25's ex-coring level (about £125m to £135m) and the order book stays near 16 to 17 weeks, revenue should run ahead of the £121m consensus over the next 12 months, either in Q4 2026 or as a spill into FY27. The 2027 organic growth rate is therefore likely to be high single digit, mostly mechanical, before any underlying recovery. The turn is real but young. The January 2027 trading update is the confirmation test.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

❌ D32

Judgement. Macro bites hardest at the company level, through public research budgets and through the discount rate on a small, levered, thinly traded share. It bites less at sector level, where commercial and semiconductor demand is strong, and least at industry level.

Company:

  • fiscal stress and sovereign deficits (France at 152 basis points over Germany, US deficits) feed directly into the research budgets that drive about two-thirds of Judges' revenue;
  • sterling at about 1.34 to the dollar (forecasts 1.31 to 1.38 by mid-2027) acts on a cost base that is about 80% to 90% in sterling against about 50% of revenue in dollars and 33% in euros;
  • higher rates raise the cost of the roughly £45m to £48m of net debt and the funding cost of acquisitions.

Sector. Test and measurement is up 25.2% over twelve months, but split. Renishaw, Vaisala and Jenoptik are rising on chip and data-centre capex; Judges and Halma are falling.

Market regime. The current regime is a discount-rate shock: a US 10-year yield of about 5.1%, the Fragility tilt set to Double Underweight, and a market refusing re-ratings. That penalises a £230m AIM stock trading about $1m a day with leverage of 2.1x, whatever its fundamentals.

For the model.

  • FX. The Risks report's sensitivities (10% sterling move against the dollar is −£1.4m EBITDA and −13.8p EPS) apply the group margin to translated revenue and so understate transaction exposure for a business with mostly sterling costs. On my estimate, a 10% sustained sterling appreciation against both dollar and euro, once 12-month hedges roll off and before repricing, could cost a high single-digit £m of EBIT. Treat currency as a material, under-quantified sensitivity and phase it in with a one-year lag.
  • Interest. Assume about £3m to £3.5m.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

❌ D48

Judgement. The sector's strongest accelerating drivers are semiconductor wafer-fab equipment (forecast +12% in 2026 and +22% in 2027), AI data-centre build-out, battery and energy-storage R&D, and defence. They touch Judges only at the edges, so they are a real but small offset, not an accelerator of group demand.

Evidence. Eight of 25 businesses grew in H1 2026 on semiconductor and battery demand. Rest of World revenue excluding coring grew 9%. Renishaw's Position Measurement grew 26% and Oxford Instruments' commercial orders grew 25.4%, which shows how strong the driver is for businesses positioned in it. Judges has no disclosed data-centre or AI exposure, and semiconductor exposure is sub-scale; the July memo's sources found no AI tailwind in any subsidiary. Management's pivot to commercial and industrial markets ("four to five times larger than academia") is the right direction but will take years to change the mix.

For the model. Assume commercial and industrial sales (about one-third of revenue) grow mid to high single digits through 2027 to 2028, subject to the semiconductor cycle. Do not apply peers' 20% to 40% growth rates. The value of the driver is mainly that it lowers the share of revenue exposed to academia over time.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

❌ D40

Judgement. Four further dynamics matter. Two are clearly negative (Geotek coring timing, sentiment and liquidity in UK small caps), one is negative but fading (offshore wind), and one is positive but slow (the acquisition-target pool and seller behaviour).

  1. Geotek coring. These are lumpy, high-margin government oceanographic expeditions, historically about three years in four. Each is worth about 50p of EPS on broker sensitivities (about a quarter of FY26 EPS). The next expedition has slipped from 2026 to early 2027 and now to no earlier than 2028. The FY26 to FY27 consensus correctly excludes coring, so it is upside, not risk, to current numbers. It remains binary and customer-controlled; contract signature is the dated milestone.
  2. Offshore wind. Postponements hit the geotechnical testing businesses and worsened in H1 2026. There is no visibility on reversal; treat it as a flat-to-down segment through 2027.
  3. Small-cap market conditions. Coverage is thin (Jefferies dropped coverage in April 2026; three brokers remain). The share is down 40.5% over twelve months and 24.2% over three, sits at the 4th percentile for relative strength, and its 200-day average has fallen for twelve months.
  4. Seller behaviour. The longest acquisition-free period since 2021 is put down to sellers delaying in uncertain markets, while private equity and Chinese strategic buyers contest the pool of targets. When markets stabilise, seller supply tends to return, the counter-cyclical pattern seen after 2020 (Armfield, CoolLED).
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

❌ D35

Thematic drivers in turn:

  • Research budgets. Strong headwind, now bottoming. The decisive driver for Judges.
  • Industrial and semiconductor capex. Moderate tailwind but small exposure.
  • Regulation-led demand. Weak tailwind: fire testing standards, ISO and environmental compliance.
  • AI and automation. Neutral now, mildly negative long term.
  • Chinese competition and localisation. Moderate structural headwind.
  • Energy transition and offshore wind. Headwind.
  • Macro (rates, sterling). Moderate headwind to earnings and to the multiple.

By level:

  • Value chain. Customers are capital-constrained and deferring; suppliers are neutral; the routes to market are stable.
  • Industry. Concentration is rising towards scale and software players.
  • Sector. Sharply split, and Judges sits on the wrong side, with academic rather than AI exposure.

Overall judgement. The external headwinds were strong and dominant over the last 18 months. They are now plateauing and slightly easing. Tailwinds are weak and narrow. The external picture is net negative but improving at the margin. The single most important external fact is that research-budget weakness, Geotek's deferral and offshore-wind delays all hit at once, so a partial easing of any one of them, especially China and the US academic channel, has an outsized effect from a trough base. The asymmetry is therefore better than the level of headwinds suggests.

Internal change forces

❌ D4513 questions
C-1150C-1255C-1330C-1458C-1540C-1655C-1742C-1835C-1940C-2055C-8632C-8745C-2145

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judges is mid-way through a deliberate institutional transformation: from a founder-run, hands-off acquisition vehicle to a Halma-style professionally managed group with an interventionist centre. In its design this is the right response to the founder's succession and to the failures at Armfield, Fire Testing Technology and Scientifica.

The timing is poor:

  • the CEO, COO and CFO all change within 18 months, during the deepest downturn on record;
  • the acquisition engine, historically about 12 of the 19 points of revenue CAGR, has stalled since 2024;
  • the balance sheet (2.1x) and a 1.1x-covered dividend constrain its restart;
  • the only near-term earnings contributions from internal change are modest (the £2m cost action, Patent Box, working capital).

For the model, internal change supports lower risk of future surprises and a gradual margin repair at underperformers. It does not support any acquisition contribution before H2 2027, any re-rating catalyst from guidance before March 2027, or any rapid return to the 30% ROTIC target. The decisive internal unknown is whether the new deal team can source deals at about 5x EBIT without the founder. That evidence will not arrive within six months.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders or other key actors changed, and what does the change signal?

C50

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. Leadership change is near-total and concentrated in 2025 to 2026. Together it signals the deliberate "Halma-isation" of a founder-run model: institutional depth replacing one exceptional deal-maker. It is credible in design but introduces real execution risk at the worst point in the cycle, and the CFO's departure is the most consequential element for the model.

Who changed:

  • Chief executive. Founder David Cicurel, chief executive since 2002, became Non-Executive Chair on 9 February 2026. Dr Tim Prestidge (ex-Renishaw and Halma, at Judges since February 2023) became chief executive.
  • Chief operating officer. Mark Lavelle left the board in May 2025 and retired in September 2026.
  • Chief financial officer. Brad Ormsby, in post since 2015 and the architect of cash discipline and the main investor contact, resigned on 23 September 2026 and stays until 23 September 2027. No successor has been named.
  • New central roles:
  • Rik Armitage, Group Acquisitions Executive from July 2025, ex-Oxford Instruments corporate development;
  • John Dunne, Portfolio Chief Executive, ex-Halma;
  • Dr Ian Wilcock, Group Commercial Director from September 2024, ex-Oxford Instruments and Renishaw.
  • Governance. An Executive Committee (2025), a Nomination Committee (January 2026), a new Audit Committee chair (Sue Nyman, May 2026), and a change of auditor from BDO to RSM for FY2026 after Financial Reporting Council enquiries led to restatements.
  • Major holders. Unchanged and consulted. Cicurel keeps about 9.3% and stays involved in deals. There are no activists.

What it signals. The positive reading is reduced key-person risk, more operating intervention in underperformers, and more acquisition capacity. Prestidge bought shares at 4,335p in April 2026. The negative reading is that the founder's personal sourcing and judgement of sellers, which underpinned 6x EBITA buying and a 29% average ROTIC, is not yet replicated. Three of the top four executives have turned over within 18 months. A new CFO will set FY27 to FY28 guidance and inherits a 2.1x geared balance sheet.

For the model. Expect FY27 guidance (March 2027) to be conservative. Do not assume acquisitions resume until the deal team shows a completed transaction.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced?

C55

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The strategy (buy niche scientific-instrument exporters at 3x to 7x EBIT, hold for ever, run them decentralised) is unchanged. The priorities within it have shifted. The change is adequately articulated and well resourced centrally, but it is incremental and its effects will take two to three years to show.

The shifts:

  1. From passive ownership to active portfolio intervention: talent, product development, manufacturing efficiency, sales effectiveness, financial controls. Each subsidiary has a three-to-five-year plan asking how it could double its EBIT. Underperformers are being fixed through cost cuts, product exits and Armfield's insourcing.
  2. A deliberate move of subsidiary selling effort towards commercial and industrial markets.
  3. Building an acquisition function that does not depend on the founder.
  4. Defensive balance-sheet management: the facility extended to July 2030 and acquisitions paused.

Resourcing. Central costs rose about 75% in FY25. R&D was protected at 7.0% of revenue (£10.2m). Senior hires came from Halma and Oxford Instruments.

What is absent: a group-wide software or connectivity platform, any portfolio pruning (management keeps a zero-divestment stance even after Armfield was written down), and quantified mid-term financial targets beyond 30% ROTIC.

For the model. This is a better-run version of the same machine, not a new machine. Margin recovery depends on volume. The intervention model's main contribution is narrowing losses at the three or four weakest businesses, a gradual uplift of perhaps 1 to 2 percentage points of group margin over three years if executed.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised?

❌ D30

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. Guidance has not been raised. It was rebased down by about a third in January 2026, from roughly 300p to a range of 200p to 250p of adjusted EPS, and has since been held at the bottom of that range (management "in line with market expectations" of about 200p). The only mid-term ambition is the long-standing 30% return on total invested capital. It was reaffirmed but is not credible on any near-term horizon: actual ROTIC was 22.7% in 2023, 17.8% in 2025 and 12.9% in H1 2026. There has been no capital markets day and none is announced. The new chief executive and incoming CFO will set FY27 guidance in March 2027.

Assessment. There is no positive guidance force. The 200p to 250p range was built on explicit conservative inputs (no US recovery, no Geotek coring), so in principle there is room to land above the bottom. The heavy H2 weighting (about 80% of FY EPS) makes that unlikely in practice.

For the model. FY26 guidance equals consensus at about 200p. FY27 is unguided; consensus is 219p to 224p (three brokers). The first credible mid-term framework will probably not come before a new CFO is in post, late 2027.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?

C58

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards are clearly improving from a low-process base. The improvement is substantive in cost discipline, operational oversight and incentive governance, and partial in board governance and reporting quality.

Execution and operations:

  • an Executive Committee and a Portfolio Chief Executive;
  • monthly forecasting (introduced by the former chief operating officer);
  • ISO9001 targeted for all subsidiaries by end-2027;
  • distributor benchmarking;
  • internal audit of every subsidiary at least once every four years, and within 12 months of joining for new acquisitions;
  • more than 90% of subsidiary leadership through the leadership programme.

Cost discipline. About £1.8m to £2.0m was removed at underperforming businesses in H1 2026, mainly through headcount, while about £1.0m was reinvested in talent. Management describes this as targeted, not indiscriminate.

Governance:

  • malus and clawback from January 2026;
  • a Nomination Committee;
  • a new Audit Committee chair;
  • a new auditor after Financial Reporting Council-driven restatements (2024 cash and overdraft grossed up by £1.7m, an £8.3m Geotek-related parent impairment reversed).

Gaps:

  • board independence is only three of eight;
  • the Chair is the former chief executive and chairs the Nomination Committee;
  • the share-option hurdle of 5% EPS growth a year is soft;
  • reporting remains a "black box" with no subsidiary data;
  • management deflects questions on Armfield.

For the model. Improving standards lower the risk of further surprises from inside the portfolio. Do not credit them with margin gains beyond the £2m cost action until evidence appears in the FY26 results.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units?

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. Barely at all. This is a deliberate policy choice and a mild negative.

Judges has made no disposals. Management is rehabilitating Armfield rather than selling it, even after the parent company wrote its investment down by £7.0m and its goodwill was fully impaired. Simplification is limited to product-line exits inside businesses: Scientifica discontinued its non-differentiated multiphoton microscopy range, and Armfield is insourcing. The perimeter of 25 businesses in two segments is stable.

Why it matters.

  • Low-return units dilute group ROTIC. In 2025 twelve businesses regressed against seven that progressed, and the three troubled units (Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, Scientifica) absorb central bandwidth.
  • The zero-divestment ethos supports seller trust in Judges as a buyer, which is part of its sourcing advantage.
  • Peers show the other path: Halma sold NovaBone and Hydreka; Jenoptik is seeking to sell Prodomax.

For the model. No disposal proceeds and no mix uplift from pruning. Assume the underperformers move from a drag to roughly break-even contribution growth over 2027 to 2028 rather than being removed.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. Judges is entering new markets modestly and sensibly, mainly by redirecting existing subsidiaries towards commercial and industrial research rather than by geographic leaps.

Evidence:

  • management directs subsidiaries to commercial and industrial research markets, estimated at four to five times the size of academia (semiconductor process, battery development, energy storage, industrial process optimisation);
  • four subsidiaries hit record FY25 performance targeting commercial applications, and eight grew in H1 2026;
  • geographic steps are bolt-on and low risk: full ownership of Geotek do Brasil (the 18% minority bought for £1.9m in December 2025, immediately earnings enhancing) and international expansion within Lavelle's later group role;
  • product renewal continues: Korvus HEX systems and Fire Testing Technology's new cone calorimeter (delayed a year).

Why it is sensible. It uses existing technology and channels and reduces exposure to public funding without big capital bets.

Why it is slow. Industrial customers are themselves deferring big-ticket capital. Subsidiaries are small, with limited sales reach (direct sales only in the United States; distributors elsewhere). Oxford Instruments needed several years and scale to rebalance to 37% semiconductor orders.

For the model. Allow the commercial share of revenue to rise by perhaps 2 to 3 percentage points a year. That marginally lowers cyclicality but does not change 2026 to 2028 growth materially.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?

❌ D42

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. Pricing is nominal and defensive, not real. Real price increases above cost inflation are at best latent in the strongest niches, and are not evidenced in the numbers.

Evidence for pricing power:

  • management says about 85% of businesses can pass on cost increases, and subsidiaries pass on input costs in 90% to 95% of cases;
  • gross margins above 50% in proprietary niches;
  • Korvus prices freely because its instruments have no clear market benchmarks;
  • peers (Renishaw tariff surcharges converted to permanent rises of about 1% of revenue; Oxford Instruments repricing its US order book; Jenoptik raising prices twice on scarce AI optics) show the industry can price.

Evidence against:

  • the adjusted operating margin fell 170 basis points in FY25 with "price realisation lagging input inflation";
  • academic buyers are capped by budgets and resist increases (Jefferies survey, April 2026);
  • Armfield's inflexible pricing contributed to share loss;
  • no price contribution has ever been quantified;
  • the FY25 report notes no price or discount disclosure in the period.

For the model. Assume price of about 2% to 3% a year, roughly offsetting wage inflation and leaving margin unchanged. Treat any real pricing gain as an upside sensitivity confined to commercial and industrial and premium vacuum niches.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they?

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. Acquisitions are stepping down, not up. There were zero acquisitions in FY25 and H1 2026, the longest gap since 2021; the last were Luciol, Rockwash and Teer in 2024, plus the Geotek do Brasil minority in December 2025. Discipline on price is intact. The integration and selection record has deteriorated, and capacity is constrained.

Discipline. Management keeps to 3x to 7x EBIT (typically 4x to 6x, about 5x) and refuses to overpay. The pool is large: about 2,000 UK targets and about 100 opportunities a year.

Sensibleness. The strategic logic is unchanged and sound. In fragmented niches the acquisition arbitrage is widest for small buyers paying 3x to 7x when borrowing costs 5% to 6% (the Industry Digest).

Record:

  • Geotek (£80m, 7x EBIT, May 2022, the largest deal) brought lumpiness and pulled ROTIC down;
  • Armfield and Scientifica, the third and fourth largest deals, underperformed: Armfield lost share, and £8.7m of parent impairments were taken in 2025;
  • Rockwash missed its earn-out and its goodwill was written down £2.0m;
  • Korvus, Sircal, CoolLED and Deben succeeded. The Wisdom Library's "Cockroach Pattern at M&A Integration Level" applies: a second visible failure after Armfield and Rockwash would signal systemic problems, though Judges' light-touch model means failures surface slowly through impairments.

Capacity. Net debt is £45.3m at 2.1x EBITDA, against a 3.0x covenant. £58.6m is drawn on a £90m committed revolving facility, leaving £31.4m of committed headroom, plus an uncommitted £50m accordion. Dividends take about £8m to £8.5m a year.

For the model. Exclude acquisitions from the base case for FY26 to FY27. As an upside sensitivity, each £10m deployed at about 5x EBIT adds about £2m of EBIT and, after interest at about 6% and tax, about 15p to 17p of EPS. Resumption is unlikely before H2 2027.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns, or is management increasingly mooting either?

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used more, but defensively. Leverage rose to 2.1x because earnings fell, not because of investment. Shareholder returns are being maintained ahead of earnings. Management explicitly rejects buybacks.

The dividend. The interim was raised 10% to 36.0p despite adjusted cover of only 1.1x (4.3x a year earlier). Cicurel conceded that this cover "is clearly not sustainable". This is the 19th or 20th year of increases of at least 10%; the dividend CAGR is 21%. Consensus has FY26 DPS of 126p (+10%) on EPS of about 200p, cover of about 1.6x, and a cash cost of about £8.4m against consensus operating cash flow of about £21m and capex of about £5m.

Debt. Net debt rose from £42.6m to £45.3m in H1. The facility was extended to July 2030 with covenants unchanged (3.0x gearing, 3.0x interest cover).

Buybacks. The board has "considered" them but regards them as less effective than acquisitions, organic investment and dividends. That holds even though the shares trade at about 14.5x forward earnings, an earnings yield of about 7% against debt at about 6%.

Assessment. The dividend promise supports confidence and the yield (about 3.7%). It consumes cash that would otherwise rebuild acquisition headroom, and it creates a sentiment cliff if EBITDA disappoints, because a cut would break a 20-year record.

For the model. Assume +10% DPS in FY26 and FY27, conditional on FY26 EPS of at least about 190p. Assume no buybacks. Deleveraging to about 1.5x to 1.7x by end-FY27 absent acquisitions.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

C55

Judgement. Five further positive forces are present. Together they are worth roughly 15% to 25% of FY26 EPS over two to three years if delivered, but each is modest alone.

  1. Patent Box. Enhanced use cut the H1 2026 adjusted tax rate to 18%, against 25% UK headline. Shore Capital credits it with about 30p of net H2 benefit. It is real but depends on qualifying intellectual property.
  2. Working-capital release. Working capital is 16% of sales (FY25) against a 10% aspiration. Moving to target would release about £8m, worth about 18% of net debt. That funds acquisitions or deleveraging and cuts interest by about £0.5m.
  3. Full-year effect of the H1 2026 cost actions (about £2m gross annualised at underperformers), plus a further turnaround at Fire Testing Technology (new products) and Armfield (insourcing complete).
  4. China procedure resolved. Shipments delayed in 2026 convert in Q4 2026 to H1 2027.
  5. Geotek services and the digitisation of cores (Rockwash) growing, and Geotek do Brasil fully owned.

Plausible but not yet present: a high-quality acquisition by the new deal team, and a coring contract signed in 2027 for a 2028 expedition.

For the model. Include Patent Box (tax rate about 18% to 20%) and the cost actions in the base case. Treat working-capital release as a deleveraging lever, and the acquisition and Geotek items as upside scenarios.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

❌ D32

Judgement. Several negative corporate developments are present. The most model-relevant are the CFO's departure, the dividend and leverage squeeze, and the risk of further impairments in the portfolio.

  1. CFO resignation. Effective September 2027, no successor named. He was the executive most associated with financial discipline and investor relationships.
  2. Leverage of 2.1x and dividend cover of 1.1x reduce flexibility; another earnings miss would force a choice between the dividend record and acquisition capacity.
  3. ROTIC at 12.9% and falling, with £8.7m of 2025 parent impairments (Armfield, Scientifica) and goodwill impairments of Armfield (£2.3m) and Rockwash (£2.0m) showing selection and integration slippage. A second cockroach is plausible: Fire Testing Technology's margin halved.
  4. Talent and founder attrition. Turnover was 19% in 2025, against 16% in 2024 and a UK average of 15%. Experts flag founders leaving after earn-outs, and it is the least-monitored failure mode.
  5. Reporting and audit. Financial Reporting Council restatements and an auditor change; development capitalisation up 28% to £1.8m.
  6. Central cost creep. Central costs rose 75% in FY25 to fund the professionalised centre, a permanent overhead drag of perhaps £2m a year versus the founder era.

For the model. Keep central costs at about £5m a year (segment consensus "unallocated" is −£5.1m to −£5.2m). Assume no further write-downs in adjusted figures, but expect statutory volatility. Hold a downside scenario in which FY26 lands at about 165p to 180p and the dividend is held flat in FY27.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. Very little. No controlling actor has signalled a different strategy. The ownership structure makes pressure for change unlikely in the near term.

David Cicurel (about 9.3% beneficial, the largest individual holder) is Chair, chairs the Nomination Committee and remains active in deal-making. He designed the current strategy and repeatedly defends it ("our purpose is to create shareholder value"; growing the dividend 10% a year means "shareholders do not need to sell"). The succession was set after consulting the largest institutional holders, who backed it. There is no activist, no short-seller report and no disclosed short interest. Ralph Elman (Deputy Chair, on the board since 2002) adds continuity.

The only latent sources of change:

  • the new CFO (2027) could reshape capital allocation, for example on dividend policy or buybacks;
  • institutional holders could press on dividend cover or buybacks if the shares stay at about 14x;
  • a bid. The share sits at the bottom of its valuation range, and Chinese and private-equity buyers are active in UK scientific instruments. Cicurel's stake and the board's culture make a recommended sale unlikely, but it is not impossible at a large premium.

For the model. Assume strategic continuity. Treat capital-allocation change (a slower dividend, buybacks) as a low-probability upside to EPS per share and a bid as a tail, neither in the base case.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

❌ D45

Judgement. The positive corporate changes are moderately broad and well resourced, but not yet strong enough to drive the earnings path. They are designed for the next five years, not the next 18 months.

Broad:

  • leadership professionalisation;
  • central acquisition and portfolio capability;
  • cost discipline at underperformers;
  • standards and governance improvements;
  • the commercial pivot;
  • tax optimisation.

Well resourced. Central costs, senior hires, R&D protected at 7%, and a funding line to 2030.

Not strong:

  • no new guidance;
  • no acquisition;
  • the £2m cost action is only about 2% of the fixed cost base;
  • ROTIC still falling;
  • the CFO leaving;
  • capital allocation stretched by a dividend promise.

For the model. Corporate change contributes perhaps 15p to 30p of EPS by FY28 through cost actions, Patent Box and underperformer repair. The rest of any recovery must come from external volume. That supports the July memo's view that internal change cannot outweigh the external shock.
