Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4816 questions
C-2460C-2562C-2655C-2745C-2862C-2960C-3158C-8455C-3238C-3352C-3445C-3545C-3652C-3730C-3840C-3955

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judges' industry structure is genuinely supportive where it competes: small, bounded, concentrated niches; specification-led, margin-first rivals; and high switching costs. Prices have held through the worst downturn in the group's history, which is strong evidence that recovering volume will rebuild margins without price concessions.

The value chain's weakness is upstream and downstream of that structure, not inside it:

  • demand is heavily dependent on politically driven public research budgets (about two-thirds of sales to universities and institutes), and those budgets proved fragile;
  • the market for acquiring niche businesses is getting more contested;
  • the industry is consolidating towards software-enabled, commercially exposed scale players, whose relative strength keeps widening the valuation gap.

Precedents in the sector suggest Judges fits the "trough earnings, turned order book, falling price" setup that typically precedes sharp recoveries in thinly covered, high fixed-cost names. Precedents also warn that turnarounds take two to three years and that serial acquirers with drifting returns de-rate durably.

For the model:

  • niche margins recover with volume;
  • organic growth is capped at about 4% to 7% beyond the FY27 rebound;
  • about 1 percentage point a year of drag from share loss in challenged units and in China;
  • political shocks drive the downside scenarios;
  • acquisitions are an increasingly expensive, later-dated upside rather than a dependable engine.
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C60

Judgement. There are two answers.

Inside its niches. Most Judges subsidiaries hold the number one or two position, because they choose markets small enough to lead. Examples:

  • UHV Design (ultra-high-vacuum manipulation);
  • Oxford Cryosystems (cooling for X-ray diffraction, integrated by the dominant OEMs);
  • Sircal (rare-gas purifiers for spectrometer OEMs);
  • EWB Solutions (edge-welded bellows);
  • Geotek (core logging and pressure coring);
  • Korvus (deposition systems);
  • Thermal Hazard Technology.

Experts describe these as quasi-monopolistic with few competitors. Industry evidence suggests shares in specialised fields rarely shift by more than about 1% a year.

Exceptions:

  • Armfield, now about 15% of education engineering equipment, behind GUNT and contested by TecQuipment and Edibon;
  • Fire Testing Technology, under price pressure;
  • Scientifica, competing with Sutter and Siskiyou in electrophysiology.

As a group. Judges is a small participant in a scientific-instruments market of about $38.6bn. Its revenue is £146m, against Halma at £2.58bn (about 18 times), Renishaw £816m, Spectris about £1.3bn and Oxford Instruments £423m. It is not a scale player in any broad category.

Implication. Niche leadership supports gross margins above 50% and customer stickiness. Group-level smallness means no purchasing or software scale and a high sensitivity to any single funding source.

For the model. Margin and pricing assumptions should be set at niche level (high and stable) for about 20 of the 25 businesses. Assume structurally lower and declining economics for the three or four challenged ones.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution?

C62

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. Concentration is high and steep at the level that matters for Judges, the individual niche. It is moderate in the broader instrument market.

Niche level. The top one or two players typically hold 50% to 60% or more of specialised measurement niches. The pattern is similar in specialised semiconductor metrology (key players at 50% to 60%) and optical alignment (about 60%). The niches are small enough that new entrants find them unattractive, which is the "bounded market" condition the Wisdom Library entry "Scale Needs a Bounded Market" requires for a scale advantage to be real.

Broader market. The top five global players (Thermo Fisher, Danaher, Agilent, Waters, Shimadzu and similar) hold well above 30% of laboratory instruments. The top five control over 70% of high-precision optics (Jenoptik file) and Hexagon about 50% of Chinese metrology. The distribution is steep: the top five hold many times the share of numbers six to ten.

Distribution and services are not concentrated. The top twenty distributors hold under 25%.

Implication. Within each niche Judges faces a small number of rational rivals. The concentration that threatens it is that of large platform players that may bundle software and service across niches.

For the model. Supports stable niche pricing (no price-war assumption) and limited share loss outside the named problem businesses.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C55

Judgement. Shares in Judges' core niches have been stable. New entrants have taken meaningful share only in price-sensitive or commoditising sub-segments and inside China.

Evidence of stability:

  • multi-decade instrument lives;
  • qualification and re-certification costs;
  • embedded analysis software;
  • shares rarely shift more than about 1% a year;
  • churn practically non-existent once instruments are embedded (Morgan Stanley).

Evidence of erosion:

  • Armfield's share fell from 40% to about 15% since 2016, roughly 2.5 percentage points a year, to European rivals (GUNT, TecQuipment, Edibon), not to new entrants;
  • Fire Testing Technology's margin fell from 30% to about 15% as cheaper options emerged;
  • in China, local competitors depressed foreign exporters' share (Fire Testing Technology is named) and state grant categories exclude foreign instruments;
  • Chinese firms undercut Western and Japanese brands by 20% to 30% in lower-end metrology (Renishaw file), and Renishaw reports Chinese capture of 20% to 30% of new placements in its low end.

There is no quantified figure for new-entrant share in Judges' niches overall.

Judgement on direction. Stable in the premium core, eroding at the low end and in China. China and Hong Kong fell from 13.5% of revenue (2023) to 10.4% (2025). Part of that is cyclical and administrative, but part is plausibly structural localisation.

For the model.

  • China: grow below group in 2027 to 2028 after the 2026 catch-up, perhaps flat to low single digit.
  • Group: keep about 1 percentage point a year of share-loss drag in organic growth.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones, and is this company among them?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Share is consolidating towards large, diversified, software-enabled players and towards players with commercial and semiconductor exposure. Judges is among the advantaged players inside its niches, but not among those gaining share in the broader industry.

Evidence. Zeus notes rising concentration as large diversified instrumentation groups use scale, broader portfolios and M&A to gain share. Halma deployed a record £451m to £475m on acquisitions in FY26. Oxford Instruments grew commercial orders 25.4% and Advanced Technologies orders 28%. Renishaw's encoder business grew 26%. Spectris was taken private and repositioned.

Judges. It wins inside niches through specification, service and customer intimacy. It does not participate in the consolidation of software, service and workflow, and its own acquisition engine (its route to "consolidate towards the advantaged") has stalled. The decentralised model deliberately forgoes cross-niche scale.

Assessment. Consolidation in Judges' fragmented niches is mostly done by acquirers buying whole businesses rather than by organic share shift. Judges is one of those acquirers, but now competes with private equity, Chinese strategic buyers and larger consolidators. The Industry Digest's point that the arbitrage is widest for small buyers paying 3x to 7x EBIT still favours Judges, provided it can source deals.

For the model. No organic share-gain tailwind. Inorganic growth is the only consolidation lever and is excluded from the base case until a deal completes.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return on capital first, or volume first; benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C62

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. In Judges' core niches competitors are benign and margin-first. They compete on technical specification, reliability and service, avoid direct overlap, and prices have not fallen as volumes fell. The exceptions are state-supported Chinese entrants (volume-first) and selective discounting in system replacements at large vendors.

Evidence:

  • Business Foundations describes "generally benign competitive dynamics, with operators competing on technical specifications and product reliability rather than pricing and systematically avoiding direct overlap";
  • in the 2025 to 2026 downturn the margin damage came from operating leverage, not from price cuts: no source reports discounting at Judges;
  • academic buyers defer rather than negotiate down;
  • high-end peer tiers (Hexagon, Zeiss, Heidenhain; Zeiss and Jenoptik) are described as rational with value-based pricing.

Against:

  • Chinese competitors use subsidies of about 3% of revenue and local-content rules;
  • a former Agilent marketing expert reports aggressive discount programmes to win system replacements since the pandemic;
  • Jefferies' April 2026 survey finds academic customers increasingly resistant to further price rises.

For the model. Prices hold in a downturn, so recovery margins come back through volume, not price. Limit price increases to about cost inflation while academic budgets are tight.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years? Do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C60

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The attitude has been stable through the downturn, and a pricing shark attack on Judges' niches is unlikely. The instability that exists is structural and slow (Chinese localisation and replication), not tactical and reactive.

Evidence. Over 2024 to 2026, despite the deepest demand fall in years, no source reports price wars in Judges' niches. Peers' responses were cost programmes (Renishaw £21m, Spectris £30m) and commercial pivots, not price cuts.

The most aggressive behaviour seen:

  • Chinese companies acquiring UK scientific businesses for their intellectual property (confirmed by Prestidge, March 2026);
  • Chinese replication: a former Veeco chief technology officer says Chinese firms acquire, replicate and improve technologies once a category becomes strategically important or high-volume;
  • in Armfield's education market, rivals exploited its inflexible pricing and weak innovation, a gradual erosion rather than a sudden attack.

Would rivals pounce on weakness? In the small, bounded niches the payoff is too small for large players. The risk lies where a niche becomes strategically important to China (semiconductor-adjacent, battery). There, state-backed rivals may price aggressively.

For the model. No pricing-shock scenario in the base case. Keep a structural China and low-end erosion drag rather than an event risk.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C58

Judgement. The structure is supportive at niche level: concentrated, bounded niches; benign, specification-led rivals; high switching costs; modest supplier power; fragmented customers. That support is moderately robust in about 80% of the portfolio and fragile in three places.

The three fragile places:

  1. Demand concentration on public research funding. About two-thirds of sales go to universities and research institutes. This is not an industry-structure weakness in competition terms, but it means structure cannot protect volume when budgets freeze.
  2. The low end and China. Chinese localisation, replication and subsidies are eroding price-sensitive niches (Armfield, Fire Testing Technology).
  3. The acquisition market. Rising competition from private equity, Chinese buyers and larger consolidators threatens the 3x to 7x entry multiples on which the group's compounding depends.

For the model.

  • Niche margins: assume they recover with volume. Pricing structure supports a return towards 20% or more adjusted operating margin once volume normalises.
  • Industry growth: do not assume it exceeds about 4% to 5% (the market forecast is about 4.5% a year).
  • Acquisitions: treat any resumption as progressively more expensive.
C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C55

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement. The sector's precedents identify four patterns. Judges currently fits the underperformance side of three of them, but it also fits the classic setup for a sharp recovery from a trough, so the patterns are two-sided for the next 18 months.

  1. Outperformance comes from exposure to the funded growth driver of the cycle. Today that is semiconductor, AI and data-centre capex: Renishaw +55.6%, Jenoptik +98.8%, Vaisala +48.4% over twelve months. Academic-exposed names underperform during funding freezes (Bruker, Oxford Instruments' academic side, Judges −40.5%). Judges lacks the current driver.
  2. Serial acquirers compound and re-rate while cadence and ROTIC hold, and de-rate when either slips. Judges compounded TSR at 25% a year from 2005 to 2025 on 6x EBITA deals and a 29% average ROTIC. Its P/E fell from about 35x to about 20x and now about 14.5x as ROTIC fell (22.7% to 12.9%) and deals stopped. Halma, by contrast, holds 16% ROTIC and a 23x to 30x multiple. The Industry Digest warns that "a rising entry multiple with drifting returns is the early warning of a compounder losing its engine". Judges shows drifting returns now; it does not yet show a rising entry multiple, because it has done no deals.
  3. High fixed-cost makers are best bought at the turn, when operating leverage pays most. The Industry Digest records high fixed costs removing about a third of Judges' EBIT excluding coring on a modest volume decline. The sector rule "a worry clearing needs little to improve: trough earnings and a turned order book against a falling price is the setup" describes Judges now: trough FY26 EPS, an order book back to 17.3 weeks, and the price down 24% over three months. The Sector Digest names Judges explicitly as "early and against the price", tested at the January 2027 trading update.
  4. In thinly covered names the multiple moves first and far. Judges has three brokers and trades about $1m a day, so a believed recovery story could re-rate it quickly. Equally, the share has fallen 20% after a results update that held guidance.

Also relevant: the Wisdom Library's "Turnarounds Take Longer Than You Think" (two to three years, a roughly 30% base rate for turnarounds) and "Name the Trigger Before You Buy the Turnaround". The trigger here is dated (January and March 2027 updates) but not yet observed.

For the model.

  • Operational: precedents (Judges' own 2014-16 and 2020 recoveries; peers' cost programmes plus volume, as at Renishaw where margin went from 15.7% to 18.7% in a year) show margin recovers fast once volume returns. The base case should show margin recovery lagging the order turn by two to four quarters.
  • Share price: the pattern favours Judges once orders and revenue both turn positive year on year, likely visible from H1 2027.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

❌ D38

Judgement. Customer demand now partly supports a recovery thesis: orders are stabilising and turning, deferred orders are being released, and commercial demand is resilient. The support is fragile, because it rests on administrative catch-up and shipment conversion rather than restored budgets.

Supportive:

  • intake from Q1 −18% to Q2 flat to Q3 about +15%;
  • year-to-date −1%;
  • order book 17.3 weeks (15.7 at December);
  • China tax exemptions resolving;
  • semiconductor and battery customers growing;
  • UK orders +5%, Rest of World +12%;
  • a highly fragmented customer base (thousands of institutions, no material concentration, negligible bad debt);
  • customers have ordered and are asking only to delay shipment, so demand is deferred rather than cancelled, and no source reports cancellations.

Fragile:

  • US orders are still −7% and grant flows "uneven";
  • Jefferies' survey found 75% of academic lab managers describe the environment as weak, 94% have had grants cancelled, and biomedical research spending is down about 16%;
  • a Columbia clinician-scientist reports instrument and consumables spend halved versus two years earlier;
  • Europe −20% (OEM and end-customer softness);
  • offshore wind deteriorating;
  • the FY27 US budget request proposes cuts again;
  • the China recovery is refill, not growth.

Q4 risk. Management expects a "greater-than-usual" Q4 weighting. Shipping delayed orders depends on customers' funding paperwork and on production slots.

For the model. Base case: FY26 revenue about £122m to £128m, at or modestly above the £121m consensus because 2026 book-to-bill is above 1. Allow up to about £5m of Q4 shipments to slip into FY27. FY27 organic growth about 6% to 9%.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C52

Judgement. Routes to market are neutral to slightly hindering. They are not currently a source of disruption, but they limit control and visibility.

Structure. Judges sells mostly through independent distributors, agents and OEM partners, with direct sales staff only in the United States. Business Foundations cites distributors handling about 85% of global sales. OEM embedding is a genuine strength: Oxford Cryosystems into X-ray diffraction OEMs, Sircal into spectrometer OEMs, EWB into ultra-high-vacuum systems.

Weaknesses:

  • credit exposure to individual overseas distributors can become material and concentrated;
  • distributors obscure end-user demand, which feeds the "black box" visibility problem;
  • managing distributors needs skills the subsidiaries often lack (Wilcock), which is why distributor benchmarking was introduced.

Emerging threat. Multi-vendor service and lifecycle providers (Shimadzu's Zef Scientific acquisition, a service market growing about 7% a year) and specialist aggregating distributors (AS One) could capture customer relationships and service margins from small manufacturers.

No current stress. No distributor change or channel destocking was reported in the period.

For the model. No channel effect on revenue. Treat channel opacity as a reason to widen the forecast range rather than to change the central case, and keep a small bad-debt reserve.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

❌ D45

Judgement. Existing competitors' behaviour mildly hurts Judges overall. It does not hurt in the core niches, where rivals are rational. It hurts in three troubled businesses and, indirectly, through larger peers' faster pivot to commercial markets and software.

Hurting:

  • GUNT, TecQuipment and Edibon have taken Armfield's education share;
  • cheaper fire-testing equipment and better software rivals halved Fire Testing Technology's margin;
  • Sutter and Siskiyou and integrated laser-microscope suppliers led to Scientifica's multiphoton exit;
  • large peers (Oxford Instruments, Halma, Renishaw, Waters) are converting the same macro conditions into growth through commercial and semiconductor exposure, AI software and cost programmes. That widens the performance and valuation gap: Judges' adjusted EBIT margin was 8.6% in H1 2026, against Halma at 22.7% and Oxford Instruments at about 18%.

Not hurting. In vacuum, cryogenics, gas purification and coring, rivals are few and compete on specification. Experts describe Judges' specialised niches (UHV Design, Oxford Cryosystems) as quasi-monopolistic with high switching costs.

Fragility. The core position is robust. The challenged businesses are probably about 10% to 15% of revenue (no disclosed figure) and may keep eroding.

For the model. Keep the core at mid-cycle niche margins (above 20%). Model the challenged segment flat to down in revenue with margin repair limited to cost actions.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

❌ D45

Judgement. Overall the new-entrant threat is moderate and slow. Chinese standard entrants are the most material type.

  1. Adjacent entrants (large workflow and platform vendors moving down into niches through automation, software and multi-vendor service). Medium threat over three to five years. Danaher with NVIDIA, Waters' AI Link and Halma's automation strategy aim to own integrated workflows, which could relegate stand-alone niche instruments to components. Low near-term threat, because their economics do not justify entering niches below £1m.
  2. Standard entrants (lower-cost copycats, mainly Chinese, plus Indian suppliers). Medium and rising threat in price-sensitive niches and inside China: 20% to 30% price gaps, local-content policies, subsidies. Already visible in China (Fire Testing Technology) and in education equipment. Low threat in high-precision vacuum, cryogenic and physics-based niches, where replicating validation and reputation takes five to ten years (Renishaw analogue) and customers distrust unproven references (Vaisala analogue).
  3. Orthogonal entrants (AI-only analysis, digital twins, virtual metrology, alternative low-cost diagnostic methods). Low near-term threat; they mostly shift value to software rather than displace hardware.
  4. Entrants into the acquisition market (private equity, Chinese strategic buyers). Already present and the most consequential for EPS compounding, because they raise prices and shrink the pool of targets.

For the model. Embed the standard-entrant drag in the organic growth assumption (part of the roughly 1 percentage point a year share-loss drag). Treat acquisition-market entrants as a reason to assume fewer, smaller deals at somewhat higher multiples once acquisitions resume.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives a threat, and how is that changing?

C52

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. Substitution is a low but slowly rising threat. The biggest practical alternative is no purchase or a delayed purchase, which is a cycle effect rather than substitution.

Substitutes:

  1. Software and AI that reduce the number of physical measurements (AI-driven experiment design, virtual metrology in semiconductors, image interpretation in electron microscopy). These shift some value to software and may reduce replacement demand; physical validation remains essential.
  2. Longer instrument lives and refurbishment or multi-vendor service. These lengthen replacement cycles. Replacement is under 20% of annual sales.
  3. Cheaper "good enough" alternatives, mostly Chinese.
  4. Outsourced testing (contract research organisations, outsourced wet labs funded by venture capital as biotechs bypass academic labs). This can shift instrument purchases from universities to commercial labs, which may still buy Judges' products, so it is neutral to mildly positive given the commercial pivot.
  5. Digital alternatives in diagnostics (smartphone imaging, nanopore). Mostly not validated yet and application-specific.

Direction. Rising slowly with AI, but the 18-month impact is negligible.

For the model. No explicit substitution haircut. A mild headwind to long-run organic growth of perhaps 0.5 percentage points a year beyond 2028.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

❌ D30

Judgement. Regulators and political actors are the dominant negative actors in Judges' value chain, and the company's position against them is fragile because it has no influence over them.

Hurting:

  • The US executive. Budget proposals cutting the National Institutes of Health and National Science Foundation, freezes on grant obligation, and indirect-cost caps (blocked by the courts). Congress restored funding in principle, but flows remain uneven, and Q3 2026 National Institutes of Health grants were −8% year on year.
  • China. Tax-exemption procedures for government-funded instruments (−47% orders in H1, now resolving); domestic-substitution grant categories; rare-earth export licensing.
  • The UK government. Corporation tax up to 25% and higher employer National Insurance, about £3m a year.
  • The EU. CBAM and PFAS rules that raise compliance costs, and defence possibly crowding out research in the 2028 to 2034 framework.

Helping:

  • Horizon Europe +€500m and the UK rejoining Horizon;
  • UK Patent Box;
  • UK life-sciences funding;
  • possibly US tariffs on China improving UK exporters' relative position;
  • regulation-led demand in fire testing and environmental measurement.

Fragility. High. A single US budget cycle swung group organic US sales by −22%, and the FY2027 request repeats the cuts. China's localisation is a long-term policy direction.

For the model. The base case should assume another US stopgap year with flat-to-modestly-up academic orders in FY27. Run a downside scenario in which enacted cuts (even half the request) take US academic orders down another 10% to 15% in FY27.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

❌ D40

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. Disruption forces are acting on five value-chain actors:

Actor What is acting on it Effect on Judges Status
Demand Political funding shocks to academic customers (US, China) and capital deferral by industrial and energy customers (offshore wind) The decisive disruption Bottoming, not yet reversing
Competition Chinese replication and localisation at the low end; software, automation and workflow platforms at the high end Squeezes stand-alone niche hardware makers Slow, structural
Regulators and politicians US budget policy, Chinese procurement and substitution rules, UK taxation Direct and immediate Fragile
Suppliers Exits of specialist suppliers (Armfield forced to insource); rare-earth export controls Localised Small
The acquisition market Chinese and private-equity buyers Disrupts Judges' input of new businesses at low multiples Structural, not cyclical

Overall. Judges' position is resilient in its core product niches but fragile at the level of demand and of acquisition inputs. The two engines of its compounding model, organic volume and cheap acquisitions, are both being disrupted at the same time, which has not happened before in its history.

For the model. The base case should not assume both engines restart together. Model organic recovery first (FY27) and acquisitions later (FY28 or later), each as an explicit, separate assumption.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C55

Judgement. Suppliers are mostly a support. Supply is a minor and localised source of risk, not a material driver of the forecast.

Support:

  • sourcing is decentralised and largely local (UK) across many small suppliers;
  • pre-agreed call-off orders secure volume price breaks;
  • lead times have normalised, allowing inventory reduction;
  • logistics were unaffected by the Iran conflict;
  • capital intensity is low at about 4% of revenue on capex, with an assembly-only model (Armfield outsources about 90% of fabrication);
  • no supplier concentration is flagged at group level.

Risks:

  • Armfield's suppliers exited post-Covid, forcing a multi-year insourcing and redesign project with execution, cost and working-capital needs;
  • some units face specialised single-source suppliers for custom vacuum and optoelectronic components, and Scientifica's dependence on third-party optics contributed to its product failure;
  • small subsidiaries cannot force pricing or schedules;
  • Chinese rare-earth export controls;
  • steel, copper and Hastelloy inflation (Thermal Hazard Technology);
  • inventory buffers kept working capital elevated at 16% of sales.

For the model. Supply has no effect on revenue. Gross economics are neutral. The working-capital release (from 16% towards about 12% to 13% over two years, not the full 10% target) is the supply-related upside.

Company - Competitive advantage

C547 questions
C-4158C-4238C-4358C-4458C-4548C-4645C-4752

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judges' competitive advantage is real but narrower than its history implies.

At product level about 85% to 90% of revenue sits in genuinely protected niches:

  • number one or two shares in bounded markets;
  • multi-decade installed bases;
  • embedded software and OEM design-in;
  • prices that held through a severe volume collapse.

That supports a confident assumption that margins recover with volume.

The edges are eroding (Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, Scientifica). Below-peer R&D and limited software are the long-run vulnerabilities, and Chinese localisation erodes the low end.

At group level, the decisive historical advantage was the mechanical, low-multiple acquisition engine run by a trusted founder. It is narrowing:

  • the founder has stepped back;
  • competitors for deals are rising;
  • the balance sheet and dividend absorb the cash that once fed it.

Scale confers financing and diversification, not operating advantage. Pricing power is defensive (cost pass-through), not a margin lever.

For the model:

  • mid-cycle niche margins are recoverable;
  • organic growth of 4% to 6% after the FY27 rebound;
  • price contributes about cost inflation;
  • the acquisition engine is a separate, uncertain, later-dated scenario rather than an assumed constant.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C58

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement. Judges holds two layers of advantage, and they move in opposite directions. The second, the group-level acquisition advantage, is what produced the historical compounding, and it is the one that is narrowing.

Layer 1: niche product moats in about 20 of 25 businesses.

  • Customer-related moats (strong, stable in the core).
  • Instruments run for decades; replacing them means redesigning, re-testing and re-certifying research protocols.
  • Embedded analysis software and preventive maintenance contracts lock customers in.
  • Commissioning large equipment needs two to three weeks of on-site engineering.
  • OEM design-in: Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal and EWB are built into other makers' systems.
  • Churn is practically nil once instruments are embedded.
  • Production and knowledge moats (moderate). Proprietary designs and assembly know-how support gross margins above 50%.
  • Weak point: R&D is only 7.0% of revenue, against 8% to 11% at peers.
  • Weak point: software capability is limited.
  • Armfield, Fire Testing Technology and Scientifica show these moats erode when product investment lapses.
  • Scale-based moats (weak at group level; real at niche level). Niches are bounded, which deters entrants because the prize is too small. Subsidiaries are sub-scale against platforms in purchasing, software and service.
  • Regulatory moats (minimal). Some standards-based demand exists, for example in fire testing.
  • Culture moats (moderate). Entrepreneurial autonomy, about 40% employee share ownership, and frugality.

Layer 2: the group-level acquisition advantage. Judges is a trusted, "honourable" permanent home for retiring UK founders. That reputation, plus a pool of about 2,000 targets, let it buy at 3x to 7x EBIT (about 6x EBITA on average) against double-digit multiples at Halma. This was the real source of a 29% average ROTIC and a 25% TSR CAGR.

It is narrowing:

  • private equity and Chinese strategic buyers now contest deals;
  • the founder has stepped back from day-to-day dealing;
  • leverage of 2.1x and a dividend covered 1.1x limit capacity;
  • no deal has been done since 2024.

Direction. Niche moats are stable in the core and narrowing at the edges. The acquisition moat is narrowing but intact on price discipline.

For the model. Assume niche margins recover to mid-cycle levels. Assume no return to the historical rate of acquisition compounding without evidence from deals.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what does that scale confer?

❌ D38

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement. Judges is small. Its scale confers financing, diversification and central-talent advantages over its own subsidiaries and over private competitors in niches. It confers almost no operating scale advantage, and it leaves Judges disadvantaged against large instrument platforms.

Company Revenue Employees
Judges (FY25) £145.8m about 822
Halma (FY26) £2,582m n/a
Spectris (FY24) £1,299m about 7,400
Jenoptik €1,046m 4,453
Renishaw (FY26) £816m about 4,975
Vaisala €597m 2,476
Oxford Instruments £423m n/a

Market capitalisation is about £230m, against £2bn to £13bn for these peers. Most Judges subsidiaries employ fewer than 100 people.

What scale confers:

  • Access to debt. A £90m revolving facility plus a £50m accordion, extended to 2030. A single niche business could not fund acquisitions this way.
  • Portfolio diversification. One failure, such as Armfield, does not sink the group.
  • A central team. Acquisitions executive, portfolio chief executive, commercial director, a leadership programme, and internal audit.
  • Credibility with sellers.

What it does not confer:

  • purchasing power, since the model forgoes synergies by design;
  • group software or connectivity platforms;
  • a global direct sales and service network (Renishaw has subsidiaries in 36 countries);
  • the ability to absorb a big funding shock without margin collapse.

For the model. Scale gives no margin advantage. Central costs of about £5m are a fixed overhead that weighs more heavily at low volumes, which is part of the operating leverage.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C58

Judgement. The addressable market is large relative to Judges in aggregate and does not cap growth. Realistic mid-term organic growth is about 4% to 7% a year, with acquisitions historically adding about 10 or more percentage points when the engine runs.

Product market. The global scientific-instruments market is projected at about $38.6bn by 2034, growing about 4.5% a year (Zeus). Precision instrument demand has historically grown at about twice GDP. Commercial and industrial R&D is estimated at four to five times the academic market. Judges' current niches are individually small and close to saturated in share, so growth comes from market growth, product renewal, geographic reach and mix shift towards commercial markets. Judges' own long-run organic growth was 7% a year for revenue and 8% for EBIT over 19 years, achieved in a better academic-funding era.

Acquisition market. About 2,000 potential UK targets and about 100 opportunities a year, against Judges' historical 1.3 deals a year. The pool does not constrain growth; sellers' willingness and competition do.

Constraints over the mid term:

  • US academic budgets have structurally declining real support;
  • Chinese localisation;
  • instruments lasting longer;
  • the commercial pivot takes years.

For the model.

  • Organic growth after FY27's rebound. FY27 itself, about 6% to 9%, is partly mechanical. For FY28 to FY29, about 4% to 6% excluding coring.
  • Total growth with acquisitions. 8% to 12% if deployment resumes at £10m to £20m a year.
  • What would need to happen for more. US academic recovery plus commercial share gains, both outside management's control.
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

C58

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement. Across most of the portfolio Judges is demonstrably not inferior, and probably leading. In three businesses inferiority has been demonstrated. That limits the group-level claim to "not inferior for about 85% to 90% of revenue".

Evidence of non-inferiority or leadership:

  • leading niche shares (number one or two) held for decades;
  • installed bases running 20 or more years;
  • OEMs choosing to integrate Judges components;
  • customer churn near zero once instruments are embedded;
  • Scientifica differentiating on service and modularity against Sutter and Siskiyou;
  • Korvus beating global rivals with its flexible HEX system;
  • Geotek's coring and core-logging capability, for which customers wait (the expedition was deferred, not lost to a rival);
  • gross margins above 50% that customers keep paying.

Evidence of inferiority:

  • Armfield's share fell from 40% to about 15%, with former staff citing weak innovation, inflexible pricing and poor technical support after acquisition;
  • Fire Testing Technology's software fell behind modern standards, its margin halved and its new cone calorimeter is a year late;
  • Scientifica's multiphoton systems suffered alignment and installation problems and were discontinued;
  • larger peers increasingly offer integrated software and AI analysis that Judges' stand-alone instruments lack.

For the model. Treat the three challenged businesses as a separate "repair" segment with flat revenue and low margin through FY27. The rest of the portfolio supports recovery to historical niche margins.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

❌ D48

Judgement. Pricing power exists and is used to pass through costs. Evidence of applying it beyond cost recovery is thin. In the current academic climate it is a defensive asset, not a source of real margin expansion.

Applied evidence:

  • management states that about 85% of businesses can pass on cost increases;
  • subsidiaries pass on input costs in 90% to 95% of cases;
  • prices held through the 2025 to 2026 volume collapse: margin damage came from operating leverage, not discounting;
  • Korvus prices without market benchmarks;
  • the historical margin of 25% to 27% (FY21 to FY23) shows past ability to price above cost.

Against:

  • price realisation lagged cost in FY25 and contributed to the 170 basis point margin fall;
  • price increases are never quantified;
  • academic customers are increasingly resistant (Jefferies survey, April 2026);
  • Armfield's pricing inflexibility lost share;
  • peers show what demonstrable pricing looks like (Renishaw's surcharges converted into permanent increases worth about 1% of revenue; Oxford Instruments repricing its order book; Jenoptik raising prices twice), and Judges has disclosed nothing comparable.

For the model. Price contribution of about 2% to 3% a year, roughly matching wage inflation, with neutral effect on margin. Upside of 0.5 to 1 percentage point a year of real price in commercial and vacuum niches once academic budgets ease. No margin assumption should rely on price.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement. This barely applies. Judges has few scale economies to share. Its model is value-based pricing in niches where the instrument is a small, critical part of the customer's research cost, not a cost-leader passing savings on.

There are no stated or de facto policies of sharing scale benefits. The decentralised model explicitly forgoes group purchasing and cost synergies. Customer value comes from specification, reliability, service and longevity. The Wisdom Library's "Scale Economies Shared" pattern (cost leaders such as Costco and Ryanair) is not Judges' source of advantage, and its absence is not a weakness here. The relevant "sharing" behaviour is service intensity and product longevity: instruments supported for 20 or more years build loyalty.

For the model. Neutral. There is no flywheel from shared scale economies, and the score is neutral rather than negative because the business model does not depend on it.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C52

Judgement. Judges' historical outperformance came largely from one mechanical force: recycling high cash conversion into acquisitions at about 5x EBIT in a deep, fragmented pool of targets. That force compounds EPS almost automatically while it runs. It is currently switched off. The organic niches have only weak gravitational forces.

The acquisition flywheel when running.

  • Pre-dividend free cash flow of about £18m to £21m a year redeployed at about 5x EBIT adds about £4m of EBIT a year, about 15% EBIT growth before any organic growth and without heroic effort.
  • That explains the 23% a year EBIT CAGR (8% organic) over 19 years.
  • The Industry Digest confirms the arbitrage is widest for small buyers at 3x to 7x EBIT, even with 5% to 6% debt costs.

Why it has stopped:

  • free cash flow after dividends is only about £8m;
  • net debt is 2.1x;
  • sellers are scarce and competition higher;
  • recent deals underperformed, which makes management more cautious.

Organic gravitational forces:

  • installed-base service and spares (service revenue £19.3m to £24.5m, about 13% to 17% of sales);
  • replacement cycles;
  • regulation-led demand. These are weak. Niche moats protect but do not widen by themselves; Armfield shows they erode without investment.

Peers. Halma's acquisition engine continues at a record pace. Renishaw's and Jenoptik's operating leverage on capacity they have already built is a mechanical tailwind in an upcycle.

For the model. The base case should be organic-only (no gravitational acquisition force) through FY27. Add an explicit acquisition scenario from FY28. The fair multiple depends heavily on whether this mechanical engine restarts.

Company - Economic strength

C5011 questions
C-4928C-5058C-5150C-5270C-5340C-5442C-7555C-7648C-7742C-7862C-7945

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judges' economics are strong in structure and weak in their current outputs.

The strengths:

  • asset-light assembly;
  • about 65% contribution margins;
  • capex about 4% of revenue;
  • cash conversion of about 100% or more of adjusted net income;
  • returns on tangible capital above 40%.

The current outputs:

  • adjusted EBIT margin about 9 points below peak;
  • ROTIC down to 12.9% from 22.7%;
  • EPS falling a third year to about 200p, about 47% below FY23.

The bridge between the two is very high operating leverage (about 60% to 65% drop-through). That is the single most important modelling mechanic.

Three modelling conclusions for the Consumer:

  1. The FY26 consensus of about 200p rests on an unusual H2. It needs either H2 revenue of about £70m (only about 7% below H2 2025) at a margin of about 20% or more, or about 30p of Patent Box tax benefit on top of a 20% H2 margin. FactSet's line items (revenue £121m, EBIT £20.3m) are internally inconsistent and should be rebuilt. My base case for FY26 is about 185p to 200p, with a downside of about 165p to 175p if Q4 shipments slip.
  2. FY27 to FY28 consensus embeds very little operating leverage (about 18% to 24% incremental margins). If volume recovers it is conservative on margin. FY28 EPS of about 230p to 250p (no coring, no acquisitions) is a reasonable base case, with an upside cone to about 300p to 350p from coring and acquisitions.
  3. Returns on total capital cannot reach the 30% target within the forecast window (about 15% to 16% by FY28 on consensus). The fair multiple therefore depends on proof that the acquisition engine has restarted, not on margins alone.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

❌ D28

Judgement. Judges is generating sharply negative EPS growth: a third consecutive decline, and the deepest. A recovery from FY27 is driven by base effects and is not yet durable. FY26 adjusted EPS of about 200p will be about 47% below the FY23 peak.

FY23 FY24 FY25 FY26E FY27E FY28E
Adjusted EPS 374.6p 283.4p 275.3p about 199p about 219p about 248p
EPS growth +3% −24% −3% −27% +10% +13%
Revenue growth +20% −2% +9% −17% +6% +8%

FY26 to FY28 are FactSet consensus medians from three brokers (two for FY28).

What drove the decline. Organic revenue excluding coring fell about 2% in FY24, was roughly flat to +2% in FY25 (sources conflict; Shore cites a 7% contraction in the core) and fell about 9% to 12% in H1 2026. Coring was absent in FY24, present in H1 2025 and absent from FY26 to at least FY27. Acquisitions added about £11.5m in FY25 and nothing since. Share count dilution runs about 1% to 1.3% a year (6.2m shares in 2019 to 6.7m in 2025).

How durable.

  • Low for FY26. About 80% of the year's EPS falls in H2.
  • Moderate for FY27. Growth is mechanical: H1 2026 comparisons are easy and China deliveries delayed from 2026 will ship.
  • Uncertain for FY28. It needs organic growth of about 6% to 8% and no FX hit.

The historical 19-year record (revenue CAGR 19%, organic 7%; EBIT CAGR 23%, organic 8%) is not a guide for 2026 to 2028, because the acquisition engine that supplied most of it is paused.

For the model. Build EPS from organic volume, cost actions, tax and interest. Keep acquisitions and coring out of the base case. On the base case, FY28 EPS of about 230p to 250p is reachable; a return to FY23's 375p needs coring plus about £40m to £60m of acquisitions plus full volume recovery, which is unlikely before FY29 or FY30.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C58

Judgement. The opportunity set for high-return deployment is large and unchanged. Acquisitions at 3x to 7x EBIT give a 14% to 33% pre-tax return, about 15% to 20% post-tax at the typical 5x. The ability to deploy over the next 12 to 18 months is constrained by:

  • leverage of 2.1x against a 3.0x covenant;
  • £31.4m of committed headroom;
  • about £8m a year of free cash flow after dividends;
  • a deal function that has not yet proved it can work without the founder.

Opportunity. About 2,000 targets and about 100 a year coming to market. The arbitrage against a 5% to 6% cost of debt is wide. Organic reinvestment needs are low: capex is about 4% of revenue and R&D 7%.

Capacity, three to five years. If EBITDA recovers to about £27m to £30m by FY28 and leverage is held at about 2.0x, debt capacity reaches about £55m to £60m. With cumulative free cash flow after dividends of about £25m over 2027 to 2029 and working-capital release of £5m to £8m, Judges could deploy about £40m to £60m between FY27 and FY29. At about 5x EBIT that is £8m to £12m of acquired EBIT, worth about 60p to 100p of EPS after funding costs.

Implication for the fair multiple.

  • If the engine restarts (one to two deals a year at about 5x, ROTIC recovering towards 18% to 20%), a fair multiple of about 18x to 22x forward P/E is justified, between the five-year median of 21.8x and its own history.
  • If it stays stalled and the company is an organic-only, academically exposed niche manufacturer, about 13x to 15x is fair, where it trades now (14.5x).

The market is pricing the stalled case.

For the model. Keep acquisitions out of the base case. Run an acquisition scenario of £15m to £20m a year from H2 2027 at 5x to 6x EBIT, funded by debt at about 6%.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C50

Judgement. Returns on tangible capital are excellent, an asset-light assembly model. Returns on total invested capital (including goodwill and acquired intangibles) have fallen from top-tier to mediocre. On new invested capital, the recent record is mixed.

ROTIC (the company's measure, total invested capital including acquired goodwill):

2023 2024 2025 H1 2026 Founder-era average Target
22.7% 16.5% 17.8% 12.9% 29% 30%

On my reconstruction, invested capital is about £120m to £125m. Each £1m of post-tax operating profit moves ROTIC by about 0.8 percentage points. On FY28 consensus EBIT of £23.8m, ROTIC is only about 15% to 16%. Even 20% needs adjusted EBIT of about £31m (the FY23 level). The 30% target is out of reach in the forecast window. Peers: Halma 16.2%, Oxford Instruments ROIC 19.9%, Renishaw 17.5%.

Return on new invested capital:

  • Geotek (£80m at 7x EBIT) gives about 14% pre-tax in the deal year but is heavily cyclical;
  • Teer (£12.3m), Luciol and Rockwash: the Rockwash earn-out was missed and its goodwill impaired;
  • Armfield and Scientifica: impaired. Recent marginal returns are well below the historical 29%.

Return on tangible capital. Tangible capital is roughly £40m to £50m (working capital about 16% of sales plus modest fixed assets; capex about 4% of revenue). Pre-tax return on tangible capital is on the order of 50% to 60% in FY25 and about 40% to 45% on FY26 consensus. This is why cash generation stays strong in a downturn.

For the model.

  • ROTIC recovers slowly, to about 14% (FY26E) to 16% (FY28E), on consensus.
  • Any acquisition at 5x EBIT is ROTIC-accretive.
  • Watch for further impairment of earlier acquisitions as a signal of capital quality.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C70

Judgement. Cash conversion is genuinely high and structurally supported by the asset-light model. It is the strongest economic attribute of the business. Reported conversion figures are flattered by timing items, and working capital is still about 6 percentage points of sales above target.

The company's measure (operating cash flow over adjusted operating profit):

FY23 FY24 FY25 H1 2025 H1 2026 Target
90% 122% 118% 86% 90% 90%
  • FY24 was flattered by a large advance payment for the Japanese coring expedition.
  • FY25 was 136% excluding the timing of Geotek payments, but helped by a reported £5.3m stretch in creditors (memo source) and £3.0m to £3.3m of inventory reduction.

Free cash flow against adjusted net income (my reconciliation). FactSet's historical "FCF" line is after dividends (see reading note 3).

  • FY25: pre-dividend free cash flow (operating cash flow £26.4m less fixed-asset capex £5.4m) was about £21m, against adjusted net income of about £18m, about 115%. After capitalised development (£1.8m) and lease payments, it is about 100%.
  • FY26 consensus: operating cash flow £21.0m less capex £5.0m gives about £16m, against adjusted net income of £13.6m, about 115% to 120%.

Working capital:

  • 16% of sales at FY25, against a 10% pre-Covid norm (about £8m of potential release) and 19% to 20% in FY24 and H1 2025;
  • seasonally high at the half-year: 23.3% of trailing revenue at H1 2026 on FactSet's definition, against 22.6% at H1 2025;
  • the H2 2026 Q4 weighting means receivables will be high at year-end, so FY26 year-end working capital could look elevated even if H2 delivers.

Uses of cash. Dividends of about £7m (FY25) rising to about £8.4m (FY26E). Capex of about £5m to £7m including development. Interest of about £3m.

For the model.

  • Free cash flow conversion of about 100% to 110% of adjusted net income through FY28.
  • Working capital falling gradually to about 13% to 14% of sales by FY28, releasing about £3m to £4m.
  • Year-end FY26 net debt of about £40m to £44m; 1.5x to 1.7x by end-FY27 absent acquisitions.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

❌ D40

Judgement. Margins are high in structure but have fallen about 900 basis points from peak. Consensus does not have them back above about 17% EBIT through FY28. That looks conservative for a recovered-volume world and realistic for a stalled one.

Gross margin. The company does not disclose one, and FactSet's "gross profit" row is not usable. Raw materials and consumables are about 31% of revenue (£44.8m in FY25), and Business Foundations states proprietary design and assembly gross margins routinely exceed 50%. So contribution margin before fixed staff costs is about 65% to 69%.

Adjusted operating margin.

FY21 FY22 FY23 FY24 FY25 H1 2025 H2 2025 H1 2026
about 26% 26.6% 25.6% 20.9% 19.2% 20.4% about 18.1% 8.6%

Brokers'-basis EBITDA margin: 28% (FY23), 24% (FY24), 22.5% (FY25).

Consensus. EBIT margin about 16.8% (FY26), 17.2% (FY27), 17.2% (FY28); EBITDA margin about 20.4% to 20.7%.

Drivers of the decline:

  • lost high-margin Geotek coring (FY22 and FY23 margins included coring);
  • organic volume declines with operating leverage of about 60% decremental;
  • cost investment ahead of growth (central costs up 75% in FY25, R&D up 70 basis points to 7%);
  • UK tax and National Insurance (about £3m);
  • wage inflation not fully priced;
  • underperformers (Fire Testing Technology from 30% to about 15%; Armfield loss-making).

Segment consensus: Vacuum is more stable (EBITDA about 25% to 27%); Materials Sciences is more cyclical (about 17%).

Mid-cycle without coring. Structurally higher central, tax and R&D costs make the 25% to 27% era hard to repeat without coring. On my estimate a recovered-volume, no-coring adjusted operating margin is about 19% to 22%, with about 2 to 3 percentage points more in coring years.

For the model. Margin path: FY26 about 15% to 17%; FY27 about 17% to 19%; FY28 about 18% to 20% (no coring). Upside to 21% to 23% with coring in 2028.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

❌ D42

Judgement. The adjusted earnings base is reasonably clean in cash terms. It is materially flattered in terms of return on capital, and the FY26 number carries an unusual tax element that the model must separate out.

Gap between adjusted and reported.

  • FY25: adjusted EPS 275.3p against statutory basic 82.7p, a gap of about 70%. FY24: 283.4p against about 157p.
  • The adjusting items are amortisation of acquired intangibles (the largest), goodwill impairments (FY25: Armfield £2.3m; Rockwash £2.0m, offset by released contingent consideration), FX derivative fair-value movements (classified as adjusting items), and acquisition costs.
  • H1 2026: adjusted PBT £3.2m against a statutory pre-tax loss of £1.7m, so about £4.9m of adjusting items in the half.

Is it flattered? Amortisation is non-cash and conversion supports the adjusted figure as cash earnings. But in a model where acquisitions are the core activity, recurring impairments (Armfield, Rockwash, the Scientifica parent write-down) show that part of acquired value decays. "Adjusted" therefore overstates economic earnings on capital deployed. Development capitalisation rose 28% to £1.8m in FY25 (about 18% of R&D Oxford Instruments about 6%, Halma about 11%). It is modest in pounds (about 3p to 5p of EPS of flattering at most), but the 2021 first-time capitalisation flattered EPS by about 10p.

Tax (the critical item for FY26).

  • The adjusted tax rate was 21% in FY24 and 18% in H1 2026, with the Patent Box, against a 25% headline rate.
  • Shore credits enhanced Patent Box relief with about 30p of H2 2026 net benefit. That looks partly like a catch-up claim.
  • The FactSet line items, by contrast, assume about 22% to 23% tax for FY26 (PBT £17.6m, net income £13.6m).
  • For the model: split FY26 EPS into recurring operating EPS and any one-off Patent Box catch-up. Model FY27 and later on a recurring rate of about 19% to 21%. If the one-off is excluded, the "recurring" FY26 EPS could be 20p to 30p lower than the headline, and the FY27 growth rate would look higher than consensus implies.

Other leakage:

  • share dilution of about 1% a year;
  • minor related-party items (a £0.4m introduction fee to the Senior Independent Director, 2023);
  • interest of about £3m to £3.5m, about 15% of FY26 EBIT.
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C55

Judgement. Operating leverage is very high, and it is the most important mechanical feature for the model. Short-run drop-through is about 60% to 65% of incremental revenue, three times the average margin. A ±10% revenue swing moves adjusted EBIT by about ±£7m to £9m, which is ±35% to 45% of FY26 EBIT.

Cost structure (FY25, adjusted cost base about £118m):

Item Amount Share of revenue Behaviour
Raw materials and consumables £44.8m 31% Variable
Staff £48.5m 33% Largely fixed in the short run (skilled engineers)
Other operating costs, including depreciation about £24.5m Mostly fixed

Fixed costs are therefore about £68m to £72m, about 47% to 50% of revenue.

Observed decremental margins:

  • H1 2025 to H1 2026: revenue −£14.5m, adjusted operating profit −£9.5m, about 65% (including loss of high-margin coring);
  • FY23 to FY24: revenue −£2.5m, profit −£6.9m (coring loss plus cost growth);
  • FY25: the business excluding Geotek lost about a third of its profit on a modest volume decline.

Broker sensitivity tables (±1 point of organic revenue = ±4.2p of EPS) imply only about 25% to 30% drop-through and understate the true leverage.

Where this bites in the forecast:

  1. The H2 2026 bridge. On my cost-structure estimate, H2 revenue of about £65m (consensus) yields adjusted EBIT of about £12m to £13.5m (18% to 20%). About £70m is needed for about £15m. Every £1m of Q4 revenue slipping into 2027 costs about £0.6m of EBIT, about 7p of EPS.
  2. FY27 to FY28 consensus. EBIT rises only £1.7m on +£7m revenue in FY27 and £1.8m on +£10m in FY28, implied incremental margins of about 18% to 24%. That is conservative if growth is volume-led and price offsets wage inflation; it is right only if management reinvests the leverage in costs.

The cone. For FY27, organic revenue of −3% to +12% maps to adjusted EBIT of about £15m to £26m (EPS about 165p to 290p). A wide cone, symmetric in mechanics and skewed upward from a trough base.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

❌ D48

Judgement. Financial leverage is moderate and comfortable for solvency. It matters mainly because it constrains acquisitions and the dividend, not because it threatens the balance sheet. Its amplification of EPS is modest.

Position at 30 June 2026:

  • adjusted net debt £45.3m (FactSet net debt £48.2m: debt £63.8m, cash £15.6m);
  • gearing 2.1x adjusted EBITDA (1.5x at December 2025);
  • covenants: net debt no more than 3.0x EBITDA and interest cover at least 3.0x, unchanged;
  • facilities: a £90m revolving facility (£58.6m drawn, £31.4m committed headroom) plus an uncommitted £50m accordion, extended to 1 July 2030 with Lloyds, Santander and HSBC;
  • a proportion of borrowing is in foreign currency to offset translation.

Amplification.

  • Interest is about £3m to £3.5m, giving interest cover of about 6x on FY26 EBIT. A ±10% EBIT move becomes about ±12% to 13% in EPS.
  • Each 100 basis point move in rates is about £0.5m, about 6p of EPS (my estimate on about £50m gross debt, less any hedged portion).

Comfort and stress.

  • At year-end FY26, net debt should fall to about £40m to £44m on H2 cash generation (about 1.6x to 1.8x on £24m to £25m of EBITDA).
  • A covenant breach would need EBITDA below about £15m to £16m with £45m to £48m of net debt, about 35% below FY26 consensus. Remote.
  • The real constraint: holding the dividend (about £8.4m) and doing a £15m to £20m acquisition would push leverage back above 2x.

For the model.

  • Net interest about £3.0m (FY26) falling to about £2.5m (FY27) with deleveraging.
  • Leverage 1.6x to 1.7x at FY26 year-end and about 1.2x to 1.4x by FY27 absent acquisitions. Consensus total debt falls to £49.5m and £38.7m.
  • No equity issuance, consistent with the anti-dilution culture.
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

❌ D42

Judgement. Only a small part of the cost base is cuttable without damaging the franchise: about £3m to £5m in total over two years, about 3% to 4% of costs and including the £2m already taken. Most of the cost base is skilled people and materials that move with volume.

By cost line:

  • Raw materials (about £45m, 31%). Variable. Procurement savings are limited because subsidiaries are small and buy separately. Perhaps 0.5% to 1% of materials (£0.2m to £0.4m) from call-off purchasing and value engineering.
  • Staff (about £48.5m, 33%). About £1.8m was removed in H1 2026, mostly through headcount at underperformers, with £1.0m reinvested in talent. Further cuts of perhaps £1.5m to £2.5m are feasible at the three or four weakest businesses. Deep cuts would damage engineering capability (Renishaw's experts warn about losing talent in downturns), and turnover is already 19%.
  • Central costs (about £5m, up 75% in FY25). These fund the new acquisitions, portfolio and commercial roles, which are core to the strategy. Perhaps £0.5m is trimmable. A reversal is unlikely.
  • R&D (£10.2m, 7%). Should not be cut: below-peer intensity already contributed to share losses at Armfield and Fire Testing Technology.
  • UK tax and National Insurance (about £3m). Not cuttable.
  • Other overheads (about £20m). Some discretionary spend (travel, consultants), perhaps £0.5m to £1m.

Peer precedents. Renishaw cut about 7% of headcount for £21m (about 3% of revenue); Spectris ran a £30m profit improvement programme. Judges' decentralised model makes a group-wide programme culturally unlikely; cuts are business by business.

For the model.

  • About £1m of incremental annualised savings in FY27 beyond the H1 2026 action.
  • Wage inflation of about £2m to £2.5m a year continues.
  • Cost-cutting is not a lever that can replace volume recovery.
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

C62

Judgement. The scope to improve is large relative to the depressed FY26 base, and it comes from a few big areas. All are congruent with the strategy, but three of the four depend on external events.

The levers, estimated against the FY26 base:

  1. Volume recovery to FY25 revenue excluding coring (about £136m) from about £123m to £125m in FY26. About +£11m to £13m of revenue at about 60% drop-through gives about +£7m to £8m of EBIT, about 80p to 95p of EPS. By far the biggest lever, and external (US funding, China, Europe, offshore wind).
  2. Geotek coring from 2028. About +50p per expedition (broker sensitivity). External and binary.
  3. Restarting acquisitions. About +15p to 17p per £10m deployed at 5x. Internal, but capacity- and seller-constrained.
  4. Repairing underperformers (Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, Scientifica back to modest profitability). About +£2m to £3m of EBIT, about 25p to 35p. Internal and slow.
  5. Smaller levers: working-capital release (about 5p of interest), Patent Box (already largely in), price ahead of inflation in commercial niches (about 10p per 1 percentage point of revenue), and mix shift to commercial markets.

Peer precedents:

  • Renishaw lifted margin from 15.7% to 18.7% in one year on +14% revenue plus cost savings;
  • Jenoptik's H1 EBITDA margin went from 15.8% to 19.7% on recovering orders;
  • Judges' own 2020 to 2022 recovery: brokers'-basis EBITDA margin from about 19% to about 28%, with counter-cyclical acquisitions (Armfield, CoolLED). Recovery from trough is achievable and can be fast once volume returns.

For the model.

  • Base case: levers 1 (partially, about half by FY28), 4 (partially) and 5.
  • Bull case: adds 2 and 3.
  • FY28 EPS cone: about 230p to 250p in the base case against about 300p to 350p in a full bull case (coring plus about £30m of acquisitions plus full volume recovery).
C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

❌ D45

Judgement. The perimeter is wide for a £146m-revenue company and is not changing. That means low predictability at group level, despite good diversification of single-product risk.

Business lines. 25 autonomous businesses in two segments: Materials Sciences (£71.9m FY25, including Geotek, Armfield and Fire Testing Technology) and Vacuum (£73.9m). They span fire testing, geotechnical and soil testing, coring, electrophysiology, microscopy preparation, vacuum, thin-film deposition, fibre-optic testing, cryogenics and gas purification.

Geography. Over 85% exported, to more than 100 countries:

North America Rest of Europe UK China and Hong Kong Rest of World
18% 27.8% 14.3% 10.4% 29.6%

FY25 shares. Rest of World includes Geotek coring. Manufacturing is mostly in the UK, plus Geotek in the US and Brazil.

Change. None. No disposals, no acquisitions since 2024, and only product-line exits.

Predictability.

  • The group gives only EPS guidance, two segments of data and order-book weeks. Analysts describe a "black box".
  • Problems at single subsidiaries surface late through impairments or trading updates.
  • Three of the last three years brought guidance changes.

For the model.

  • Forecast at segment level and stress-test the group figure with an explicitly wide band (±10% on EPS for FY27).
  • Use order-book weeks and order-intake growth as the only lead indicators.
  • Do not rely on subsidiary-level bottom-up modelling, because the data do not exist publicly.
  • Segment consensus (Vacuum £62.7m, Materials Sciences £62.6m for FY26) sums to £125m, above the £121m group median, which shows the thin coverage is internally inconsistent.
