Company - Management

C5211 questions
C-5560C-5652C-5750C-57b62C-5850C-5960C-6042C-6145C-6242C-6360C-6452

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Management is in transition from an outstanding founder-led capital allocator to a professionalised, Halma-style team. The design is sensible:

  • a credible chief executive with sector experience;
  • high-calibre hires in acquisitions, portfolio management and commercial;
  • improving standards and governance;
  • shareholder-aligned pay and ownership.

What lowers the score:

  • the recent delivery record (three years of guidance changes);
  • the CFO's resignation seven months into the new chief executive's tenure;
  • the stalled acquisition engine;
  • an opaque communication model;
  • capital-allocation rigidity (a dividend promise ahead of balance-sheet repair, and no buybacks while acquisitions are paused).

For the model:

  • give management no execution premium;
  • lean FY26 slightly below consensus;
  • expect conservative FY27 guidance;
  • treat the first acquisition under the new team and the CFO appointment as the key tests for restoring an acquisition-led growth assumption from FY28.

The culture (frugality, ownership, purchase discipline) reduces downside risk from management error. It does not by itself create upside.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision, particularly in relation to peers?

C60

Question asked How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

Judgement. The ambition is high in compounding terms and modest in scale terms, and it has not been scaled back. The gap between ambition and current delivery is now very wide, which reduces its practical force.

Stated ambition:

  • long-term compound growth through buy-and-build;
  • a medium-term ROTIC of 30%;
  • dividends growing at least 10% a year (a 21% CAGR over 19 years);
  • under Prestidge, three-to-five-year plans that ask each subsidiary how it could double its EBIT;
  • a stated commitment to "durable double-digit total returns".

Relative to peers. Halma targets doubling earnings every five years (about 15% a year) at £2.6bn of revenue. Renishaw targets high single-digit organic growth and a margin above 20%. Jenoptik targets a 20% to 21% EBITDA margin. Judges' compounding ambition matches or exceeds peers in rate, but is not one of scale, technology leadership or platform-building. There is no ambition in software, automation or global service comparable to Halma, Renishaw or Oxford Instruments.

Ambition against delivery:

  • ROTIC is 12.9% against the 30% target;
  • EPS is 47% below peak;
  • no acquisitions since 2024;
  • dividend cover of 1.1x.

The ambition is credible as a long-run statement of intent and not as a near-term forecast. The Halma-style "doubling" exercise is explicitly separate from budgets.

For the model. Ambition does not translate into near-term numbers. Use it only as evidence that management will resume acquisitions and protect R&D as soon as capacity allows.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities: widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C52

Question asked How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement. The core strategy (buy cash-generative niche exporters cheaply, keep them autonomous, recycle cash) is proven and smart. The current priorities are right but not bold. The strategy is less dynamic than its environment now demands.

Smart:

  • it exploits a structural arbitrage (small private businesses at 3x to 7x EBIT, debt at 5% to 6%);
  • it diversifies product risk across 25 niches;
  • low capital intensity;
  • technology-agnostic, so not dependent on a single trend.

Right priorities now:

  • intervening in underperformers;
  • adding central capability;
  • pivoting subsidiaries to commercial and industrial markets;
  • keeping acquisition-price discipline;
  • extending the funding line.

Not bold or dynamic:

  1. There is no response to the software and automation shift (no group software platform, no connectivity standard). This is how the moat could narrow over the mid term.
  2. There is no portfolio pruning (zero divestment) despite clear underperformers.
  3. Capital allocation is rigid: dividends before deleveraging, no buybacks even at a five-year-low multiple while acquisitions are paused.
  4. The strategy has been slow to rebalance away from academic exposure; Oxford Instruments moved faster.
  5. The strategy works in a "normal" funding environment; it has no answer to a multi-year academic freeze except waiting.

For the model. The strategy supports recovery to mid-cycle economics, not a step-change. The mid-term moat is stable to slightly narrowing; there is no strategy-driven growth acceleration.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture?

C50

Question asked How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

Judgement. The new chief executive, Dr Tim Prestidge (in post since 9 February 2026), is credible but largely unproven. His actions so far are sensible and decisive in small ways. The founder's record, which still shapes the company through the Chair, is outstanding over 20 years but poor over the last three.

Prestidge's actions:

  • sector-relevant experience: senior roles at Renishaw and Halma, eight years as a divisional chief executive at Halma, and Judges' Business Development Director from 2023;
  • a quick move to cut about £2m of cost at underperformers while protecting investment;
  • a more explicit intervention model (capability-gap filling, subsidiary strategic plans, stronger financial controls);
  • held guidance and delivered H1 at the midpoint of July's guidance;
  • bought 641 shares at 4,335p in April 2026 (about £28k, small in absolute terms);
  • no acquisition yet;
  • the CFO resigned seven months into his tenure (stated reason: a personal break after more than a decade, not disagreement).

On the Wisdom Library's operator test he has shown agency and discipline but not yet force or outlier delivery.

Cicurel (founder, chief executive 2002 to 2026, now Chair):

  • 25% TSR CAGR from 2005 to 2025;
  • 25 acquisitions at about 6x EBITA;
  • 29% average ROTIC;
  • but two profit warnings in late 2024, FY25 below its own range, the Geotek purchase at 7x pulling ROTIC down, and an overly hands-off approach that let Armfield and Fire Testing Technology drift.

For the model. No CEO premium or discount. Expect a conservative first guidance (March 2027) and steady, incremental execution. Prestidge's first acquisition and the CFO appointment are the two observable tests in the next 12 months.

C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built?

C62

Question asked How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement. Hiring well is clearly a high priority, and the senior team built in 2024 to 2026 is high calibre on paper. Its main weakness is turnover at the top (COO and CFO) during the transition.

Hires:

  • Rik Armitage (Group Acquisitions Executive, July 2025): about 30 years in M&A, ex-head of corporate development at Oxford Instruments, three laboratory-instrument acquisitions there;
  • John Dunne (Portfolio Chief Executive, 2025): ex-Halma divisional chief executive;
  • Dr Ian Wilcock (Group Commercial Director, September 2024): eight years on Oxford Instruments' management board.

Prestidge explicitly said bringing in external resource is critical to fixing underperformers. More than 90% of subsidiary leadership teams have been through the leadership programme. Shore calls the team "high calibre"; Jefferies saw the Executive Committee as positive mitigation of key-person risk.

Weaknesses:

  • the highly regarded COO Mark Lavelle retired;
  • CFO Brad Ormsby resigned, leaving September 2027, with the successor search open;
  • Halma-trained hires could add central bureaucracy to a lean model (central costs up 75%);
  • 19% staff turnover;
  • founder departures from subsidiaries after earn-outs are a known risk.

For the model. The team supports gradual operational repair and a credible restart of acquisitions in 2027 to 2028. The CFO appointment (likely H1 2027) is the key hiring test. A high-quality external CFO from a larger listed peer would reduce governance and forecasting risk.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business?

C50

Question asked How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

Judgement. The business is adequately resourced at the centre and in R&D. Some subsidiaries are under-invested (a historical legacy now being addressed), and the balance sheet is a thinner buffer than in past downturns.

Well resourced:

  • R&D raised to 7.0% of revenue (£10.2m, up 70 basis points) and protected through the downturn;
  • central talent added;
  • the facility extended to 2030;
  • management chose targeted cuts over indiscriminate ones (about £1.8m removed, about £1.0m reinvested in talent);
  • capex is modest because the model is asset-light, while Armfield's insourcing and the Vantaa-style capacity issues of peers do not apply at scale.

Under-resourced or legacy gaps:

  • R&D intensity is still below peers (Renishaw about 10.5% to 13%, Oxford Instruments 8.8%, Spectris 8.1%; Vaisala 11% to 12%);
  • the hands-off model let Armfield (share 40% to 15%), Fire Testing Technology (software) and Scientifica (multiphoton) fall behind;
  • several subsidiaries are sub-scale in sales and service reach;
  • working capital is elevated (16% of sales) partly as inventory buffers.

The buffer. Net debt of 2.1x and dividend cover of 1.1x give less cushion than in 2020, when net debt fell to about £1.3m and the company bought Armfield and CoolLED counter-cyclically. Against the "ant colony" standard (built yesterday for resilience), Judges is moderately prepared operationally but less so financially.

For the model. Keep R&D at about 7% of revenue and capex at about 4%. Assume no capacity constraints on recovery. The financial buffer limits counter-cyclical acquisitions in this downturn, unlike 2020.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

C60

Question asked How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

Judgement. The culture is distinctive and largely congruent: frugal, owner-minded, autonomous, long-term, with heavy employee ownership. Congruence is being tested by the shift towards a more interventionist centre and by a dividend promise that now competes with balance-sheet prudence.

Congruent elements:

  • autonomy is earned through delivery, with a tightly managed central cost line;
  • about 40% of the roughly 822 staff are shareholders through the Share Incentive Plan (312 people, up from 295);
  • average service of seven years, with 23% of staff over ten years;
  • low executive pay (Cicurel £289k; bonus capped at 50% of salary, 50% EPS-based, paid only if EPS beats prior highs);
  • a founder holding about 9.3%;
  • purchase discipline;
  • a permanent-ownership ethos that supports seller trust;
  • the progressive dividend as a cultural promise ("shareholders do not need to sell").

Tensions:

  1. Professional central management (Halma-trained) layered on an autonomy culture risks friction and bureaucracy.
  2. Staff turnover rose to 19% (2025, from 16%), above the UK average of 15%.
  3. Incentives are EPS-heavy (the option hurdle is only 5% EPS CAGR), which may favour margin-dilutive acquisitions over organic capital efficiency.
  4. Extreme decentralisation produced "operational blindness" in underperformers.
  5. Raising the dividend 10% at 1.1x cover keeps a cultural promise at some cost to financial congruence.

The Wisdom Library's Lifco and Avanza standard (goals, strategy, culture and execution all aligned) is largely met in design and partly in current practice.

For the model. Culture supports cost discipline and cash generation through the trough. It reduces the risk of value-destructive, overpriced acquisitions and raises the probability that the dividend is maintained.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

❌ D42

Judgement. Communication is plain-spoken on strategy and values, but opaque on the transmission mechanism. It is not very helpful for forecasting, and the "black box" is a real cost to the investment case.

Helpful:

  • consistent articulation of the strategy and acquisition criteria;
  • order-book weeks and order intake disclosed at each update;
  • regional order commentary (US −7%, China −47%, Europe −20%);
  • explicit guidance assumptions (no US recovery, no coring in FY26);
  • candour on unsustainable dividend cover and on the heavier Q4 weighting;
  • early warning of Geotek slippage;
  • timely trading updates (January, March, July, September).

Unhelpful:

  • no subsidiary-level data, and competitive sensitivity cited for not discussing businesses;
  • no quantified price, volume or mix bridge;
  • no pipeline commentary;
  • deflection on Armfield and on questions about US university settlements;
  • two reporting segments for 25 businesses;
  • three consecutive years of guidance changes undermine past signals;
  • no dedicated investor-relations executive, with the departing CFO the main contact;
  • data inconsistencies between trading updates and interims (H1 revenue £55.5m against £55.7m);
  • Financial Reporting Council-driven restatements.

For the model. The Consumer must work from group-level order and revenue data and accept wide error bands. The most useful disclosed indicators are order-book weeks (watch the 13-to-14-week floor), order-intake growth by region, and the H1/H2 split.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment?

❌ D45

Question asked How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement. The long-term record is outstanding, but the recent record (FY24 to FY26) is one of misses and justifications. It is not a record of delivery in a tough environment, and recent action has been incremental rather than huge.

Long term:

  • 20 years of compounding;
  • failed to deliver record results only five times in twenty years;
  • recovered quickly from the 2014-16 and 2020 downturns through cash generation and counter-cyclical acquisitions.

Recent:

  • FY24. November 2024 cut from about 349p consensus to a 270p to 300p range; delivered 283.4p.
  • FY25. July 2025 range of 285p to 330p (against 367p consensus); delivered 275.3p, below the range.
  • FY26. January 2026 rebased by about a third to 200p to 250p; H1 delivered on the midpoint of July's 38p to 42p guide.

The misses were mainly external, but peers such as Oxford Instruments showed better resilience through commercial exposure.

Actions taken:

  • leadership succession;
  • senior hires;
  • about £2m of cost cuts;
  • Scientifica product exit;
  • facility extended;
  • Geotek do Brasil minority bought.

Actions not taken:

  • no acquisitions in nearly two years;
  • no portfolio pruning;
  • no balance-sheet repair (the dividend rose instead).

For the model. Apply a credibility discount to management's FY26 to FY27 outlooks. Lean the FY26 forecast slightly below consensus and treat the January 2027 trading update as the first test of whether the new team can deliver a heavily back-ended year.

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

❌ D42

Judgement. Historically Judges held outlier standards in capital returns and margins:

  • the top ROCE among UK industrials from FY18 to FY22 (Jefferies);
  • EBITA margins about 400 basis points above Halma and Spirax;
  • top-decile cash conversion.

Today it is not out-executing competitors. Its outlier status is limited to cash conversion and frugal pay.

Current evidence:

  • sixth of six peers on fundamental earnings momentum;
  • an 8.6% H1 2026 margin against Halma at 22.7% and Oxford Instruments at about 18%;
  • ROTIC of 12.9% against Halma's 16.2%;
  • organic decline while Halma grew 16% and Renishaw 17% in constant currency.

Peers handled the same academic headwind better through commercial pivots (Oxford Instruments' commercial orders +25.4%) and cost programmes (Renishaw).

Remaining outlier traits:

  • cash conversion of about 118% (FY25);
  • dividend record (20 years of at least 10% increases);
  • acquisition price discipline (3x to 7x against Halma at 10x to 12x);
  • bottom-quartile executive pay.

The new team's standards. Raising standards through ISO9001, financial controls and subsidiary plans is necessary catch-up, not outlier.

For the model. No execution premium. Assume peer-average execution on cost and pricing and below-peer growth, given the end-market mix.

C-63

To what extent does the company put shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent is its governance?

C60

Question asked Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

Judgement. On the evidence Judges puts shareholders first, ahead of management, in economic terms. Formal governance scaffolding is weaker than at FTSE peers but improving.

Shareholders first:

  • executive pay is bottom-quartile (Cicurel £289k in 2025; CFO £307k; bonus capped at 50% of salary; nil bonuses in weak years: only the ESG element paid in 2025; 2023 options lapsed);
  • the founder holds about 9.3% to 10.7%, far above a peer median of about 0.4%;
  • no dilutive equity issuance (debt and cash fund acquisitions);
  • 20 years of progressive dividends;
  • zero open-market insider sales in the last twelve months, and insider purchases (Prestidge, Nyman);
  • malus and clawback introduced in 2026;
  • the succession was consulted with major holders;
  • no poison pills; a single share class.

Governance weaknesses:

  • only three of eight directors independent (38%; Halma 73%, Oxford Instruments 57%);
  • the Chair is the former chief executive and chairs the Nomination Committee;
  • the Deputy Chair (Elman) has been on the board since 2002;
  • the board exceptionally extended an option expiry for Cicurel (a gain of about £349k, January 2026);
  • the Senior Independent Director received a £0.4m introduction fee on the Geotek deal (2023);
  • the option hurdle of 5% EPS CAGR is soft;
  • Financial Reporting Council-driven restatements and an auditor change (BDO to RSM);
  • whistleblowing relies on internal channels.

The Wisdom Library's "Insider Ownership Is Not Alignment" caveat applies only mildly: low pay and no selling corroborate the alignment.

For the model. Governance does not change the earnings forecast. It supports low risk of value-extracting behaviour. Keep a small risk premium for board independence and audit transition.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

C52

Judgement. Judges was an enlightened capital allocator for 20 years: about 6x EBITA purchases, a 29% average ROTIC, and cash recycled without dilution. The recent record has slipped, and it is not a dynamic repurchaser. It explicitly rejects buybacks.

Record:

  • 25 acquisitions since 2005;
  • standout successes (Fire Testing Technology at £3.7m in 2005, Sircal, Deben, CoolLED, Korvus);
  • Geotek (£80m at 7x, the largest deal) added lumpiness and diluted ROTIC from 22.7% to 17.8%;
  • Armfield and Scientifica (the third and fourth largest deals) underperformed and were impaired;
  • Rockwash missed its earn-out;
  • the 2024 deals (Teer £12.3m, Luciol, Rockwash) are too recent to judge. Recent marginal returns on new invested capital are clearly below the historical 29%.

Current allocation choices:

  1. The dividend first. +10% at 1.1x cover, consuming about £8m a year.
  2. No buybacks. The board considers acquisitions and organic investment superior. Yet with acquisitions paused and the share at about 14.5x forward P/E (an earnings yield of about 7%, an EV/EBIT of about 13x, versus about 5x for private targets), acquisitions remain far more accretive than buybacks, so the board's logic holds only if deals actually happen. A buyback at a five-year-low multiple funded by debt at about 6% would be EPS-accretive (about 1% to 2% per £5m) but would strain leverage.
  3. Discipline. Management refuses to overpay, which is enlightened.

For the model. Assume capital is returned only through dividends (+10% a year while EPS of at least about 190p holds) and redeployed into acquisitions from H2 2027 at 5x to 6x EBIT as an upside scenario. Assume no buybacks.

Risks

❌ D384 questions
C-8025C-8138C-8235C-8355

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The dominant risk is not structural failure. The cash, tangible returns and niche moats contradict that. It is that the turn proves later and shallower than the FY26 to FY27 consensus assumes. That would happen through a slip in Q4 2026 conversion, a second leg of US funding cuts, or Geotek slipping again, at a time when both growth engines are already stalled and leverage (2.1x) and a 1.1x-covered dividend leave little room.

Sentiment risks are amplified by:

  • small size and illiquidity;
  • thin, house-broker coverage;
  • management change;
  • a hostile discount-rate regime.

The Risks report's FALSE FRIEND verdict should be weighed by its mechanisms, not its label (see reading note 6):

  • deferred shipments in the order book are real;
  • cash conversion flattered by Geotek advances is real;
  • the "masked structural decay" reading is not supported, given intact pricing and orders recovering to −1% year to date.

Valuation already prices a trough multiple on consensus, so downside of about 30% to 40% needs an earnings miss.

For the model:

  • base case: FY26 about 185p to 200p;
  • downside case: about 170p FY26 and about 185p to 190p FY27;
  • explicit tails for Geotek, the dividend and FX.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

❌ D25

Judgement. Yes, severely. Both of Judges' growth engines, organic volume and acquisitions, have decelerated at the same time and are running far below normal. That has not happened before in its listed history. The input change force (US academic funding) has stopped worsening but has not yet started improving.

Organic engine.

  • The normal rate is about 7% a year (19-year average).
  • Excluding coring: about −2% in FY24, roughly flat to +2% in FY25 (sources conflict), and about −9% to −12% in H1 2026.
  • Headline H1 2026 revenue fell 21%, including the loss of coring revenue against H1 2025.
  • Order intake: −12% in H1 2026 (Q1 −18%), recovering to −1% year to date by September.
  • The 2024 and 2025 back-to-back declines broke the historical pattern of single-year dips.

Acquisition engine. It normally adds about 1.3 deals a year and roughly 10 to 12 percentage points of revenue growth. There have been no acquisitions in FY25 or H1 2026, the longest gap since 2021.

Input force.

  • US National Institutes of Health grants: Q3 2026 −8% year on year (+3% on a trailing twelve-month basis).
  • Lab surveys remain weak: 75% describe the environment as weak.
  • The FY27 budget request proposes new cuts.
  • China administrative delays are resolving.
  • Bruker's book-to-bill has been above 1 for several quarters, and Deutsche Bank argues the US academic bottom is in.

The force is decelerating in its rate of decline, consistent with the "research-budget floor" read in the Sector Digest. It is not yet a positive driver.

For the model. FY26 is the trough. FY27 organic growth is about 6% to 9%, mostly mechanical. FY28 to FY29 is about 4% to 6%. Acquisitions are excluded until proven.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

❌ D38

Judgement. Seven sudden, high-magnitude fundamental risks matter. The first is the most plausible and the most immediate.

# Risk Plausibility Magnitude
1 Q4 2026 shipment or conversion failure, leading to another guidance miss About 30% to 35% for a miss of 5% or more FY26 EPS about 165p to 185p against about 200p consensus; FY27 cut 5% to 10%; share −15% to −25%
2 US funding second leg down About 15% to 20% US academic orders −10% to −15% more; FY27 EPS −8% to −12%
3 Geotek coring cancelled or deferred beyond 2028 About 20% Removes the main FY28 to FY29 upside (about 50p)
4 Dividend cut About 10% to 15% Sentiment shock larger than the cash effect
5 A second acquisition "cockroach" About 25% over 18 months Statutory, plus loss of trust in the acquisition engine
6 Sterling spike About 10% to 15% Unhedged after 12 months; up to about £5m to £9m of EBIT on my estimate
7 Governance or accounting event during the auditor and CFO transitions Low, about 5% to 10% High credibility damage

Detail on each:

  1. Q4 conversion. Customers asked for delayed shipments, China paperwork has to be finalised, and about 80% of FY EPS falls in H2. Every £1m of Q4 revenue that slips costs about 7p.
  2. US funding. This would happen if the FY27 appropriations process enacts even part of the requested cuts (National Institutes of Health −10%, National Science Foundation −54%) or freezes grants again.
  3. Geotek. Oceanographic programmes are government-funded and the expedition has already slipped twice (2026, then 2027, now 2028 or later).
  4. Dividend. This would follow if FY26 to FY27 earnings disappoint while net debt stays at about 2x. It would end a 20-year record of at least 10% increases.
  5. Acquisition cockroach. Further impairment at Fire Testing Technology or Scientifica, or another deal falling short of plan (the Wisdom Library's "Cockroach Pattern at M&A Integration Level" puts a second failure at 30% to 50% conditional on a first).
  6. Sterling. Above about 1.40 to the dollar, together with euro strength.
  7. Governance. RSM's first audit (FY26), with prior Financial Reporting Council restatements, could find further adjustments. There are no fraud signals: no short sellers, clean cash.

For the model. Build the downside scenario around risk 1 plus risk 2: FY26 EPS about 170p, FY27 about 185p to 190p. Treat risks 3 to 7 as tails.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

❌ D35

Judgement. Sentiment risks are elevated because Judges is a small, illiquid, thinly covered, levered UK stock in a market regime that is punishing exactly that profile.

  1. Discount-rate and small-cap risk-off. The US 10-year yield is about 5.1% and the Fragility tilt is Double Underweight. The market capitalisation is about £230m and the stock trades about $0.8m to $1.4m a day, so forced selling can move it 10% or more without news. It already fell about 20% in the three months after an update that held guidance.
  2. Sector read-across from academic-exposed peers. A profit warning or negative academic commentary from Bruker, Oxford Instruments, Thermo Fisher or Waters, or weak monthly National Institutes of Health grant data, would hit the stock regardless of its own orders.
  3. De-rating of serial acquirers and quality compounders. Halma's Avo-driven de-rating (−15% on results day), compounder outflows, or failures at other UK roll-ups.
  4. US political headlines. Budget proposals and grant freezes. The FY27 budget request already proposes deep cuts, so every appropriations headline is a risk.
  5. Coverage and broker risk. Only three brokers, two of them corporate or house brokers with no formal rating (Zeus, Shore) and Investec at Buy. A single bearish initiation or a target cut could matter. "100% Buy" is not an independent signal.
  6. Management-change perception. The CFO's resignation, and any further departure (for example a founder of a large subsidiary), would feed the "black box" narrative.
  7. China and US–China trade or export-control escalation.

For the model. Sentiment does not change the forecasts. It argues for a valuation discount and a wide price cone until order growth is visible in reported revenue (H1 2027).

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C55

Judgement. On consensus earnings the shares already trade at a trough multiple. The 24-month-forward P/E of about 14.1x to 14.5x is at the 0th percentile of five years (low 13.8x) and below the 10-year 10th percentile (15.1x). Further downside must come mainly from earnings disappointment combined with a modest further de-rating. A reasonable trough scenario implies about 30% to 40% downside. A consensus-delivery scenario implies little downside from the multiple.

Scenarios (price about 3,450p):

Scenario Basis Price Change
Consensus holds, multiple at five-year low 24-month-forward EPS 2.44 at 13.8x about 3,370p −2%
Moderate disappointment FY27 EPS about 200p at 13x about 2,600p −25%
Trough, P/E basis FY26 about 170p and FY27 about 185p to 190p, at 12x to 13x P/E about 2,250p to 2,450p −30% to −35%
Trough, EV basis FY27 adjusted EBIT about £17m at 11x EV/EBIT, less about £45m to £48m of net debt, on 6.7m shares about 2,100p −40%
Jefferies' published bear case 12x EV/EBITA 3,110p n/a

The Jefferies figure was set in early 2026 on earnings that have since been cut.

What supports a floor:

  • cash conversion of about 100%;
  • a dividend yield of about 3.7% (FY26E 126p);
  • returns on tangible capital above 40%;
  • no solvency risk;
  • potential bid interest (Chinese and private-equity buyers active in UK scientific instruments).

For the model and the later sets. Downside is driven by earnings and probably capped at about 35% to 40% on a twelve-month view.

Upside if the engine restarts:

Scenario Basis Price
Base FY28 EPS about 240p at the 10-year median of 21.6x about 5,200p
Partial About 18x about 4,300p

The skew is favourable, but only conditional on earnings stabilising.

Guidance

❌ D446 questions
C-6845C-7042C-7148C-7235C-8555C-7338

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judges' guidance is an EPS-range system with few building blocks.

  • FY26. The formal range is 200p to 250p, but the company has anchored to the 200p consensus floor. The inputs are conservative (no US recovery, no coring); the execution is demanding (about 80% of EPS in H2, a Q4 surge).
  • FY27. No guidance yet. Informal commentary is modestly upbeat.
  • Record. No upgrades since FY23 and three consecutive downgrades. The downgrades were prompt and honest, which preserves some credibility. The new chief executive has no guidance record.

For the model:

  • treat FY26 guidance as a ceiling more than a floor, with my base case at about 185p to 200p;
  • expect conservative FY27 guidance in March 2027 (plausibly bracketing about 200p to 230p);
  • look for the first upgrade opportunity in the July 2027 trading update, when easy comparisons and orders recovered from 2026 could show through.

The lack of specificity means the Consumer must build the H2 2026 and FY27 bridges independently.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

❌ D45

Judgement.

FY26. Guidance is effectively in line with consensus. Both sit at the bottom of the formal range.

  • In January 2026 the board set 200p to 250p of adjusted EPS (cut from about 300p consensus).
  • At the July trading update it said full-year expectations were in line with consensus of about 202.8p, and at the September interims it affirmed market expectations (about 200.5p consensus).
  • FactSet consensus is now 199p median and 200p mean (Zeus 199p, Shore 200p, Investec 206p).

The formal range's midpoint (225p) is therefore about 12% above consensus. In practice the company has anchored to the 200p floor.

Why the floor. The range's conservative inputs (no US recovery, no coring) were offset by events after January: the China tax delay, a weaker Q1, offshore wind. The remaining path depends on an unusually heavy Q4.

FY27. There is no guidance. Consensus is 219p median and 224p mean (Shore 212p, Zeus 219p, Investec 243p). Management's qualitative stance (order momentum returning, cost actions giving leverage) is consistent with consensus. It has not said so formally and will not before March 2027.

For the model. Use about 200p as the guided FY26 figure, with my base case slightly below it (about 185p to 200p). Treat FY27 as unguided. The first FY27 guidance from the new team is likely to be conservative: a range bracketing about 200p to 230p would be in keeping with recent practice.

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

❌ D42

Judgement. There is no recent record of upgrading. Since FY23 the behaviour has been prompt, repeated downgrading: FY24 in November 2024, FY25 in July 2025, FY26 in January 2026. The founder's historical style was "under-promise and over-deliver", which produced beats against conservative bases rather than formal mid-year upgrades. The new chief executive has no record yet.

Evidence.

  • Historically Cicurel guided conservatively and delivered consistent multi-year outperformance.
  • FY23 landed inside a 367p to 379p range (374.6p).
  • The Change Forces report classifies current management as an "honest tracker forced into a highly reactive posture", not a sandbagger.
  • Downgrades were rapid and proactive: management reset quickly rather than defending stale numbers, which is a positive for credibility in the downward direction.
  • Upward revisions are absent because the environment has not allowed them.
  • Peers such as Halma and Vaisala show the "guide low, upgrade twice" pattern. Judges' chief executive (ex-Halma) may adopt it, but there is no evidence yet.

For the model. Do not assume any guidance upgrade in FY26. For FY27 the likelier pattern is a conservative opening range followed by a "trading in line" or "ahead" update at the July 2027 trading statement if orders sustain. That is a 2027 catalyst, not a 2026 one.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

❌ D48

Judgement. The FY26 guidance is conservative in its stated inputs and not conservative in its required execution. The implied out-year path (consensus, since there is no guidance) is conservative against the last five years, and roughly appropriate given today's weaker but stabilising change forces.

FY26.

  • About 200p implies EPS −27% and an adjusted operating margin of about 15% to 17%, against 19% to 27% over FY21 to FY25.
  • It assumes zero US recovery and zero coring, both conservative relative to how forces are evolving.
  • But it depends on about 80% of EPS in H2 (historically about 50% to 55%) and on a Q4 shipment surge.
  • Adjusted for change forces (the US worse than ever, China delayed, offshore wind weak), the inputs are realistic, not sandbagged.

FY27 to FY28 (consensus):

FY27 FY28 History
Revenue growth +6% +8% Organic average about 7%
EBIT margin about 17% about 17% 19% to 27%
EPS growth +10% +13%
Coring and acquisitions None None

Against the last five years this is conservative. Against current forces (US flat, China normalising, commercial resilient, operating leverage of about 60%) the margin path looks conservative if volumes recover, and fair if they do not.

For the model.

  • FY26. Risk skewed slightly down (execution).
  • FY27 to FY28. Risk skewed up on margins (operating leverage) and neutral on revenue.
  • The best-founded upward revision is FY28 EBIT (about £24m consensus against about £26m to £28m achievable if revenue reaches about £140m to £145m at a margin of about 19% to 20%).
C-72

How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

❌ D35

Judgement. Guidance is not very specific. It is an EPS range with two stated assumptions and no published building blocks. That limits its usefulness and contributes to the "black box" discount.

Given:

  • the FY26 adjusted EPS range (200p to 250p), later anchored to "market expectations";
  • explicit assumptions of no US recovery and no Geotek coring revenue;
  • commentary on order intake, order-book weeks and the expected Q4 weighting;
  • an indication of cost actions (about £2m);
  • Patent Box support mentioned qualitatively.

Not given:

  • revenue, margin, tax or interest guidance;
  • segment or regional bridges;
  • price and volume split;
  • cost inflation;
  • the size of coring sensitivity (supplied by brokers, about 50p per expedition);
  • acquisition assumptions;
  • medium-term targets other than the 30% ROTIC.

Consequence. Brokers' line items are inconsistent with one another (revenue £120m to £130m, EBITDA £24.6m to £26.5m for similar EPS), and the H2 bridge can be built several ways (high margin, or Patent Box catch-up). The Consumer must build the bridge independently.

For the model. Treat brokers' line items as indicative only. Rebuild FY26 bottom-up from H1 actuals, an H2 revenue assumption, about 60% drop-through, the cost actions, and an explicit tax-rate choice.

C-85

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C55

Judgement.

FY26. Informal commentary is in line with formal guidance and consensus. It is careful, not promotional.

FY27 and beyond. Informal commentary is modestly more positive than the cautious consensus.

More positive:

  • emphasis on the summer order rebound (−12% to −1% year to date) and on China procedures resolving;
  • eight businesses growing on semiconductor and battery demand;
  • cost actions "should deliver positive operating leverage when demand recovers" (relayed by Shore);
  • Cicurel: long-term growth drivers "completely unaffected";
  • Q4 phasing is "planned for and manageable";
  • the deal pipeline is "healthy";
  • the 30% ROTIC ambition reaffirmed.

More cautious:

  • the admission that 1.1x dividend cover is "not sustainable";
  • acknowledgement that US funding "has not flowed normally" and customers asked for delayed shipments;
  • geopolitical caution;
  • a refusal to give pipeline detail.

Why. Management wants to defend the long-term compounding narrative and dividend confidence while avoiding another formal downgrade. Hence formal anchoring to the consensus floor for FY26 and upbeat framing for 2027.

For the model. The informal commentary supports an FY27 forecast at or slightly above consensus (about 220p to 235p), if FY26 delivers. It should not be used to raise FY26.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

❌ D38

Judgement. Six features are material and uncommon.

  1. Reporting. AIM listing and a decentralised "black box": no subsidiary-level data across 25 businesses, two segments, management declining to discuss individual units or the pipeline.
  2. Order-book weeks as the main KPI. Useful as a lead indicator: Risks and Sector notes treat about 13 weeks as the floor, and it is now 17.3. But it contains shipments customers have asked to defer, which makes it a softer measure than it looks.
  3. Coverage. Very thin and house-dominated: Zeus and Shore (no formal ratings), Investec (Buy); Jefferies dropped coverage in April 2026 at Underperform. The "100% buy share" and +67 point buy-rating change are artefacts of composition, not conviction. Consensus has no independent bear.
  4. Investor relations is concentrated in the executives. There is no investor-relations officer, and the departing CFO is the main investor contact.
  5. The dividend commitment. At least 10% a year for 20 years is quasi-guidance that management signals even at 1.1x cover.
  6. Calendar and data quality. - Results cadence: a January pre-close update (next January 2027), full-year results (24 March 2027), a July trading update and September interims. - Data inconsistencies between trading updates and interims. - Restatements prompted by the Financial Reporting Council. - Reliance on Patent Box in FY26 EPS. - A change of auditor for FY26.

For the model. Expect sparse, binary information events (January, March, July, September). Forecast error between them will be large.

Sell-side estimates have been cut deeply (about −33% over twelve months) across all three remaining brokers. Guidance was rebased on conservative inputs, so the classic clearing conditions are mostly in place.

What remains:

  • FY26. It still carries execution risk. The FactSet line items are internally inconsistent: revenue of £121m with an H2 margin of about 24% would be needed for EBIT of £20.3m. A Patent Box element of uncertain recurrence is embedded in the narrative bridge.

C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

C50

Question asked How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

Judgement. Consensus has three different credibility profiles:

  • FY26: demanding in its H2 phasing and internally inconsistent in its line items;
  • FY27 to FY28: credible on revenue and conservative on margins and operating leverage;
  • throughout: excludes acquisitions and coring, both historically integral.

Line by line (FactSet median, three brokers; FY25 actual on the brokers' basis):

  • Revenue. £146m → £121m (−17%) → £128m (+6%) → £138m (+8%). History: +14%, +24%, +20%, −2%, +9% (FY21 to FY25), with about 7% organic plus acquisitions. FY26 is consistent with H1 −21% and H2 about −14%. The orders data (year to date −1%, book-to-bill above 1) argue FY26 revenue could be higher (£123m to £128m) unless Q4 slips. FY27 and FY28 are plausible without acquisitions.
  • EBITDA margin. 22.5% (FY25) → 20.4%, 20.7%, 20.6%. History: 28% (FY23), 24% (FY24).
  • EBIT margin. About 16.8%, 17.2%, 17.2%, against company-adjusted 26.6%, 25.6%, 20.9% and 19.2% (FY22 to FY25).
  • The FY26 inconsistency. Revenue of £121m with EBIT of £20.3m requires an H2 margin of about 24% on about £65m of revenue. On my cost-structure estimate that needs about £70m of revenue, or a large tax benefit. The narrative bridge (Shore: H2 margin "over 20%" plus about 30p of Patent Box) differs from the line-item bridge.
  • FY27 to FY28 incremental margins. About 18% to 24%, against roughly 60% structural drop-through. Conservative if growth is volume-led.
  • Tax. About 22% to 23% implied (PBT £17.6m, net income £13.6m in FY26). Company-adjusted 21% (FY24) and 18% (H1 26). Depending on Patent Box, consensus may be slightly conservative on FY27 to FY28 tax, while FY26's EPS may embed a one-off.
  • Interest. About £2.7m (FY26) to £2.6m (FY27) implied, against about £3.5m to £4.2m historically on FactSet. Slightly light unless deleveraging is fast.
  • Share count. About 6.7m to 6.8m, flat. Fine, with about 1% dilution.
  • Capex. £5.0m to £5.3m (about 4% of revenue), in line with history.
  • Operating cash flow. £21.0m to £21.9m.
  • DPS. 126p, 139p, 146p (+10%, +10%, +5%). Credible if EPS of at least about 190p holds.
  • Total debt. £58.3m → £49.5m → £38.7m. Assumes no acquisitions.
  • EPS. 199p, 219p, 248p, against 374.6p, 283.4p and 275.3p (FY23 to FY25).

Credibility against change forces and foundations.

  • FY26. Slightly optimistic: base case about 185p to 200p.
  • FY27. Fair to slightly conservative: about 215p to 235p achievable if FY26 delivers and Q4 slippage moves into FY27.
  • FY28. Conservative on margin: about 240p to 260p achievable without coring or acquisitions.
  • Upside not in consensus. About 50p from coring and about 15p to 17p per £10m of acquisitions.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

❌ D30

Judgement. The record is poor over one and three years. It is good over five to twenty years and in earlier comparable downturns.

Last year:

  • FY25 adjusted EPS of 275.3p came in below the July 2025 range of 285p to 330p (about 10% below the midpoint), roughly in line with the reduced consensus of about 275p by January 2026;
  • H1 2026 landed at the midpoint of the July 2026 guidance (39p against 38p to 42p) and in line with consensus.

Last three years (start-of-year consensus against outcome):

Year Start-of-year consensus Outcome Gap
FY24 about 349p 283.4p about −19%
FY25 about 367p to 375p 275.3p about −26%
FY26 about 300p about 200p consensus now about −33%

Each year required a mid-year or start-of-year downgrade. Brokers called this "unprecedented" for Judges.

Last five years. FY21 to FY23 were record years, met or beat (FY23 374.6p within 367p to 379p), with margins at a peak of 26% to 27%.

Comparable conditions. In the 2014-16 downturn (organic −3%) and in 2020 (H1 organic −12%) Judges met reduced expectations and recovered quickly, helped by counter-cyclical acquisitions and deleveraging (net debt to about £1.3m in 2020). The current downturn is longer (two-plus years), deeper and coincides with leadership change and an acquisition pause. So the earlier pattern of resilience is less applicable.

For the model. Apply a negative bias to start-of-year expectations until a clean year is delivered. FY26 is the test, with heavy H2 dependence.

C-74

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

C62

Judgement. Estimates have been cut deeply across all analysts, and guidance was rebased lower with conservative inputs. Both "clearing" conditions are largely met. FY26 is not fully de-risked, because its execution profile is demanding. FY27 estimates are still drifting slightly lower.

Depth of cuts:

  • FactSet FY26 EPS −33.9% over twelve months (range 4.47 to 2.00) and FY27 −33.4%;
  • FY27 revisions: EPS −33%, EBITDA −30%, sales −13% over twelve months;
  • three months: EPS −8%, EBITDA −5%, sales −4%, target price −10%;
  • one month: zero.
  • Jefferies cut EPS by up to 36% before dropping coverage. Zeus and Shore rebased by 33% in January 2026.
  • Every broker sits at the bottom of the guidance range (199p to 206p).

Rebasing. The January 2026 range (200p to 250p, down from about 300p) assumed no US recovery and no coring. That is a "kitchen-sink of inputs". The 2024 and 2025 resets were also aggressive.

Not sandbagged. The H2 hurdle (about 160p against about 134p in H2 2025) makes the rebased figure a stretch on execution. Analysts call the year a "trough" but not a beatable one.

Read-through. This matches the "huge change-from-current clearing event" setup (deep cuts, rebased guidance, disruptive twelve months). Capitulation is ambiguous: the share is −40% over twelve months, but house brokers keep targets of 7,000p to 7,200p. The Wisdom Library's "Composite Signal Masks Component Deterioration" applies: the positive buy-share change hides still-negative profit-line revisions over three months.

For the model. Treat FY27 to FY28 estimates as largely cleaned. Treat FY26 as the last risk point. Positive revisions are plausible from H1 2027 if orders hold.

Stock market narrative

C581 question
C-2258

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The market narrative today is "former premium compounder turned academic-funding casualty in a stalled roll-up, with management change", priced at a five-year-low multiple.

A credible staircase exists:

  1. January and March 2027: an FY26 delivered without a further downgrade and a conservative FY27 guide.
  2. July to September 2027: reported growth and margin recovery against very easy H1 2026 comparisons, plus a possible first acquisition and a new CFO.
  3. 2028: Geotek coring and re-established acquisition compounding.

The early steps are confirmatory and depend on Q4 2026 execution. The mid steps are mechanical and well founded if order intake holds. The large novel steps are binary and later-dated.

For the later sets, the narrative can shift from "casualty" to "recovery" within 6 to 12 months if the January update is clean. It will not return to "compounder" until an acquisition completes and ROTIC visibly rises.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C58

Judgement. There is a plausible staircase. The near steps are confirmatory rather than novel. The most novel and best-founded step is the mechanical return to reported growth in 2027 against very easy comparisons. The big novel upside steps (Geotek coring, an acquisition restart, new mid-term targets) sit in months 6 to 36 and are less well founded.

Next six months (to about April 2027):

  1. January 2027 pre-close trading update. Delivery of FY26 at about 200p, Q4 conversion, full-year order intake and order-book weeks. Moderately novel, the first "no further downgrade" year since FY23. Medium-founded, given the Q4 risk.
  2. China catch-up shipments and confirmation that the tax-exemption procedures are resolved. Low novelty; well founded on management's and JEOL's statements.

  3. 24 March 2027 FY26 results: - first full-year outlook under Prestidge (FY27 guidance); - final dividend (+10% would mark a 20th consecutive year); - possibly a CFO successor; - possibly the first acquisition under the new deal team. Medium novelty. CFO and acquisition news are low to medium-founded on timing.

  4. US FY27 appropriations outcome (likely another stopgap) and monthly grant data. Low novelty, poorly founded as a positive.

Months 6 to 18 (to about April 2028):

  1. July 2027 trading update and September 2027 interims. H1 2027 laps H1 2026's −21% revenue and −72% EPS, so headline organic growth (plausibly double-digit revenue growth and EPS more than doubling from 39p) and margin recovery towards about 18% to 20%. Highly novel to the market (the first reported growth since H1 2025). Well founded mechanically if the H2 2026 run-rate holds.
  2. Restart of acquisitions. One or two deals at about 5x EBIT under Armitage. Novel and value-signalling. Medium-founded: there is capacity, but sellers and competition constrain it.
  3. Geotek contract signature for a 2028 expedition. Novel (re-opens about 50p of upside). Low to medium-founded given two slips.
  4. CFO handover (September 2027) and leverage falling to about 1.3x to 1.5x, with dividend cover rebuilding to about 1.7x. Low novelty, well founded.
  5. Repair at underperformers. Armfield insourcing complete; Fire Testing Technology's new cone calorimeter. Low to medium novelty.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029):

  1. The Geotek coring expedition itself (2028 or later), worth about 50p of EPS in the year. Novel; medium-founded.
  2. Compounding from acquisitions re-established and ROTIC rising towards about 18% to 20%. Novel; medium-founded.
  3. First mid-term targets or a capital markets event from the new chief executive and CFO, plausibly framed in Halma style. Novel; low to medium-founded.
  4. Structural mix shift (commercial share up) and European FP10 funding from 2028. Low novelty.

For the model and the later sets. The narrative staircase most likely to move consensus is the H1 2027 reported recovery plus the July 2027 trading update. The binary novel upsides (coring, an acquisition restart) should be scenario layers, not base case.
