Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB, AIM: JDG): a UK buy-and-build group of about 25 autonomous niche scientific-instrument manufacturers selling mainly to academic, research-institute and industrial laboratories worldwide, reported in two segments, Materials Sciences (including Geotek coring and core logging, Armfield, Fire Testing Technology) and Vacuum (including UHV Design, Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal, Scientifica, Korvus).

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • Halma (HLMA-GB): decentralised UK acquirer of niche technology businesses with published sector margin data.
  • Renishaw (RSW-GB): UK-based precision metrology maker with a largely fixed UK cost base and a recent headcount and cost programme.
  • Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB): UK scientific-instrument maker with high academic exposure that shifted to commercial markets.
  • SDI Group (SDI-GB): the closest listed small UK serial acquirer of scientific-instrument businesses.
  • Spectris (private since its acquisition by private equity): profit improvement programme in scientific instruments.
  • Bruker (BRKR-US): academic-exposed instrument maker reporting through the 2025–26 US funding disruption.
  • Jenoptik (JEN-DE) and Vaisala (VAIAS-FI): European precision-instrument peers with fixed-cost home manufacturing.
  • Private competitors GUNT and TecQuipment: rivals to Judges' Armfield subsidiary.

Inputs:

  • Fixed and variable costs. What share of Judges' cost base is fixed versus variable, given raw materials and consumables of £44.8m and staff costs of £48.5m against FY2025 revenue of £145.8m (Annual Report 2025)?
  • H1 2026 movement. How did adjusted operating profit move with revenue between H1 2025 and H1 2026 (revenue £70.2m to £55.7m; adjusted operating profit £14.3m to £4.8m; interim results, 23 September 2026)? How much of the fall came from the absence of Geotek coring rather than from the rest of the portfolio?
  • Earlier downturns and recoveries. How did Judges' margins move in the 2014–16 downturn, in 2020 (H1 2020 organic revenue down 12%), in the 2021–23 recovery and in FY2024–FY2025? What incremental margin did recovering volume produce in each recovery?
  • The 2026 cost actions. What did the actions taken in 2026 consist of (cost base reduced by £2.0m at underperforming businesses, H1 2026), how much is permanent, and what was reinvested? What further restructuring is feasible at Armfield, Fire Testing Technology and Scientifica?
  • Central costs. How large are group central costs after the reported 75% rise in FY2025 (Jefferies), and which new central roles are permanent?
  • UK taxes and wages. What is the ongoing effect of higher UK employer National Insurance and corporation tax (company: about £3m a year)? What wage inflation is running for skilled engineers at UK instrument makers?
  • Peer cost programmes. What did peer programmes actually deliver against what was promised, and how long did each take: Renishaw's £21m cost programme with a 7% headcount reduction; Spectris' Profit Improvement Programme targeting £30m? What drop-through have peers reported on recovering volume?
  • Decentralised model. Does the decentralised model prevent or enable group-wide procurement or overhead savings?
  • H2 2026 margins. What adjusted operating margins do brokers assume for H2 2026 (Shore Capital: over 20% in H2 2026, 23 September 2026), and what revenue level does that require?

Focus: How much adjusted operating profit Judges recovers for each £1m of revenue regained as order intake recovers, and how quickly. Whether the FY2025 central-cost step-up, UK tax changes and wage inflation permanently lower the margin Judges can reach at mid-cycle volumes, compared with the 25.6% adjusted operating margin of FY2023.

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • Halma (HLMA-GB): value-based pricing in regulated niches.
  • Renishaw (RSW-GB): tariff surcharges converted into permanent price rises.
  • Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB): repricing of its US order book; claimed 10% price premium.
  • Bruker (BRKR-US) and Waters (WAT-US): pricing to academic and commercial laboratories.
  • SDI Group (SDI-GB): small niche instrument businesses selling to academia.
  • Jenoptik (JEN-DE): price rises on scarce optical components.
  • Private competitors GUNT, TecQuipment and Edibon (education equipment against Armfield); Sutter Instrument and Siskiyou (electrophysiology against Scientifica).

Operating conditions:

  • US academic research-funding freezes and grant-obligation delays (2025–2026);
  • Chinese government procurement and R&D tax-exemption delays and domestic-substitution rules;
  • sterling strength against the US dollar and euro;
  • high UK wage and input-cost inflation (steel, copper, specialist alloys);
  • recovering commercial, semiconductor and battery R&D demand;
  • low-cost Chinese competition in price-sensitive niches.

Inputs:

  • List prices. What evidence exists of list-price increases at Judges' subsidiaries in 2022–2026, and of prices held or given back during the 2025–26 order downturn?
  • Management's pass-through claims. Test the claims that subsidiaries pass input-cost increases on in 90% to 95% of cases and that about 85% of businesses have pricing power (company statements and earnings calls).
  • Academic customers. How did academic customers respond to price increases during budget freezes? Look at survey evidence such as Jefferies' academic laboratory surveys (2025–2026) and procurement-platform evidence.
  • Share loss. What role did pricing play in Armfield's share loss (former employees cite a fall from about 40% to about 15% since 2016) and in Fire Testing Technology's margin compression?
  • Currency. In which currencies are subsidiaries' price lists set, and how quickly are US dollar and euro prices revised when sterling moves?
  • Contracts and channels. What pricing terms apply to OEM supply contracts (for example Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal, EWB Solutions) and to distributor agreements?
  • Service and consumables. How does pricing of service, spares and consumables compare with pricing of instruments?
  • Discounting. Is discounting on system replacements spreading across scientific instruments (expert commentary, 2026)?
  • Tariffs and inflation clauses. Is there tariff or inflation pass-through evidence at UK instrument exporters?

Focus: Whether Judges can raise prices faster than UK wage and input inflation once academic budgets ease, or only match it. How much of its US dollar and euro revenue can be repriced, and how quickly, if sterling strengthens further.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Judges' long-run growth has come from a cash-to-acquisitions compounding loop that has stalled while leverage, a progressive dividend and high operating leverage interact, so whether these loops turn reinforcing or balancing will shape revenue, margins, cash flow and EPS over the forecast period.

Peers:

  • Halma (HLMA-GB): decentralised acquisition engine still running at record pace.
  • SDI Group (SDI-GB): small UK instrument acquirer through the same downturn.
  • Lifco (LIFCO-B-SE), Indutrade (INDT-SE) and Addtech (ADDT-B-SE): Nordic serial acquirers of small niche businesses.
  • Diploma (DPLM-GB) and discoverIE (DSCV-GB): UK acquisitive industrials.
  • Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB) and Bruker (BRKR-US): academic-demand cycle comparators.
  • Private-equity buyers of UK scientific-instrument businesses: competing for the same targets.

Inputs:

  • The acquisition flywheel. High cash conversion funds acquisitions at 3–7x EBIT, which lifts EPS and the share price and reinforces sellers' trust. No acquisitions were completed in FY2025 or H1 2026, the longest gap since 2021 (Jefferies; interim results, 23 September 2026).
  • The leverage and dividend loop. Lower earnings raise gearing (2.1x adjusted EBITDA at June 2026 against 1.5x at December 2025) and squeeze dividend cover (1.1x at the 2026 interim, management calling it unsustainable), which may constrain acquisitions and growth.
  • Operating leverage on order recovery. Year-to-date order intake improved from 12% below the prior year at June to 1% below in September 2026. Recovery could lift margins and cash, cut leverage and reopen acquisition capacity.
  • Product investment and share. R&D was 7.0% of revenue in FY2025, below several peers. Lower product investment can lead to share loss (Armfield, Fire Testing Technology), then lower margins, then less investment.
  • The acquisition market. Rising competition for targets from private equity and from Chinese buyers seeking UK intellectual property (chief executive's comments, 2026) may raise multiples or shrink deal flow.
  • People. Subsidiary founders leave after earn-outs; staff turnover was 19% in 2025.
  • Academic demand. Academic funding flows feed the order book, then revenue, then customers' renewal of equipment budgets.

Focus: Whether the acquisition-compounding loop is likely to restart within 18 months, and what has historically restarted it for small serial acquirers after a downturn: deleveraging, seller supply, valuation. Whether leverage and the progressive-dividend commitment act as a balancing loop that delays the restart.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Judges' EPS over 2026–2029 depends on several discrete inputs that company guidance does not specify and that brokers model inconsistently: the Patent Box tax effect, Geotek coring timing, acquisition accretion, interest, currency transaction exposure and share count.

Peers:

  • Halma (HLMA-GB): FX translation and acquisition accretion disclosures.
  • Renishaw (RSW-GB): UK exporter with a rolling FX hedging programme and published sensitivities.
  • Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB): UK exporter reporting FX headwinds.
  • SDI Group (SDI-GB): small acquirer's deal accretion.
  • Vaisala (VAIAS-FI): exporter with a home-currency cost base and published FX sensitivities.

Inputs:

  • Tax rate. Is the UK Patent Box effect recurring or partly a one-off catch-up? The adjusted tax rate was 18% in H1 2026, against 21% in FY2024 and a 25% headline rate. Shore Capital credits Patent Box with about 30p of net H2 2026 EPS benefit (23 July 2026).
  • Geotek coring. When will the next major Geotek coring expedition happen? The company said no earlier than 2028 (interim results, 23 September 2026), after earlier expectations of 2026 and early 2027. What EPS contribution does an expedition make? Zeus published a sensitivity of about 50p per expedition (September 2025). What determines the customer's commitment?
  • Acquisition accretion. What accretion arises from acquisitions at the company's stated 3–7x EBIT range given current borrowing costs, and what entry multiples are UK niche instrument businesses now achieving?
  • Interest. What is the interest cost and margin on the multibank facility extended to July 2030 (£58.6m drawn of a £90m revolving facility at June 2026)?
  • Currency. What is the true currency transaction exposure, with about 50% of revenue in US dollars and 33% in euros against a largely sterling cost base (Annual Report 2025)? What is the hedge cover and maturity, and what would a sustained 5–10% sterling move do to operating profit after hedges roll off?
  • Dividend. Will the dividend policy (at least 10% annual growth subject to cover) be maintained through 2027?
  • Share count. What is the trend in share count dilution from options and the Share Incentive Plan?
  • Working capital. How fast can working capital be released (16% of sales in FY2025 against a 10% aspiration)?
  • Capitalised development. What is the level and trend of capitalised development costs (£1.8m in FY2025)?

Focus: How much of FY2026 adjusted EPS reflects non-recurring tax benefits. How sensitive FY2027–FY2028 EPS is to sterling, interest rates and the timing of Geotek coring and acquisitions.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 14,986 words · written 24 October 2026, 03:14 to 03:41 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Reading notes for the Consumer (definitions and anchors that every answer below uses)

  1. Basis. Every profit figure below is on the company's adjusted basis (before amortisation of acquired intangibles, impairments, acquisition costs and fair-value movements on currency derivatives), which is the same basis as the brokers' EBIT and EPS. "EBITDA" is adjusted operating profit plus depreciation (including depreciation of right-of-use assets), about £4.7m in FY25, which reconciles to the brokers' FY25 EBITDA of £32.7m. FactSet's history rows for "EBIT (adjusted)", "gross profit" and the 32.6% FY25 tax rate are amortisation-burdened standardised lines and are not used.
  2. The anchors. FY25: revenue £145.8m, adjusted operating profit £28.0m (19.2%), adjusted PBT about £23.8m, adjusted EPS 275.3p on about 6.67m shares, DPS 115p declared for the year. H1 26: revenue £55.7m, adjusted operating profit £4.8m (8.6%), adjusted PBT £3.2m, adjusted EPS 39.0p, adjusted net debt £45.3m (2.1x), order book 17.3 weeks. H2 25: revenue £75.6m, adjusted operating profit about £13.7m (18.1%), EPS about 134p; H2 25 contained no coring revenue, so it is the clean comparison for H2 26.
  3. Cost structure used throughout. Raw materials and consumables £44.8m (31% of revenue) are variable. A further 3% to 5% of revenue (freight, agents' commissions, overtime, bonuses, travel) is semi-variable. Staff (£48.5m), other external charges (£19.0m) and depreciation (£4.7m) are the fixed block. On a 65% contribution margin, the fixed block was about £67m in FY25 and ran at about £63m annualised in H1 26 after the cost actions and the disappearance of volume-linked pay and travel. The difference between those two figures matters: about £2m to £3m of "fixed" cost comes back automatically with volume.
  4. Consensus. FactSet median of three brokers (two for FY28): FY26 revenue £121m, EBITDA £24.7m, EBIT £20.3m, EPS 199p, DPS 126p; FY27 £128m, £26.5m, £22.0m, 219p (mean 224p), 139p; FY28 £138m, £28.4m, £23.8m, 248p, 146p. The broker range for FY27 EPS is 212p (Shore) to 243p (Investec).
  5. Definitions used in my cash figures. "Free cash flow" means cash from operations after tax and interest, less capital spending including capitalised development, less lease payments, before dividends and acquisitions. "Net debt" means the company's adjusted net debt, which excludes lease liabilities (about £3m); FactSet's figure includes them. "Working capital" in the forecast form follows FactSet's definition (receivables plus inventories less payables, as a share of the last twelve months' revenue: 12.6% at December 2025, 23.3% at June 2026), not the company's 16%-of-sales measure, which includes other balances.
  6. Where I differ from the first set. The differences are recorded inside the answers; in summary: (a) I include modest acquisitions from H2 2027 and one Geotek coring expedition (placed in FY29) in the base case rather than as pure upside scenarios, because a five-year model of a buy-and-build group with a three-in-four-years coring pattern that excludes both is not a base case but a floor; the acquisition and coring contributions are separately identified so that they can be stripped out; (b) the realised incremental margin on recovery will be about 40% to 45%, not the 60% to 65% gross contribution, because semi-variable costs return and wage inflation runs ahead of academic pricing; (c) deleveraging is slower than the first set's 1.2x to 1.4x by end-FY27, because the dividend absorbs about 70% of free cash flow at FY27 profit levels; (d) free cash flow conversion is about 75% to 80% of adjusted net income on a full definition, not 100% to 110%.

Financial forecasts

C559 questions
P-0162P-0245P-0348P-0445P-0550P-0648P-0855P-0955P-1056

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts for Judges Scientific over the next 18 months to four years are shaped by one dominant mechanic and one dominant constraint, and the Consumer should hold both in mind at once.

The mechanic is fixed-cost operating leverage from a trough. About £64m to £68m of the cost base does not move with volume, so the £21m of revenue lost between FY25 and FY26 removed about £9m of adjusted operating profit, and the same mechanic returns 40p to 45p of profit for every £1 of organic revenue regained. Consensus has priced almost none of this: its FY27 to FY28 incremental margins of 18% to 24% describe a company that reinvests all of its recovery. My base case has revenue above consensus in every year (because order intake has already recovered to −1% year to date while revenue is down 14%), FY26 profit below consensus (because the H2 margin that consensus's line items require is not available from this cost structure, and the FY26 EPS carries 8p to 15p of non-recurring tax benefit), and FY27 to FY28 profit above consensus (EPS 234p and 283p against 219p and 248p). The margin recovers to 18% to 20% without coring by FY28 to FY30 and will not return to the 26% of FY22 to FY23 inside the window, because about £6m of central cost, UK tax and R&D has been added to the base since then. Price contributes 2.5% a year to revenue and nothing to margin. Cost elimination contributes about £2.8m cumulatively and is more than offset by inflation; the real internal lever is repairing three loss-making businesses, worth 25p to 35p of EPS by FY28 to FY29, of which I credit half.

The constraint is the balance sheet and the dividend acting together as a balancing loop on the acquisition flywheel. At FY26 to FY28 profit levels the dividend (growing 10% a year, cash £7.9m to £9.7m) absorbs about 70% to 78% of free cash flow, so deleveraging is slow (1.7x at end-FY26, 1.5x at end-FY27), and acquisitions can only be debt-funded as EBITDA grows. I therefore include acquisitions at about half the historical pace, £47m over FY27 to FY30 from H2 2027, worth about 73p of EPS by FY30 net of funding cost, and one Geotek coring expedition in FY29, worth about 55p; both are identified separately so that the Consumer can read the organic-only path (EPS 193p, 229p, 259p, 292p, 320p) alongside the full base case (193p, 234p, 283p, 394p, 394p).

Below the operating line the items are modestly favourable and predictable: tax at 20% to 21% on Patent Box relief, interest of £2.7m to £3.3m fixed by swaps to 2028, no buybacks, dilution of about 0.7% a year. The adjusted-to-statutory gap stays wide and a further impairment at Fire Testing Technology or Scientifica is plausible without affecting adjusted EPS or cash.

The forecasts deserve moderate weight for FY26 (decided by Q4 shipments and tested on about 22 January 2027), low-to-moderate weight for FY27 (unguided until March 2027; a mechanical rebound against a −21% comparison, with US appropriations and sterling as the external swing factors), and low weight as point estimates beyond that. The lean is slightly below consensus for FY26 and modestly above for FY27 to FY28, resting on the arithmetic of a recovered order book rather than on management's record, which is three downgrades in three years followed by prompt delivery of each rebased figure. The drivers of the forecast change are concentrated (order conversion and the acquisition restart carry over 80% of the FY26 to FY28 profit increase), and the cone is wide: about ±25% on FY27 EPS, two to three times a Halma's or a Diploma's. The largest unmodelled sensitivity is currency: a sustained 10% rise in sterling against the dollar and euro would remove about £7m of operating profit, roughly 80p of EPS, with a two-to-four-quarter lag, and no broker has quantified it.

For the investment case the net effect of the forecasts is positive relative to what consensus and a 14.5x multiple describe, but it is the positive of a cyclical recovery in a company whose structural margin ceiling has come down and whose compounding engine will restart at half speed, not the positive of a return to the 2005 to 2023 model. The dated tests are January, March and July 2027; until the first of them, the forecasts carry the weight of a mechanism rather than a demonstrated delivery.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C62

Judgement. Operating leverage is the single mechanic that will decide whether the forecasts are met, and it now works in the company's favour from a trough. It took adjusted operating profit from £34.8m (FY23) to about £18.8m (my FY26) on a revenue fall of only £11m, because about £67m of the cost base is fixed and the lost revenue was the highest-margin kind (Geotek coring, China, US academic vacuum). On the way back up it will return 40p to 45p of adjusted operating profit for every £1 of organic revenue regained in FY27 and about 40p thereafter, which is roughly twice the 18% to 24% incremental margin embedded in consensus for FY27 to FY28 but well below the 60% to 65% gross drop-through that the first set and the Industry Digest quote. Both figures are right for different purposes: 60% to 65% is the contribution margin on a marginal shipment; 40% to 45% is what reaches the operating line once volume-linked pay, commissions and travel return and once wage inflation of about £2.5m a year is netted against price of about 2.5%. Over four years the leverage rebuilds the margin to 18% to 20% without coring and about 21% in a coring year, which is 5 to 7 percentage points below the FY22 to FY23 peak; the gap is structural (central costs, UK taxes and National Insurance, higher R&D) and will not close through volume alone.

What the thing is, and its size. On the FY25 cost base of about £118m, variable cost is £45m to £52m (materials plus the semi-variable items), so fixed cost is £66m to £73m, 45% to 50% of revenue. Short-run contribution margin is 63% to 69%; I use 65%. A 10% change in revenue (£12.5m on the FY26 base) moves adjusted operating profit by about £8m before any cost response, which is 43% of FY26 profit. This is the widest earnings cone of any company in the dashboard's sector peer set, including Renishaw, whose payroll is 46% of revenue but whose group margin is now 18.7% on a base twice the size of Judges' relative to its fixed costs.

What the last four to six years show. Four observations, in order of importance for the model:

  1. FY23 to H1 26 is a pure operating-leverage episode, not a cost-inflation episode. The adjusted margin went 25.6% (FY23), 20.9% (FY24), 19.2% (FY25), 8.6% (H1 26). Revenue fell £11m from FY23 to FY26 while adjusted operating profit fell about £16m. The cost base did not run away: raw materials stayed at 31% of revenue in both FY24 and FY25; staff costs rose from £43.3m to £48.5m (£1m of that was employer National Insurance, about £1.5m central hires, the rest wage inflation and acquired businesses). The decline is lost high-margin volume landing on a fixed base.
  2. The decremental margin was about 65% in H1 26 against H1 25 (revenue −£14.5m, profit −£9.5m), inflated by the absence of coring, which carries a margin of 65% to 75%. Excluding coring, the rest of the portfolio lost about £5m of profit on about £7m of lost revenue, a decremental of roughly 70%, even after £0.8m of net cost savings. That is the clearest evidence that the lost volume was high-margin mix (China vacuum, US academic instruments), and it is also why the recovery of exactly that volume carries a high incremental margin.
  3. Fixed costs did flex. The annualised fixed block fell from about £67m in FY25 to about £63m in H1 26: £1.8m of deliberate cuts and £2m to £3m of volume-linked pay, commissions and travel that disappeared. The second part returns with volume and must be modelled as a cost of recovery, which is the main reason I cap the realised incremental margin at 40% to 45%.
  4. The historical recoveries were fast once volume returned: H2 2015 lifted the full-year margin to 17.3% from a sub-8-week order book trough; 2021 to H1 2023 took the margin from about 19% to 23.1% on 32% H1 revenue growth, with inflation running at 7% to 9% in engineering wages. In neither case did price lead; volume did.

How it shapes each line item, FY26 to FY30 (base case detail in P-10).

  • Revenue. Operating leverage does not set revenue, but the Q4 2026 weighting does set where the leverage lands. My H2 26 revenue of £69.5m (−8% against H2 25) yields adjusted operating profit of about £14.0m (20.1%), which is in line with Shore's "over 20%" for H2; every £1m of Q4 revenue that slips into January 2027 moves about £0.65m of profit and about 8p of EPS across the year-end. Consensus's FY26 line items (£121m revenue with £20.3m EBIT) require an H2 margin of about 24% on £65m, which the cost structure does not support; the internally consistent reading of the brokers is revenue slightly above £121m and EBIT slightly below £20m.
  • Adjusted operating profit and margin. FY26 about £18.8m (15.0%); FY27 £22.6m (16.7%); FY28 £27.1m (18.0%, of which £2.0m from acquisitions); FY29 £37.2m (21.0%, including about £4.8m from coring); FY30 £37.7m (19.8%, no coring). The organic-only margin path is 15.0%, 16.7%, 17.9%, 18.9%, 19.8%. On that path the business is back at the FY25 profit level in FY28 and the FY23 profit level in FY29, but never back at the FY23 margin inside the window.
  • EBITDA. £23.5m, £27.5m, £32.6m, £43.4m, £44.5m (margins 18.8%, 20.3%, 21.6%, 24.5%, 23.4%). Against consensus: FY26 £1.2m below, FY27 £1.0m above, FY28 £4.2m above (£2.5m of the FY28 gap is acquisitions).
  • EPS. The leverage doubles up with the lower share of profit taken by interest as profit recovers: adjusted operating profit +20% in FY27 becomes PBT +27% and EPS +21% after the tax rate steps up from 17% to 20%. Across FY26 to FY28 organic EPS compounds at about 16% a year on organic revenue growth of about 6%.
  • Cash. Because the incremental revenue carries 65% contribution and working capital is already elevated, cash conversion in FY27 to FY28 should run at 110% to 125% of adjusted operating profit on the company's measure, as it did in FY24 to FY25.

Why 40% to 45% and not 60% or 20%. The build for FY27 from an FY26 profit of £18.8m: organic revenue +£8.1m at 65% contribution = +£5.3m; return of volume-linked pay, commissions and travel −£1.0m; wage and other inflation −£2.8m; price +2.5% on £133m = +£3.3m; full-year effect of cost actions and further restructuring at the underperformers +£1.3m; reinvestment in talent and product −£0.5m; acquisitions +£0.4m. Net +£3.8m, so £22.6m, an incremental margin of 42% on organic revenue (47% including the acquired profit). The same arithmetic for FY28 gives about 40%. Consensus's 18% to 24% incrementals effectively assume that all of the leverage is reinvested or inflated away; that would have been true in FY24 to FY25 when the centre was being built, and it is not the plan management describes now (cost base cut, "positive operating leverage when demand recovers").

Where this is less certain, and the skew. The leverage is symmetric in mechanics and skewed upward from here only because the starting point is a trough. Three things could hold the realised incremental below 40%: a mix of recovery weighted to Materials Sciences (Armfield, geotechnical testing) rather than Vacuum, which carries lower contribution; sterling above about 1.40 to the dollar, which lowers the sterling value of the recovered volume by about 7% to 10% with a one-year lag (see P-04 and P-08); and management choosing to rebuild bonuses and R&D ahead of margin. Two things could push it above 50%: China catch-up shipments (high-margin vacuum and cryogenics) dominating H1 27, and the underperformers moving from losses to break-even faster than I assume. The 12-month cone for FY27 adjusted operating profit is about £17m to £27m on organic revenue of −3% to +12%, which is EPS of roughly 170p to 285p.

Peers, and why Judges differs. Renishaw lifted its margin from 15.7% to 18.7% in one year on 3% revenue growth because it cut £23m of cost; its leverage is now reinforced by volume. Oxford Instruments expects more than 100 basis points of margin progress on 8% revenue growth, helped by a 20% headcount cut at Andor. Halma has run a stable 21% to 23% margin through the cycle because its sales per employee are far higher and its end markets are regulated. Judges' leverage is higher than all three because its fixed block is a larger share of revenue and because it has done less structural cutting; that makes its recovery steeper per unit of volume and its forecasts less predictable.

Against the last four to six years. FY21 to FY23 was leverage plus acquisitions plus coring working together (margin 21.9% to 25.6%; EPS 192p to 375p). FY24 to FY26 is leverage working against the company with no acquisitions and no coring. FY27 to FY30 in my base case is leverage working for the company again, with acquisitions and coring arriving later and smaller than in 2021 to 2023. The profit recovery is therefore slower in absolute terms than the 2021 to 2023 one, but faster in EPS growth terms than consensus implies, because consensus has priced almost none of the leverage.

P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D45

Judgement. Financial leverage matters for this company through the balance sheet, not through the income statement. Its direct effect on EPS is modest and predictable: net financial items of about £3.2m in FY26 fall to about £2.7m in FY28 and rise again to about £3.3m by FY30 as acquisitions resume, so interest takes 17% of adjusted operating profit in FY26, 10% in FY28 and 9% in FY30, against 15% in FY25 and 4% in FY21. Its indirect effect is large: at 2.1x adjusted EBITDA in June 2026 with a dividend that absorbs about three-quarters of free cash flow, the balance sheet cannot both pay the progressive dividend and restart acquisitions until EBITDA has recovered to about £30m. That sequencing, dividend first, deleveraging second, acquisitions third, is what pushes the acquisition engine out to H2 2027 at the earliest and keeps net debt in a £40m to £60m band for the whole forecast window. There is no solvency or covenant question in any plausible case.

The position and its mechanics.

  • Facility: £90m committed revolving credit to July 2030 (£58.6m drawn at June 2026) plus an uncommitted £50m accordion; covenants of 3.0x net debt to EBITDA and 3.0x interest cover, unchanged.
  • Pricing: SONIA plus a margin of 1.85% to 3.5% that scales with leverage. The SONIA component is swapped at 3.2% (2025) rising to 3.7% (2027 to 2028), with up to £10m deliberately left unhedged. The all-in cost is therefore about 5.5% to 7%, and a 100 basis point move in market rates changes the interest bill by only about £0.1m through the unhedged portion. The leverage grid is the more important sensitivity: moving from above 2.0x to below 1.5x saves roughly 50 to 75 basis points on about £55m, about £0.3m to £0.4m a year, about 4p of EPS.
  • Cash interest is partly offset by interest on £15m to £19m of cash held in subsidiaries, and a share of borrowings is held in dollars and euros to offset translation.

EPS amplification. On my base case, a 10% change in adjusted operating profit moves adjusted PBT by 12% in FY26 and 11% in FY28. That is a gearing factor of 1.1x to 1.2x, small against the 4x to 5x gearing from operating leverage. Interest is the most predictable line in the model: £3.2m (FY26), £2.8m (FY27), £2.7m (FY28), £3.0m (FY29), £3.3m (FY30), with the rise from FY29 driven by my assumed acquisition spending, not by rates.

The balance sheet path (adjusted net debt, excluding leases).

The year-end FY26 figure benefits from the seasonal unwind of the £12m working-capital build seen between December and June (FactSet working capital £18.4m at December 2025 against £30.6m at June 2026); that unwind is why June leverage of 2.1x overstates the year-end position. If Q4 shipments slip, receivables stay high and year-end net debt would be nearer £44m to £46m (1.9x to 2.0x), the first set's downside.

The three implications that matter for the forecasts.

  1. The dividend sets the pace of everything else. At 10% growth the dividend costs £7.9m in FY26, £8.8m in FY27 and £9.7m in FY28, against my free cash flow of £10.1m, £12.4m and £14.3m: 78%, 70% and 68% of free cash flow. Cover on EPS recovers from 1.1x at the interim to about 1.5x (FY26), 1.7x (FY27) and 1.9x (FY28), which is still below the 2.4x to 3.9x of FY21 to FY24. The surplus after dividends (£2m to £5m a year in FY26 to FY28) cannot fund acquisitions of any size; they must be debt-funded, which is possible only as EBITDA grows. This is the balancing loop the first set and the Feedback Loops report describe, and I quantify its cost: a peer with no dividend (SDI Group) would have about £9m a year more to deploy, roughly one deal a year.
  2. Deleveraging is slower than the first set assumed. The first set expected 1.2x to 1.4x by end-FY27 "absent acquisitions". My figures give 1.4x absent acquisitions and 1.5x with a £5m deal, because free cash flow at FY27 profit levels is £12m, not the £18m to £21m seen in FY24 to FY25 when EBITDA was £32m to £33m. Leverage then falls to about 1.3x only in FY29, and only because EBITDA grows; absolute net debt rises from FY27 onwards in my base case because of acquisitions.
  3. Acquisition capacity is real but gated. Holding leverage at 2.0x on FY27 EBITDA of £27.5m gives debt capacity of £55m, about £13m above FY27 net debt. On FY28 EBITDA of £32.6m it gives £65m, about £15m of headroom. That supports my base case of £5m in H2 2027, £12m in FY28 and £15m a year in FY29 to FY30, about £47m in total, which is less than half of what the company spent in 2022 to 2024 (£80m). Larger spending would need either the dividend to grow more slowly or EBITDA to recover faster.

Risk cases. A covenant breach needs EBITDA below about £15m with net debt at £45m: that requires FY26 revenue below about £110m, 12% below consensus, which I put at under 5% probability. The realistic stress is softer: if FY26 EBITDA is £21m (Q4 slip) and FY27 EBITDA is flat at about £22m (a second US funding leg), year-end FY27 leverage would be about 2.0x, the interest margin would step up, and the board would face holding the dividend flat for the first time in 20 years or borrowing to pay it. I put that joint outcome at 15% to 20%. In that case FY27 EPS is about 185p to 195p and the dividend would most likely be held, not cut.

Against the last four to six years. Net debt went from about £1m (2020) to £55m (2022 to 2024) because of Geotek and the 2023 to 2024 acquisitions, with leverage of 1.2x to 1.8x; interest rose from £0.7m to £4.2m as rates rose and debt grew. FY26 is the first year in which leverage has risen because earnings fell rather than because capital was deployed. Over FY27 to FY30 the pattern reverts to the historical one: leverage held at 1.3x to 1.5x, debt rising with acquisitions, interest a stable 9% to 12% of operating profit. The balance sheet is therefore not a source of EPS growth or EPS risk in the forecast; it is a constraint on how fast the reinforcing loops in P-05 can restart.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

❌ D48

Judgement. Very little, and it arrives early. Deliberate cost elimination will contribute about £1.0m net to FY26 adjusted operating profit, a further £1.3m in FY27 and perhaps £0.5m in FY28, about £2.8m cumulative or 2% of the FY25 cost base. Against that, wage and other inflation adds £2.5m to £3m to the cost base every year, so cost actions slow the erosion of margin rather than raise it. The real efficiency lever is not cutting but repairing the three loss-making or sub-par businesses (Fire Testing Technology, Armfield, Scientifica), which together drag group profit by about £2m to £3m a year today; bringing them to modest profitability is worth about 25p to 35p of EPS by FY28 to FY29 and is included in my base case at about half that pace. There is no scope for a Renishaw-style programme (3% of revenue, 7% of headcount) without abandoning the decentralised model, and management has said it will not do that.

What has been done, and what reaches the P&L when.

  • H1 26: £1.8m to £2.0m of annualised cost taken out at underperforming businesses, mainly headcount, against £1.0m reinvested in talent and product elsewhere. Net saving about £0.8m to £1.0m annualised. About half of the gross saving was in the H1 26 result; the full annualised benefit is in H2 26 and FY27.
  • Scientifica exited multiphoton microscopy in early 2025 and completed restructuring; Armfield has had product rationalisation and headcount reductions and is finishing a multi-year insourcing of manufacturing after its subcontractors (90% of fabrication) exited.
  • Fire Testing Technology is the clearest remaining case: an operating loss of £1.21m on revenue of £5.12m in FY25 (Companies House, 11 September 2026), staff costs of £2.4m or 47% of revenue, and R&D cut from £0.65m to £0.55m while losses widened. Returning it to break-even needs either revenue of about £7m (the new cone calorimeter, a year late) or about £1m of further staff cost. I assume about £0.7m of improvement in FY27 and a further £0.5m in FY28.

What is cuttable, by line, over FY27 to FY28.

Cost line (FY25) Cuttable Reaching P&L Why
Raw materials £44.8m £0.2m to £0.4m FY27 to FY28 Call-off purchasing and value engineering; no group procurement by design
Staff £48.5m £1.5m to £2.5m gross at the three weak units FY27 (most), FY28 Headcount at Fire Testing Technology, Armfield, Scientifica; deeper cuts would damage capability; turnover already 19%
Other external charges £19.0m £0.5m to £1.0m FY27 Travel, consultants, discretionary marketing
Central costs about £5m £0.3m to £0.5m Not assumed The acquisitions, portfolio and commercial roles are the strategy; a reversal is unlikely
R&D £10.2m (7%) Nil Already below peers (8% to 13%); cutting it caused the share losses being repaired
UK tax and National Insurance about £3m Nil Not controllable; partly offset by Patent Box (P-08)

Gross cuttable about £3m to £4.5m over two years, of which about £2m has already been actioned. Net of reinvestment (management reinvested half of the H1 26 gross saving), the incremental net contribution beyond H1 26 is about £1.3m in FY27 and £0.5m in FY28.

The offsets, which are larger. UK engineering wage inflation is running at 4% to 5% (peers reported 7.5% to 9% in 2023), about £2.2m a year on the staff base; other cost inflation about £0.6m; employer National Insurance already in the base. Volume-linked pay, commissions and travel that fell with H1 26 volume return with it, about £1m in FY27. So the fixed cost block rises from about £63m (H1 26 run-rate) to about £66m in FY27 and £68m in FY28 despite the cuts. Price of about 2.5% (P-04) covers the inflation but not the reinstated semi-variable costs. The net effect of "cost" in the FY26 to FY28 bridge is therefore roughly zero; the bridge is volume.

Peer precedents, and why they do not transfer. Renishaw's £23m gross programme (7% headcount, including a loss-making business closure) cost about £16m one-off and took 12 to 18 months; it added about 2.5 points of margin and was possible because Renishaw is one integrated company. Spectris delivered £30m of a £50m target through ERP and integrating acquisitions, with £18.3m of restructuring cost. Oxford Instruments cut 20% of headcount at one site for £5m. SDI Group, the closest model, lifted its laboratory-equipment segment margin from 7.2% to 11.7% through selective local cost control, which is the Judges pattern. A Judges equivalent of Renishaw's programme would be about £4m, which is roughly the gross cuttable figure above, but it would be executed business by business over two to three years, not announced as a programme, and the decentralised model means there are no shared-service, ERP or procurement savings to find. The former Halma executives now running the centre know this model; their intervention is capability-led (talent, product, commercial), not cost-led.

The underperformer repair is the real lever. Fire Testing Technology (loss about £1.2m), Armfield (loss-making; goodwill fully impaired; investment written down £7.0m) and Scientifica (impaired £1.7m; margin below group) together probably contribute negative £1.5m to £2.5m of operating profit today on about £15m to £20m of revenue. Bringing all three to a 10% margin would add about £3m to £4.5m, 35p to 50p of EPS. I assume about £1.2m of improvement in FY27 and £0.8m in FY28 (included in the 42% and 40% incrementals in P-01), leaving the rest as upside. The precedent for success is Judges' own: it has fixed businesses before (GDS grew EBIT 3.5x after purchase), but it has also let Armfield drift for a decade. The Wisdom Library's turnaround base rate (about 30%, two to three years) argues for crediting half, which is what I do.

The mid-cycle margin ceiling. This is the most important conclusion for the out-years: the structural additions since FY23 (about £2m of central cost, about £3m of UK tax and National Insurance, about £1m of extra R&D) lower the margin the company can reach at any given volume by about 4 percentage points. On FY23's revenue mix the FY23 margin of 25.6% would today be about 21% to 22%. Jefferies' view that margins will not return to 25.6% until FY27 is wrong in timing; they will not return to it inside the window at all without coring. My base case has 19.8% in FY30 without coring and about 21% in a coring year, which I regard as the mid-cycle ceiling for this cost structure.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D45

Judgement. Price will contribute about 2.5% a year to revenue, about £3m a year, and nothing to margin. It is a cost-recovery mechanism, not a profit lever: it covers wage and other inflation of £2.5m to £3m a year almost exactly, with a lag of two to four quarters, so that in any year in which volume is flat the margin drifts down by about half a point and in any year in which volume rises the whole of the margin gain comes from volume. Real pricing (above cost inflation) exists only in the OEM-embedded vacuum and cryogenic businesses and in commercial and industrial channels, perhaps a third of revenue, and is worth at most 0.5 to 1 point of margin over the window; it is offset by negative real pricing in education equipment, fire testing and China. The one condition under which price would move the forecast materially is currency: sterling at 1.40 or above would require list-price rises in dollars and euros that academic customers would not accept quickly, and the resulting margin loss (6% to 8% of operating profit per 10% move, after hedges roll off) is the largest single sensitivity in the model that the sell-side has not quantified.

What the evidence establishes.

  • Nominal pricing is applied and largely sticks: management states that about 85% of businesses can pass cost increases on and that subsidiaries do so in 90% to 95% of cases; list prices were raised across 2022 to 2026; no source reports discounting at Judges in the 2025 to 2026 downturn, and the margin damage was volume, not price. Competitors in the core niches are specification-led and margin-first (first set, C-28 and C-29).
  • Nominal pricing lags cost: the FY25 margin fell 170 basis points with "price realisation lagging input inflation"; Fire Testing Technology's long assembly times create a multi-month gap between cost rises and delivery; industrial peers report a three-month lag on materials pass-through; distributor agreements carry notice periods. The lag is the reason wage inflation of 4% to 5% in a year with 2.5% price costs about half a point of margin in that year.
  • Real pricing is not evidenced anywhere in the numbers. The company has never quantified a price contribution. The peers that have (Renishaw's tariff surcharges worth about 1.2% of growth and later made permanent; Oxford Instruments repricing its US order book; Halma's 1% to 2% organic price; Agilent's 200 basis points; Waters' 150 basis points) sell to commercial, regulated or industrial customers. Judges' two-thirds academic mix is the opposite case: 75% of US laboratory managers describe conditions as weak, only 23% of laboratory spend is on fixed-price contracts, and procurement consortia and platform purchasing are spreading. Academic buyers do not negotiate price down; they defer, which costs volume rather than price but has the same effect on the ability to raise list prices.
  • Where pricing was inflexible it cost share: Armfield's outsourced cost base and premium pricing lost 25 points of share to GUNT, TecQuipment and Edibon; Fire Testing Technology's margin halved as cheaper rivals and local-for-local Chinese manufacturing arrived. Chinese rivals price 20% to 30% below Western brands in the categories where they compete.

Contribution to revenue, FY26 to FY30. I model organic growth of about −11% excluding coring (FY26), +6.5% (FY27), +5.5% (FY28), +5.0% (FY29), +4.5% (FY30). Price is about 2.5 points of each year's figure. Volume is therefore only about 4 points in FY27 (the mechanical rebound against H1 26, plus China catch-up), 3 points in FY28 and 2 to 2.5 points thereafter, which is below the market's 4% to 5% growth and reflects the 1-point-a-year share-loss drag at the weak businesses and in China. This matters for how the Consumer reads reported growth: a reported organic figure of 5% in FY28 would be only 2.5% volume, and a reported 2.5% would be zero volume.

Contribution to margin. Zero in the base case, by construction: price £3.3m a year against wage inflation £2.2m, other inflation £0.6m and the lag cost of about £0.3m to £0.5m. The upside case, in which commercial and vacuum businesses take 1 point of real price from FY28 as academic budgets ease, adds about £0.5m to £0.7m a year of operating profit, 6p to 8p of EPS a year, cumulative to perhaps 20p by FY30. I do not include it. The downside case, in which academic resistance keeps price at 1.5% while wage inflation stays at 4.5%, costs about £1.2m a year, which over three years is about 2.5 points of margin and roughly 40p of FY29 EPS. I regard those two tails as roughly balanced.

Service and spares. Services recognised over time were £19.3m in FY25 (13% of revenue); peers show aftermarket gross margins 15 to 20 points above equipment. Service pricing is the one line where Judges can raise price above inflation without volume risk, and the company has not disclosed doing so. It is a modest, slow lever: raising service prices 2 points above inflation on £19m is £0.4m a year.

Currency, the pricing question that matters most. About 50% of revenue is in US dollars and 33% in euros against a cost base that is 80% to 90% sterling. Price lists for exports are set in dollars and euros and revised, in practice, annually, so a sterling move is absorbed in margin for two to four quarters and then partly repriced. The company's economic hedges (options and forwards covering the ensuing 12 months; £6.0m of dollar and £2.4m of euro cover outstanding at December 2025) protect only the near-term committed order book. My estimate of the exposure, consistent with the first set's: a sustained 10% appreciation of sterling against both currencies costs about £7m of operating profit before repricing (about 8% of revenue translated at a 65% to 70% drop-through, after allowing for Geotek's dollar costs and dollar borrowings), and repricing recovers perhaps a third of that within 12 to 18 months in commercial channels and little in academic channels. Oxford Instruments, with 49% dollar revenue and 12-to-18-month hedging, disclosed a £4.5m currency headwind in FY26 and a sensitivity of about £0.6m per cent on GBP/USD; scaled to Judges' size that is about £0.25m per cent, close to my £7m per 10%. The current rate (about 1.34) is inside the 1.30 to 1.38 band my base case assumes; the FY27 forecast is exposed to the second half of 2027 and FY28 is fully exposed.

Interplay. Pricing power and operating leverage interact badly in a downturn and well in an upturn: because price cannot be raised when volume is falling, the fixed cost base is uncovered at exactly the moment inflation is still running, which is what produced the 8.6% H1 26 margin; in the upturn, volume covers the fixed base and price covers inflation, so the full contribution margin on volume reaches profit, which is why the incremental margin in P-01 can be 40% to 45% without any real pricing.

Against the last four to six years. FY21 to FY23 saw price rises of probably 4% to 6% a year against 7% to 9% wage inflation, with volume and acquisitions masking the lag. FY24 to FY26 saw price of 2% to 3% against 4% to 5% wage inflation and falling volume, so the lag showed. FY27 to FY30 should see price and inflation roughly matched at 2.5% to 3%, which is the benign case for this business model: no margin from price, no margin lost to price. The score reflects a neutral-to-slightly-negative effect on the forecasts, with the currency tail the reason it is below 50.

P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C50

Judgement. Judges has one powerful reinforcing loop (the acquisition flywheel: cash conversion funds 3x to 7x EBIT purchases, which raise EPS and cash, which fund the next purchase) and one powerful balancing loop (earnings down, leverage up, dividend cover down, acquisitions stopped) that is currently holding the flywheel still. Over the next 18 months the balancing loop dominates: the dividend takes about three-quarters of free cash flow, leverage stays near 1.5x to 1.7x, and the first acquisition under the new team is unlikely before H2 2027 and will be small. From FY28 the operating-leverage loop (orders up, margin up, cash up, leverage down) releases the brake and the flywheel restarts at perhaps half its historical speed. Two smaller negative loops (under-investment at the weak businesses, people attrition) are being actively countered by the new centre and should net to a small drag. The result for the line items is a recovery that is organic and margin-led in FY27 to FY28, and acquisition-assisted only from FY28 to FY30; EPS grows about 21% in FY27 and 21% in FY28 from the trough with almost no help from the flywheel, and the flywheel adds roughly 22p, 47p and 73p to EPS in FY28, FY29 and FY30, net of the interest on the debt that funds it.

The loops, their state and their effect on the forecasts.

Loop Type State today Effect in my base case
1. Operating leverage: volume → margin → cash → leverage → capacity Reinforcing, both directions Turning positive: orders −18% (Q1), flat (Q2), about +15% (Q3); book 17.3 weeks +£3.8m EBIT FY27, +£2.9m FY28 organic; cash conversion 110% to 125%
2. Leverage and dividend: earnings down → gearing up and cover down → acquisitions paused Balancing (brake on loop 3) Fully engaged: 2.1x, cover 1.1x at the interim, dividend raised 10% regardless Dividends £7.9m to £9.7m FY26 to FY28 against FCF £10.1m to £14.3m; no deal above £5m before FY28
3. Acquisition flywheel Reinforcing Stalled since 2024 (longest gap since 2021); team in place; pipeline "healthy"; sellers waiting Restart H2 27: £5m, £12m, £15m, £15m (FY27 to FY30) at about 5.5x EBIT; EPS +5p, +22p, +47p, +73p net of funding cost
4. Under-investment at weak units: share loss → margin loss → R&D cut → share loss Reinforcing, negative Running at Fire Testing Technology (R&D cut 15% while losses grew) and Armfield; countered by central intervention Drag of about £2m a year today, narrowing to about £1m by FY28 (half credited)
5. People: founders leave after earn-outs; turnover 19%; more than 30% of subsidiaries changed leaders in 2024 to 2025 Reinforcing, negative, slow Mitigated by leadership programme, share ownership (312 participants), Portfolio Chief Executive No explicit figure; a reason to cap the incremental margin at 40% to 45% rather than 60%
6. Academic demand: funding → orders → revenue → customers' budgets External, cyclical At its floor; US stopgap year; China catch-up is refill not growth Organic volume +4 points FY27, +3 FY28, +2 to 2.5 later
7. Acquisition market: private equity and Chinese buyers → higher multiples → smaller spread Balancing (on loop 3) Rising; Halma pays 12x, Nordic acquirers 6.5x to 7.5x; Judges still 3x to 7x Entry multiple 5.5x rather than the 5.0x to 5.3x historical; accretion about 15p per £10m rather than 17p
8. Valuation: de-rating → no equity currency; low multiple makes buybacks competitive with deals Balancing, latent P/E 14.5x, 0th percentile of five years; buybacks rejected by the board Nil in base case; a buyback of £5m would add about 2% to EPS and is more likely under a new CFO than under the current board

Loop 1 and loop 2 together are the whole 18-month story. The sequence runs: H2 26 shipments convert the order book into revenue at a 20% margin; FY26 year-end working capital unwinds; net debt falls to about £41m (1.7x); FY27 organic revenue rises about 6.5% with 42% drop-through; free cash flow of about £12m pays an £8.8m dividend and £3m to £4m of debt; leverage reaches about 1.5x by December 2027. At that point the brake eases, not releases: capacity at 2.0x is about £13m. The brake would release faster only if the dividend grew more slowly (a cultural break management has explicitly refused) or if EBITDA recovered faster than the £27.5m I assume. The brake would re-engage if FY26 Q4 slipped and FY27 orders stalled: leverage back to about 2.0x, dividend held flat, and the flywheel pushed to FY29.

Loop 3: what restarts the flywheel, and what the precedents say. The Feedback Loops report's peer evidence is consistent: AddLife slowed acquisitions in 2023 to 2024 to deleverage, then completed three in 2025 and two in early 2026 once leverage and margins had repaired; Indutrade's acquisitions picked up in H2 2025 after a flat 2023 to 2025 once demand stabilised; Judges itself restarted within 12 to 18 months of the 2014 to 2016 and 2020 troughs, counter-cyclically (Armfield and CoolLED in 2020 to 2021 with net debt near £1m). The three restart conditions are deleveraging to about 1.5x or below (reached in my base case at end-FY27), seller supply returning when markets stabilise (sellers are currently waiting; the counter-cyclical pattern after 2020 suggests supply returns 6 to 12 months after the trough is visible), and a deal team that can source without the founder (in place since July 2025, unproven). I therefore put the first deal in H2 2027 with 65% to 70% probability, and FY28 deployment of about £12m with 55% to 60% probability. The historical rate of 1.3 deals and roughly 10 points of revenue growth a year is not reachable inside the window; my base case adds about 7 points of revenue in FY28 and 8 to 9 points in FY29 to FY30 from acquisitions, roughly half the historical rate, at a slightly higher multiple (5.5x) than the 5.3x average excluding Geotek.

Loop 4: the negative loop at the weak units is the one most likely to surprise. Fire Testing Technology shows the loop in its accounts: losses widened from £0.96m to £1.21m while R&D fell from £0.65m to £0.55m, and the new cone calorimeter is a year late. Armfield's share loss (40% to 15%) came from exactly this loop over a decade. The centre is now intervening (talent, product, Armfield insourcing, product exits at Scientifica) and the zero-divestment policy means the loop must be broken from inside rather than cut off. I credit about half the repair by FY28. The second "cockroach" risk from the first set (another impairment at Fire Testing Technology or Scientifica, about 25% over 18 months) is this loop surfacing in the statutory accounts; it would not change adjusted EPS but would damage the narrative that underpins any re-rating.

Loop 6: the demand loop has flattened, not reversed. The China catch-up is a refill of deferred orders (the "Use, Refill or Price" pattern), worth a one-off lift to H1 27 revenue of perhaps £4m to £6m, after which China grows flat to low single digit against structural localisation. The US is in a stopgap year, with the FY2027 request again proposing cuts; Q3 2026 NIH grants were −8% year on year. The order loop therefore supports the mechanical FY27 rebound but not an acceleration beyond it, which is why my organic volume growth falls from 4 points in FY27 to 2 to 2.5 points by FY29 to FY30.

Net effect on the line items.

  • Revenue. Organic +6.5% (FY27) is loop 1 and loop 6; the step to +11% in FY28 and +17% in FY29 is loops 3 and the coring lump; excluding both, growth is 5% to 6%.
  • Margin. Loop 1 adds about 1.7 points in FY27 and 1.1 points in FY28; loop 4 costs about 0.7 points today and 0.5 by FY28; loop 3 is roughly margin-neutral (acquired businesses at about 20%).
  • Cash and debt. Loop 2 keeps net debt in a £40m to £45m band through FY27; loop 3 then takes it to about £60m by FY30 while leverage falls to 1.3x to 1.4x on higher EBITDA.
  • EPS. Loops 1 and 6 deliver the FY26 to FY28 recovery (193p to about 259p organic); loop 3 adds about 22p in FY28 and 73p by FY30; loop 4 withholds about 15p to 25p relative to a fully repaired portfolio.

Where I differ from the first set. It excluded acquisitions from the base case until a deal completes. I think a base case for FY28 to FY30 that has zero acquisitions misdescribes a company whose purpose is to acquire and whose balance sheet will have capacity, and it would make the model's out-year EPS a floor rather than a central estimate; I include them at half the historical rate and identify their contribution so that the Consumer can treat FY28 EPS of about 259p (organic) and 283p (with deals) as two reference points. On the dividend-leverage loop I agree with the first set and quantify it as a 12-month delay to the flywheel relative to a non-dividend-paying peer.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

❌ D48

Judgement. Moderate weight for FY26, because the year is 80% decided by one quarter that nobody outside the company can observe before January 2027; low-to-moderate weight for FY27, because it is unguided, depends on a US appropriations outcome and a sterling rate that are outside management's control, and will be guided by a management team with no record of its own; low weight for FY28 to FY30 as point estimates but reasonable weight as a direction, because the mechanics (operating leverage from a trough, a dividend-constrained but intact balance sheet, a mid-cycle margin ceiling of about 20%) are better established than the timing. The lean is asymmetric by year: FY26 slightly below consensus (my 193p against 199p), because the H2 margin consensus needs is not supported by the cost structure and the Q4 execution risk is one-sided; FY27 modestly above consensus (234p against 219p median), because consensus embeds almost no operating leverage and sits on a revenue base that is too low given the order book; FY28 above consensus (259p organic, 283p with acquisitions, against 248p), for the same reason plus the flywheel. The lean is not a judgement that management will out-deliver; it is a judgement that consensus has been rebased to a no-recovery world and that the arithmetic of a fixed cost base in a recovery is more reliable than the record of the last three years suggests.

The record, and what kind of record it is.

  • Start-of-year consensus against outcome: FY24 about 349p against 283.4p (−19%); FY25 about 367p to 375p against 275.3p (−26%); FY26 about 300p against about 199p now (−33%). Three downgrades in three years, each prompt and each followed by delivery near the rebased figure: FY24 landed in the 270p to 300p range; FY25 landed 3% below its 285p to 330p range; H1 26 landed at the midpoint of 38p to 42p.
  • The misses were almost entirely external in origin (US funding freeze, China procedure, coring deferral, offshore wind). They were not misses of execution against an internal plan, and they were not accompanied by cash or balance-sheet surprises: cash conversion was 122%, 118% and 90% in FY24, FY25 and H1 26. The Change Forces work called management an "honest tracker" rather than a sandbagger, and I agree: the FY26 rebasing to 200p to 250p assumed no US recovery and no coring, and the company has since anchored to the 200p floor rather than defending the midpoint.
  • The long record (20 years, five non-record years, 25% TSR CAGR) is of a different company: founder-run, acquisition-led, in a benign funding era. It tells the Consumer that the model works when both engines run, not that the current team will hit numbers.
  • The new team's record is five months long: £2m of cost cuts, guidance held, H1 delivered on the midpoint, a share purchase by the chief executive, a CFO resignation. The CFO who built the forecasting discipline leaves in September 2027; a successor will set FY28 guidance in March 2028. The first set expected conservative FY27 guidance in March 2027; I agree, and I expect it to be framed as a range bracketing consensus (roughly 210p to 240p) with the same two stated assumptions (no US recovery, no coring).

How the record translates into weight and lean, by horizon.

Horizon Weight Lean against consensus Why
FY26 (decided by Q4 26; tested 22 January 2027) Moderate Down about 3% (193p against 199p); downside tail to 165p to 175p (25% to 30%); upside to 205p to 210p (15%) Q4 slippage risk is one-sided; the required H2 margin of about 24% on £65m is not achievable, so consensus's EPS needs either revenue above £68m or a large Patent Box catch-up; both are plausible, neither is assured
FY27 (unguided until March 2027; tested at the July 2027 update and September 2027 interims) Low to moderate Up about 5% to 7% (234p against 219p to 224p); cone 185p to 285p Easy H1 comparison (−21% revenue, −72% EPS), China refill, 17.3-week book, 42% drop-through; offsets are US appropriations, sterling after hedges roll, and the FY26 tax catch-up not repeating
FY28 (two brokers) Low as a point, moderate as a direction Up about 4% organic (259p), about 14% with acquisitions (283p), against 248p Consensus incremental margins of 18% imply the leverage is reinvested away; acquisitions are excluded by both brokers
FY29 to FY30 (no consensus) Low Not applicable; my 394p in both years relies on one coring expedition and about £47m of acquisitions Direction (EPS above the FY23 peak by FY29 to FY30) is more reliable than the year

Probability-weighted view of FY27 EPS, as the Consumer will need it for the third set. Below 200p: 25% (Q4 26 slips and the slipped revenue does not fully recover, or a second US funding leg, or sterling above 1.42). 200p to 260p: 50% (my base case sits at 234p inside this band). Above 260p: 25% (China refill larger than assumed, US academic orders turning positive, a coring contract signed early for 2028, or a larger first acquisition). The distribution is centred slightly above consensus and is wider than consensus's own 212p to 243p spread.

Why lean up in FY27 to FY28 when the record is of misses. Three reasons, and one caution.

  1. The misses were against expectations built in a different demand regime; consensus has now been cut 33% in twelve months and sits at the bottom of the company's own range with no recovery assumed. The clearing conditions (deep cuts across all brokers, rebased guidance with conservative inputs) are largely met.
  2. The direction of the next surprise is set by the order book, not by management: year-to-date intake moved from −12% to −1% between June and late September, which is a 15% rise in Q3, and the book is level with a year earlier. Revenue lags intake by about four months, so H1 27 revenue is substantially written already.
  3. Operating leverage is arithmetic. Consensus's FY27 incremental margin of about 24% would require the company to add about £3m of cost on £7m of revenue, which neither the cost actions nor management's stated intent supports. The caution: this is the fourth consecutive year in which an analyst could have written "consensus is conservative on margin", and the first three were wrong because volume fell. The lean therefore rests entirely on the order book converting; if January 2027 shows Q4 slippage of more than about £5m, the FY27 lean should be removed, not reduced.

How much the forecasts should be allowed to move the investment case. The Consumer should treat the base case as the centre of a cone whose width for FY27 EPS is about ±25% and for FY28 about ±30%, which is two to three times the width that would apply to Halma or Diploma. Predictability improves materially only after two events: the January 2027 trading update (Q4 conversion, full-year intake, order-book weeks) and the July 2027 trading update (the first reported half of organic growth). Until then the forecasts carry the weight of a mechanism, not of a demonstrated delivery.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C55

Judgement. The items below operating profit are, on balance, a modest positive for EPS over the window and a modest negative for the quality of the FY26 figure. Tax is the one that changes the forecast: the adjusted rate has moved from about 23% (FY25) to 18% (H1 26) on Patent Box relief, I expect FY26 to land at about 17% including a catch-up element worth about 8p to 15p of EPS that does not recur, and I model 20% in FY27 and 21% from FY28, so that tax is worth about 20p to 25p of EPS a year relative to the FY25 rate. Interest falls from 15% of operating profit (FY25) to about 10% (FY28), worth about 10p of EPS at FY28 profit levels. The share count drifts up by about 0.7% a year with no buybacks and no issuance for cash. The gap between adjusted and statutory earnings stays wide (statutory EPS will be roughly 45% to 60% of adjusted) because amortisation of acquired intangibles runs at £12m to £14m a year and further impairments at the weak businesses are plausible; that gap does not affect cash but does affect the credibility of "adjusted" as a measure of return on the capital deployed. Free cash flow, on the full definition, is lower than net income (75% to 80%) in FY26 to FY28 and lower in absolute terms than in FY24 to FY25 (£10m to £14m against £17m to £18m), while the dividend keeps rising, which is the arithmetic behind the leverage constraint in P-02.

Tax: the item that moves the numbers.

  • The facts: adjusted tax rate 21% (FY24), about 23% (FY25, derived), 18% (H1 26); UK headline 25%; Patent Box applies a 10% rate to profits from qualifying patents; Shore credits "about 30p" of combined cost-action and Patent Box benefit to H2 26 while keeping its own long-run rate at 23%; Zeus models 20% for FY26 and FY27; the company says ongoing investment in innovation will sustain the relief.
  • My reading: the Patent Box benefit is structurally recurring at about 3 to 5 points of rate (a company whose products rest on proprietary design and which spends 7% of revenue on R&D will have a meaningful qualifying stream), so 20% to 21% is the right recurring rate, between Zeus and Shore. H1 26's 18% on a half with very low profit, and the size of the H2 benefit Shore describes, point to a first-year or catch-up element: enhanced claims typically bring prior-period relief through in the year the claim methodology is established. I therefore model FY26 at 17% (H2 about 16.5%), of which 3 to 5 points, about 8p to 15p of FY26 EPS, is non-recurring. The Consumer should treat recurring FY26 EPS as about 180p to 185p, not 193p, when judging FY27 growth. This is the first set's point (C-54) made quantitative; I put the one-off somewhat smaller than its 20p to 30p because part of the lower rate is permanent.
  • Sensitivity: each point of tax rate is about 3p of FY27 EPS and about 4p of FY28 EPS. Shore's 23% against my 20% to 21% is worth about 8p to 10p of FY27 EPS. The FY26 annual report (March 2027) tax note will settle the split between recurring and catch-up.
  • Acquired businesses may qualify less, which is why I step the rate to 21% from FY28 as acquisitions resume.

Interest. Covered in P-02: £3.2m (FY26), £2.8m (FY27), £2.7m (FY28), £3.0m (FY29), £3.3m (FY30). SONIA is swapped to 2028 at 3.2% to 3.7%, so the sensitivity to market rates is about £0.1m per 100 basis points; the sensitivity to leverage through the margin grid is about £0.3m to £0.4m across the whole 1.85% to 3.5% range. Interest cover rises from about 6x (FY26) to 10x (FY28) and 11x (FY30). Against FY21 to FY23, when interest was £0.7m to £3.4m, the absolute cost is similar but it now takes a smaller share of a recovering profit.

One-offs and the adjusted-to-statutory gap.

  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles: about £12m to £14m a year on the existing book, falling slowly and then rising with new deals. It is non-cash and correctly excluded from adjusted EPS; it is also the largest reason statutory EPS (82.7p in FY25) is a third of adjusted (275.3p).
  • Impairments: FY25 took £2.3m (Armfield goodwill) and £2.0m (Rockwash) at group level, plus £8.7m of parent-company write-downs. Another impairment at Fire Testing Technology (loss-making, R&D cut) or Scientifica is plausible within 18 months (about 25%); the FY26 audit is RSM's first after Financial Reporting Council-driven restatements, which raises the chance that balances are tested hard. No effect on adjusted EPS or cash.
  • Currency derivatives: fair-value movements on the economic hedges are adjusting items because the hedges are not under IFRS 9. With sterling moving, these can be several hundred thousand pounds either way in statutory profit each half; adjusted EPS is unaffected, but the cash settlement of the hedges is real and shows in operating cash flow.
  • Acquisition costs: nil in FY26; perhaps £0.3m to £0.5m a year from FY28.
  • Capitalised development: £1.8m in FY25 (18% of R&D, against 6% at Oxford Instruments and 11% at Halma), up from £1.4m. It flatters adjusted operating profit by about £0.5m to £0.8m net of amortisation of earlier capitalisation, about 6p to 9p of EPS; the amortisation charge rises as the balance grows, so the flattering effect shrinks over the window. I keep capitalised development at about £1.8m to £2.2m a year in capital spending.
  • Minorities and associates: none since the 18% minority in Geotek do Brasil was bought in December 2025 for £1.9m; a small positive to FY26 EPS (about 2p to 3p) already in the run-rate.

Share count, buybacks and issuance. Shares outstanding rose from 6.2m (2019) to 6.7m (2025), about 1.3% a year, mostly from the Share Incentive Plan (matching shares, 312 participants) and option exercises (about 0.11m dilutive shares in FY24). I model +0.05m shares a year from the plan and options (about 0.7%), with option exercise proceeds of about £0.2m a year as the only issuance for cash. No buybacks: the board has considered and rejected them as inferior to acquisitions, organic investment and dividends, and nothing in the leadership change points the other way until a new CFO is in post. For reference, £5m spent at about 3,450p would retire about 145,000 shares (2.2%) and, funded at 6% debt, would add about 1.5% to EPS; it would also take leverage to 1.8x on FY27 EBITDA. I regard a buyback as a low-probability upside (under 15% before FY28) that would signal a change of capital-allocation philosophy rather than add much to EPS.

Dividend. FY26 126p (+10%; interim 36.0p declared), FY27 139p, FY28 152p, FY29 168p, FY30 184p, each +10%, the 20th to 24th consecutive years of at least 10% growth. Cash cost £7.9m, £8.8m, £9.7m, £10.7m, £11.9m. Cover on my EPS: 1.5x, 1.7x, 1.9x, 2.3x, 2.1x. The commitment holds in my base case because FY26 EPS stays above about 185p; it would be tested, and the dividend most likely held flat rather than cut, in the downside case of FY26 EPS below about 175p with FY27 flat.

Free cash flow runs at 75% to 80% of adjusted net income throughout, lower than the first set's 100% to 110%, because I deduct capitalised development (£1.8m to £2.2m), lease payments (£1.5m to £1.9m) and a working-capital build with revenue; on the first set's narrower definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) my figures are about 105% to 115% of net income, so the two are consistent. The important change against the last four to six years is the dividend's share of free cash flow, which roughly doubles. That, more than anything below the operating line, is what shapes the balance sheet and the acquisition timetable.

EPS bridge FY25 to FY28, to show where the below-the-line items sit. 275p (FY25) → −90p lost coring and volume net of price, cost actions and lower interest (to about 185p recurring FY26, at a 20% rate) → +8p FY26 tax catch-up (193p reported) → +40p FY27 organic operating recovery → −9p FY26 catch-up not repeating and rate to 20% → +5p lower interest → +5p acquisitions → −1p dilution (234p) → +34p FY28 organic operating → −4p tax to 21% → +1p interest → +19p acquisitions → −2p dilution (283p). Across FY25 to FY28 the below-the-line items net to roughly +12p (tax about +15p relative to the FY25 rate of about 23%, interest about +8p, dilution about −11p), small against the operating line but worth stating because the FY26 to FY27 tax step hides about 9p of underlying FY27 growth.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C55

Judgement. The net picture is a V in profit with a slow slope on the right-hand side and a flat line in cash. Revenue regains its FY25 level in FY28 only with acquisitions (organically in FY29); adjusted operating profit regains FY25's £28m in FY28 and FY23's £35m in FY29; the margin never regains the FY22 to FY23 level of 26% and settles at 18% to 20% without coring; EPS regains FY25's 275p in FY28 and FY23's 375p in FY29 to FY30, but only with one coring expedition and about £47m of acquisitions; free cash flow stays below the FY24 to FY25 level until FY29 while the dividend keeps compounding at 10%, so net debt does not fall. The drivers of the difference between the next four years and the last four to six are concentrated: volume recovery on a fixed cost base accounts for about 60% of the FY26 to FY28 profit change, the acquisition restart about 20%, tax about 10% and cost actions about 10%; coring is a single lump worth about 55p that moves whole years. For the third set's purposes, the forecast is therefore highly dependent on one external driver (academic and commercial order flow converting to shipments) and one internal decision (the pace of acquisitions), and almost independent of price, materials, interest or share count.

The main line items, last four to six years against the next four.
