The bridge that explains the difference (adjusted operating profit, £m; "volume" excludes the price component of revenue growth). FY25 £28.0m → FY26E £18.8m: coring absent −4.0; organic volume excluding coring and price (about −£17.6m of revenue) at a 55% contribution, lower than the marginal 65% because part of the lost volume is low-margin Materials Sciences and offshore-wind work, −9.7; price +3.0; wage and other inflation −3.1; cost actions net of reinvestment +0.8; volume-linked pay, commissions and travel falling away +2.5; other, currency and the Geotek do Brasil minority +1.3. FY26E → FY28E £27.1m: organic volume (about +£8.8m) at 65% +5.7; price +6.6; inflation −6.0; semi-variable costs returning −1.8; cost actions and underperformer repair +2.5; reinvestment −0.9; acquisitions +2.0; other +0.2. FY28E → FY30E £37.7m: organic volume (about +£6.2m) at 65% +4.0; price +7.4; inflation −6.3; reinvestment and semi-variable −0.5; underperformer repair +1.0; acquisitions +5.2; coring nil in FY30 (the FY29 figure of £37.2m includes about +4.8 from one expedition); other −0.2.

Where the forecasts differ from the past, item by item.

  • Growth composition. Acquisitions supplied about 12 of the 19.5 points of annual growth in FY21 to FY23. In my FY27 to FY30 they supply about 5 points, and only from FY28. Organic growth of about 5% is below the 7% nineteen-year average because US academic funding is not assumed to recover to its 2023 run-rate and because about 1 point a year is lost at the weak businesses and in China.
  • Margin level. The structural additions (central costs about +£2m, UK tax and National Insurance about +£3m, R&D about +£1m) lower the recovered-volume margin by about 4 points against FY23, from 25.6% to a ceiling of about 20% (22% with coring). Consensus's 17.2% for FY27 to FY28 is in my view 1 to 2 points too low for FY28; my 19.8% for FY30 is at the top of what the cost structure allows.
  • Earnings quality. FY26 EPS carries 10p to 15p of non-recurring tax benefit. From FY27 the adjusted figure is clean in cash terms but the statutory figure will remain 40% to 55% of it because of amortisation and the likelihood of further impairments.
  • Cash and capital. Free cash flow after dividends was £10m to £12m a year in FY23 to FY25; it is £2m to £5m in FY26 to FY28. The compounding engine therefore runs on debt capacity created by EBITDA growth, not on retained cash, until FY29. Return on total invested capital does not approach the 30% target: about 15% in FY27 to FY28 and about 19% in FY29 (with coring); acquisitions at 5.5x EBIT are accretive to it, which is a reason to expect management to pursue them as soon as leverage allows.

Concentration of the drivers (for D-03 in the third set). Of the roughly £8.3m increase in adjusted operating profit from FY26 to FY28 in my base case, organic volume and price net of inflation and returning semi-variable costs contributes about £4.5m (54%), acquisitions £2.0m (24%), cost actions and underperformer repair net of reinvestment £1.6m (19%), and other items about £0.2m. Of the roughly 90p increase in EPS over the same period, operating profit contributes about 98p (74p organic, 24p acquired), tax −13p (the catch-up dropping out and the rate rising to 21%), interest +6p and dilution −3p. So two drivers, order conversion to volume and the acquisition restart, carry over 80% of the forecast change, and the first of them is external. That is a concentrated set of drivers, and it is why the cone in P-06 is wide.

Skew and width over the next twelve months. FY27 adjusted operating profit of about £17m to £27m around a base of £22.6m: the downside needs Q4 26 slippage plus a second US funding leg or sterling above 1.42; the upside needs the China refill to be larger and faster than £5m and US academic orders to turn positive. The distribution is centred slightly above consensus and skewed modestly up, because the downside cases are mostly timing (revenue moving between years) while the upside cases are level (revenue that consensus does not contain). Over four years the skew is more clearly positive, because acquisitions and coring are excluded from consensus altogether and my base case includes only half of what the company has done historically.

Peers. Judges' forecast profile (EPS +20% a year from a trough on 5% organic growth) is the profile of a fixed-cost maker at the turn, which the Industry Digest says to buy at the turn and the Sector Digest describes as "early and against the price". Renishaw is a year ahead on the same path (margin 15.7% to 18.7%, EPS forecasts rising 15% in a quarter); Oxford Instruments is six months ahead (orders +44%). Halma and Diploma do not have this profile: their growth is steadier and acquisition-fed throughout, which is why their multiples are 23x to 30x against Judges' 14.5x. The net impact of the forecasts on the investment case is therefore positive relative to what the market has priced, but it is the positive of a cyclical recovery in a company whose structural margin ceiling has come down, not the positive of a compounder returning to form.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C56

Judgement in one paragraph. FY26 is the trough: revenue of about £125m (−14%), adjusted operating profit of about £18.8m (15.0%) and adjusted EPS of about 193p, slightly below the 199p consensus, with the year decided by Q4 shipments that are mostly already in the order book. FY27 is a mechanical, margin-led recovery: organic revenue +6.5% to about £135m, operating profit £22.6m (16.7%), EPS about 234p, modestly above consensus, with almost no help from acquisitions. FY28 adds the first meaningful acquisitions and takes operating profit back to the FY25 level (£27.1m, 18.0%) and EPS to about 283p (259p organic). FY29 and FY30 reach about 394p of EPS, above the FY23 peak, on organic growth of about 5%, a margin of about 20% without coring, one coring expedition (placed in FY29) and about £47m of acquisitions at 5.5x EBIT. The dividend compounds at 10% throughout, which keeps net debt in a £40m to £60m band and leverage at 1.3x to 1.7x. The forecasts are concentrated on two drivers, order conversion and the acquisition restart, carry a cone of about ±25% on FY27 EPS, and are tested at three dated events: the trading update of about 22 January 2027 (Q4 conversion), the results of 24 March 2027 (FY27 guidance and the tax note) and the July 2027 trading update (the first reported half of organic growth).

H2 26 carries 80% of FY26 EPS, as management has signalled. H1 27 is the half the market will read as "the turn": revenue +13%, operating profit up about 80%, EPS more than doubling from 39p. H2 27 growth is only 4% because it is measured against a Q4-heavy H2 26; the Consumer should expect the July 2027 and September 2027 updates to look much stronger than the January 2028 one.

Why these are the central numbers and not others.

  1. FY26 revenue £125m, not £121m. FY26 order intake will finish close to FY25's level (year to date −1% at late September after Q3 at about +15%), while FY26 revenue is 14% below FY25; the book-to-bill above 1.0 means that either Q4 26 converts (my case) or FY27 revenue is higher still. H2 26 at £69.5m is −8% against an H2 25 that had no coring, which is a smaller decline than H1's −13% excluding coring, consistent with the order recovery lagging into revenue by about four months. The margin of 20.1% follows Shore's "over 20%" and my cost structure. I lean below consensus on profit and EPS, not on revenue.
  2. FY27 organic +6.5%, not +10%. The easy H1 comparison and the China refill (about £4m to £6m of deferred shipments) give H1 27 about +13%; H2 27 is only +4% because it laps the Q4 26 surge. The US is assumed flat (another stopgap year); Europe recovers slowly from −20% orders; commercial grows mid to high single digit; offshore wind stays flat to down. Price is 2.5 points of the total. The first set's 6% to 9% range is right; I sit at its lower-middle because of the H2 comparison.
  3. Incremental margin 42% then 40%. Reconciled in P-01 and P-03: gross contribution 65%, less returning semi-variable costs, less wage inflation ahead of academic pricing, plus the full-year cost actions and half the underperformer repair.
  4. Acquisitions at half the historical pace, from H2 27. Leverage of about 1.5x at end-FY27, a deal team in place since July 2025, a "healthy" pipeline, seller supply returning 6 to 12 months after the trough is visible, and a dividend that leaves only £3m to £5m a year of retained cash. £47m over FY27 to FY30 at 5.5x EBIT adds about £8.5m of EBIT by FY30 (£7.2m in the FY30 year, with the last deal at half-year effect) and about 73p of EPS net of funding cost. The first set excluded this; I include it with the contribution separately shown, for the reasons in P-05.
  5. One coring expedition in FY29. The customer has approved long-term funding; the schedule has slipped three times; the historical pattern is three years in four; I put 40% on 2028, 30% on 2029, 15% on 2030 and 15% on none inside the window, so a single expedition in FY29 is the most probable single scenario, worth about £7m of revenue and about £4.8m of operating profit (about 55p of EPS). It makes FY30 EPS flat on FY29 in the table; excluding coring, FY30 EPS grows about 16% on FY29.
  6. Tax 17%, 20%, 21%, 21%, 21%. Patent Box is recurring at 3 to 5 points below the headline rate; FY26 carries a catch-up of about 8p to 15p (P-08).
  7. No currency effect. Sterling at 1.30 to 1.38 to the dollar. A sustained 10% appreciation against both the dollar and the euro would cost about £7m of operating profit, roughly 80p of EPS, with a lag of two to four quarters; this is the largest unmodelled sensitivity and is not in consensus either.
  8. Dividend +10% a year. The 20-year record and management's explicit priority; cover rebuilds to 1.9x by FY28. A hold (not a cut) is the response in the downside case.

The cone, with probabilities.

  • FY26 EPS. Base 193p (50%); downside 165p to 180p (30%: £3m to £6m of Q4 revenue slipping into January, costing 25p to 45p; partly recovered in FY27); upside 200p to 210p (20%: Q4 fully converts at a margin above 21%, or the Patent Box catch-up is larger).
  • FY27 EPS. Base 234p; below 200p 25%; 200p to 260p 50%; above 260p 25%. The downside combines a Q4 26 slip that does not fully recover with a second US funding leg (US academic orders −10% to −15%) or sterling above 1.42; the upside combines a larger China refill, US academic orders turning positive and an early coring contract signature for 2028.
  • FY28 EPS. Base 283p (259p organic); range about 210p to 340p; the acquisition pace is the main swing inside the range, with coring in 2028 (40%) the main swing above it (+55p).

What the Consumer should watch, in order, with the dates.

  1. About 22 January 2027, FY26 trading update: full-year order intake (the test is intake for FY26 within 2% of FY25, which implies a book-to-bill above 1.0); order-book weeks at December (15 to 17 is consistent with my case; below 14 is not); confirmation that FY26 adjusted EPS is "in line" (about 195p to 205p); any comment on Q4 shipments deferred into 2027. This single event resolves most of the FY26 cone and about a third of the FY27 cone.
  2. 24 March 2027, FY26 results: the tax note (recurring rate against catch-up); first FY27 guidance under Prestidge (I expect a range near 210p to 240p with "no US recovery, no coring" assumptions); dividend +10% to 126p; CFO successor; any first acquisition; Fire Testing Technology and Armfield commentary.
  3. July 2027 trading update and September 2027 interims: H1 27 revenue growth of about 13% and margin of about 14% are my markers; order intake growth by region (the US turning positive would move FY28 above my case); leverage at June 2027 (about 1.7x seasonally).
  4. Through 2027: US FY2027 appropriations (a stopgap is the base case; enacted cuts of even half the request would take US academic orders down another 10% to 15% and FY27 EPS to the bottom of the cone); NIH monthly grant data; sterling against the dollar and euro; a Geotek contract signature (which would add about 55p to whichever year it names); the first acquisition and its multiple.

Where this base case differs from the first set's steer and from consensus, in summary. Against the first set: higher confidence in FY26 revenue above consensus but a lower FY26 margin; acquisitions and one coring expedition inside the base case rather than as scenarios; realised incremental margin of 40% to 45% rather than 60%; slower deleveraging and a lower free cash flow conversion on a full definition. Against consensus: revenue 3% to 9% higher in every year; FY26 EBIT 7% lower and EPS 3% lower; FY27 EBIT 3% and EPS 7% higher; FY28 EBIT 14% and EPS 14% higher (4% organic). The direction of the differences is the point: consensus describes a company that has stopped compounding; my base case describes a fixed-cost business recovering from a trough at half its old acquisition pace, with a margin ceiling 4 to 5 points below its peak and a dividend that limits how fast the engine can restart.

Adjusted operating profit: £14.0m (20.1%), £8.6m (13.7%), £14.0m (19.3%). The H1 2026 actual was revenue £55.7m (−20.7%), EBITDA £7.1m (12.7%), adjusted operating profit £4.8m (8.6%).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports two segments, Vacuum and Materials Sciences, with central costs unallocated. FY2025 actual: Vacuum revenue £73.9m, Materials Sciences £71.9m (including Geotek's coring expedition in H1 2025, about £6m at a margin of 65% to 75%); segment EBITDA on the brokers' basis about £37.7m before central costs of about £5.0m, giving group EBITDA of £32.7m; Vacuum is the more stable segment (EBITDA margin about 27%), Materials Sciences the more cyclical (about 20% excluding coring, about 24% with it).

My inputs by segment (revenue £m; EBITDA £m; margin), with acquisitions shown separately because I do not allocate them:

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Vacuum 63.0; 16.5; 26.2% 68.5; 18.8; 27.4% 72.5; 20.5; 28.3% 76.1; 22.2; 29.2% 79.5; 23.5; 29.6%
Materials Sciences, excluding coring 62.2; 12.0; 19.3% 64.8; 13.4; 20.7% 68.1; 14.9; 21.9% 71.5; 16.1; 22.5% 74.7; 17.4; 23.3%
Geotek coring (in Materials Sciences) 0; 0 0; 0 0; 0 7.0; 4.9; 70% 0; 0
Acquired businesses (new deals from H2 2027) 0; 0 2.0; 0.5 10.0; 2.6 22.3; 5.8 35.8; 9.4
Central costs −5.0 −5.2 −5.4 −5.6 −5.8
Group 125.2; 23.5; 18.8% 135.3; 27.5; 20.3% 150.6; 32.6; 21.6% 176.9; 43.4; 24.5% 190.0; 44.5; 23.4%

The FY2026 segment split matches FactSet's segment consensus (Vacuum £62.7m, Materials science £62.6m, which sum to £125.3m against the FactSet group median of £121m; the segment figures are the internally consistent ones). Vacuum carries the China catch-up in H1 2027 (cryogenics, UHV, deposition) and the commercial and semiconductor growth; Materials Sciences carries the three businesses under repair (Fire Testing Technology, Armfield, Scientifica is in Vacuum), the offshore-wind geotechnical weakness and the coring lump. FactSet's segment EBITDA consensus (Vacuum £16.0m, Materials science £10.4m, Unallocated −£5.1m for FY2026) sums to £21.3m, below the group consensus of £24.7m, which is a further sign that the three brokers' line items are not reconciled; my FY2026 group EBITDA of £23.5m sits between the two.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  1. Order intake to revenue. Group order intake fell 18% in Q1 2026, was flat in Q2 and rose about 15% in Q3, taking the year-to-date figure from −12% at June to −1% at late September; the order book was 17.3 weeks at June 2026 (15.7 at December 2025, 17.4 a year earlier). Revenue lags intake by about four months, so FY2026 revenue (−14%) is well below FY2026 intake (roughly flat), and the difference converts in Q4 2026 and H1 2027. I assume H2 2026 revenue of £69.5m (−8% against an H2 2025 that had no coring) with a heavy Q4, then H1 2027 of £62.9m (+13%) including about £4m to £6m of China shipments deferred from 2026, and H2 2027 of £72.4m (+4%) against the Q4-heavy comparison.
  2. Organic growth after the rebound. Excluding coring and acquisitions: −11% (FY2026), +6.5%, +5.5%, +5.0%, +4.5%. Price is about 2.5 points of each year (cost recovery, not real pricing); volume is about 4 points in FY2027, 3 in FY2028 and 2 to 2.5 thereafter. The United States (about 25% of revenue, half academic) is assumed flat through another stopgap funding year; China grows flat to low single digit after the 2026 catch-up; Europe recovers slowly from −20% orders; commercial and industrial (about one-third of revenue) grows mid to high single digit; offshore-wind geotechnical work stays flat to down; about 1 point a year is lost to share erosion at the weaker businesses and in China.
  3. Operating leverage. The fixed cost block (staff £48.5m, other external charges £19.0m, depreciation £4.7m in FY2025) was about £67m in FY2025 and ran at about £63m annualised in H1 2026 after £1.8m to £2.0m of cost actions and the disappearance of volume-linked pay, commissions and travel. Gross contribution on marginal revenue is about 65%; the realised incremental margin I use is 42% in FY2027 and 40% thereafter, after returning semi-variable costs (about £1m), wage inflation of 4% to 5% (about £2.2m a year) and other inflation (about £0.6m), offset by price (about £3.3m a year), the full-year effect of cost actions and about half of the available repair at the three loss-making or sub-par businesses (about £1.2m in FY2027, £0.8m in FY2028).
  4. Margin ceiling. The structural cost additions since FY2023 (about £2m of central cost, about £3m of UK corporation tax and employer National Insurance, about £1m of R&D) lower the recovered-volume adjusted operating margin by about 4 points: 19.8% in FY2030 without coring, about 21% in the FY2029 coring year, against 25.6% in FY2023.
  5. Geotek coring. The company says the next expedition is "no earlier than 2028"; the customer has long-term funding approved; the pattern has been three years in four. I place one expedition in FY2029 (revenue £7.0m, EBITDA £4.9m, about 55p of EPS) as the most probable single scenario (my probabilities: 40% for 2028, 30% for 2029, 15% for 2030, 15% none in the window). It makes FY2030 EPS flat on FY2029; excluding it, FY2030 EPS grows about 16%.
  6. Acquisitions. None before H2 2027. Leverage of about 1.5x at end-FY2027, a deal team in place since July 2025 and seller supply returning as markets stabilise support £5m in FY2027, £12m in FY2028 and £15m a year in FY2029 and FY2030 at about 5.5x EBIT, about half the historical pace; cumulative acquired EBITDA of about £9.4m in FY2030, about 73p of EPS net of the interest on the debt that funds the purchases.
  7. Cash. Cash conversion (cash from operations over adjusted operating profit) of 110% to 125% in FY2026 to FY2028 as working capital is held roughly flat in pounds while revenue grows; free cash flow (after tax, interest, all capital spending and lease payments, before dividends and acquisitions) of £10.1m, £12.4m, £14.3m, £20.1m and £21.1m; dividends of £7.9m, £8.8m, £9.7m, £10.7m and £11.9m; adjusted net debt (excluding leases) of £40.9m, £42.3m, £49.7m, £55.3m and £61.1m at the year-ends, or 1.7x, 1.5x, 1.5x, 1.3x and 1.4x EBITDA. Financial items: the facility is SONIA plus 1.85% to 3.5% with SONIA swapped at 3.2% to 3.7% to 2028, so interest is driven by debt levels and the leverage grid, not by market rates.
  8. Tax. Patent Box relief is recurring at 3 to 5 points below the 25% headline: 20% in FY2027 and 21% from FY2028 (acquired businesses qualify less). FY2026 at 17% includes a first-year or catch-up element of about 8p to 15p of EPS that does not recur.
  9. Currency. Sterling assumed at 1.30 to 1.38 to the dollar. About 50% of revenue is in dollars and 33% in euros against a cost base that is 80% to 90% sterling; hedges cover about 12 months. A sustained 10% appreciation against both currencies would cost about £7m of adjusted operating profit (about 80p of EPS) with a lag of two to four quarters; this is not in the inputs.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • FY2026. In January 2026 the board set adjusted EPS guidance of 200p to 250p, assuming no recovery in US academic demand and no Geotek coring revenue. At the July 2026 trading update and the September 2026 interims it said full-year expectations were in line with market consensus (about 200p to 203p). My FY2026 adjusted EPS of 193p is about 3% to 4% below that anchor and at the bottom of the formal range, because my H2 2026 adjusted operating margin of 20.1% (Shore Capital: "over 20%") on revenue of £69.5m gives adjusted operating profit of £18.8m against the FactSet median EBIT of £20.3m; my revenue of £125.2m is above the £121m median. Management has flagged a "greater-than-usual" Q4 weighting, customers asking for deferred shipments, cost actions of about £2m, and a larger Patent Box benefit; all are in my inputs.
  • FY2027 and beyond. There is no financial guidance. The first FY2027 guidance is expected with the FY2026 results on 24 March 2027; I expect a conservative range bracketing consensus with the same two stated assumptions. My FY2027 EPS of 234p compares with the FactSet median of 219p (mean 224p; range 212p to 243p). My FY2028 EPS of 283p (259p excluding new acquisitions) compares with 248p from two brokers.
  • Dividend. The policy is growth of at least 10% a year, subject to cover; the interim was raised 10% to 36.0p at 1.1x cover, which management called unsustainable as a cover level. My inputs hold 10% growth throughout, with cover rebuilding to 1.5x (FY2026), 1.7x (FY2027), 1.9x (FY2028) and 2.1x to 2.3x thereafter.
  • Mid-term. The only stated ambition is a 30% return on total invested capital. My inputs imply about 12% in FY2026, 14% to 15% in FY2027 to FY2028 and 18% to 19% in FY2029 to FY2030; the target is not reached inside the window.
  • Acquisition criteria. 3x to 7x EBIT, borrowing up to about 3x EBITDA under a 3.0x covenant; my 5.5x and leverage held at 1.3x to 1.7x are inside both.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Operating profit. The company's "adjusted operating profit" (before amortisation of acquired intangibles, goodwill impairments, acquisition costs and fair-value movements on currency derivatives, which are not under IFRS 9 hedge accounting) is the same basis as the brokers' EBIT. FY2025: £28.0m (19.2%). FactSet's history rows "EBIT (adjusted)" (£12.8m, 8.8% for FY2025) and "gross profit" are standardised lines burdened with amortisation and must not be used as operating margin history.
  • EBITDA. Brokers' EBITDA is adjusted operating profit plus depreciation of property, plant and equipment and right-of-use assets and amortisation of capitalised development (together about £4.7m in FY2025): FY2025 £32.7m (22.5%). FactSet's history "EBITDA (reported, FactSet operating)" is £32.1m for FY2025 and differs slightly; the "Actuals on the brokers' basis" rows in the financials block (revenue £146m, EBITDA £32.7m, EPS £2.71) are the like-for-like base for consensus growth.
  • EPS. Adjusted basic EPS on about 6.7m shares: FY2025 275.3p (FactSet brokers' basis £2.71; statutory basic 82.7p; FactSet diluted before unusual items 74.2p). My cross-check EPS below is adjusted basic on average shares of 6.70m rising to 6.90m.
  • Tax. The adjusted tax rate applies to adjusted PBT; FactSet's reported FY2025 rate of 32.6% is on statutory profit after non-deductible impairments and amortisation and is not meaningful for the model.
  • Net debt. The company's adjusted net debt (£45.3m at June 2026; £42.6m at December 2025) excludes lease liabilities of about £3m; FactSet's figure (£48.2m at June 2026) includes them.
  • Free cash flow. FactSet's history "free cash flow" line reconciles to operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex less dividends paid (FY2025: 26.4 − 5.4 − 7.1 = 13.9) and is not a conversion measure. My cross-check free cash flow is cash from operations after tax and interest, less all capital spending (fixed assets and capitalised development), less lease payments, before dividends and acquisitions.
  • Dividends. The company declares an interim (paid in H2) and a final (paid in H1 of the following year); FactSet's FF_DPS is by payment period, so FactSet's FY2026 figure will be about 118p against the 126p declared for the year.
  • Half-year phasing. The company reports in halves; the H1 2026 interim results (23 September 2026) gave revenue £55.7m (the July trading update said £55.5m) and adjusted EPS 39.0p ("about 40p" in July); the interim figures are used.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Earnings per share 1.93 2.34 2.83 3.94 3.94
Free cash flow 10.1 12.4 14.3 20.1 21.1

Earnings per share is adjusted basic, in pounds (1.93 = 193p), built as: revenue £125.2m, £135.3m, £150.6m, £176.9m, £190.0m; adjusted operating profit £18.8m, £22.6m, £27.1m, £37.2m, £37.7m; net financial items −£3.2m, −£2.8m, −£2.7m, −£3.0m, −£3.3m; tax 17%, 20%, 21%, 21%, 21%; adjusted net income £12.9m, £15.8m, £19.3m, £27.0m, £27.2m; average shares 6.70m, 6.75m, 6.80m, 6.85m, 6.90m. Excluding new acquisitions and the FY2029 coring expedition, EPS would be 1.93, 2.29, 2.59, 2.92 and 3.20. Free cash flow is in millions of pounds on the definition above: EBITDA less the change in working capital (−£0.4m, −£0.1m, −£1.4m, −£2.7m, −£1.7m), less cash tax (£2.3m, £3.9m, £5.1m, £7.2m, £7.2m), less net interest, less capital spending including capitalised development, less lease payments. Dividends paid (£7.9m, £8.8m, £9.7m, £10.7m, £11.9m) and acquisitions are deducted after it to reach the net debt path of £40.9m, £42.3m, £49.7m, £55.3m and £61.1m.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB, AIM: JDG): a UK buy-and-build group of about 25 autonomous niche scientific-instrument manufacturers selling mainly to academic, research-institute and industrial laboratories worldwide, reported in two segments, Materials Sciences (including Geotek coring and core logging, Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, GDS) and Vacuum (including UHV Design, Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal, Scientifica, Korvus, CoolLED), with about 85% of revenue exported, about 50% of revenue in US dollars and 33% in euros against a largely sterling cost base, and a 20-year record of acquisitions at 3x to 7x EBIT.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Halma (HLMA-GB), the decentralised UK acquirer whose former executives now run Judges' centre, with published sector margins and a record of margin stability through cycles; Renishaw (RSW-GB), a UK precision-instrument exporter with a fixed UK cost base that moved its adjusted operating margin from 15.7% (FY2025) to 18.7% (FY2026) after a cost programme and a volume recovery; Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB), a UK scientific-instrument maker with high academic exposure that pivoted to commercial and semiconductor markets, reporting group order intake up about 44% in the half to September 2026 (trading update, 13 October 2026); SDI Group (SDI-GB), the closest listed small UK serial acquirer of laboratory-instrument businesses through the same downturn; Bruker (BRKR-US), the academic-exposed instrument maker that completed a restructuring in Q2 2026; AddLife (ADDT-SE) and Indutrade (INDT-SE), Nordic serial acquirers that paused and then restarted acquisitions after 2023 to 2025 slowdowns; Diploma (DPLM-GB) and discoverIE (DSCV-GB), UK acquisitive industrials with higher dividend cover; private competitors GUNT, TecQuipment and Edibon (education equipment against Armfield) and Lafayette Instrument/Sutter (electrophysiology against Scientifica). Operating conditions: a US academic research-funding environment of repeated continuing resolutions and grant-flow delays (NIH FY2026 new grants 11% below the 2021 to 2024 average, Morgan Stanley, 12 October 2026; FY2027 budget request proposing NIH about −10% and NSF −54%); resolution of Chinese R&D tax-exemption procedures after a 47% fall in China and Hong Kong orders in H1 2026 (interim results, 23 September 2026), alongside domestic-substitution rules; recovering commercial, semiconductor and battery R&D demand; UK engineering wage inflation and higher employer National Insurance; sterling in a 1.30 to 1.38 range against the US dollar; interest rates of 5% to 6% for acquirers; private-equity and Chinese strategic competition for UK niche instrument targets; a founder-to-professional management transition (new chief executive from February 2026, CFO leaving September 2027). Inputs: The expected changes to test: (1) a return to organic revenue growth from H1 2027 as order intake, which improved from 12% below the prior year at June 2026 to 1% below at late September 2026 (interim results and Shore Capital, 23 September 2026), converts to shipments after a heavily Q4-weighted 2026; (2) an adjusted operating margin recovering from 8.6% in H1 2026 on recovering volume, against the 19.2% of FY2025 and the broker expectation of over 20% in H2 2026 (Shore Capital, 23 September 2026), given staff costs of £48.5m and raw materials of £44.8m on revenue of £145.8m (Annual Report 2025); (3) a restart of acquisitions after none in FY2025 or H1 2026, under a Group Acquisitions Executive appointed July 2025, while leverage was 2.1x adjusted EBITDA and interim dividend cover 1.1x at June 2026; (4) the repair of Fire Testing Technology (operating loss £1.21m on revenue £5.12m in FY2025, Companies House, 11 September 2026), Armfield (goodwill fully impaired, 2025) and Scientifica; (5) a pivot of subsidiaries towards commercial and industrial markets, with eight of 25 businesses growing in H1 2026. The record to test: FY2024 to FY2026 guidance changes (November 2024, July 2025, January 2026) and delivery against each rebased range; the 2014 to 2016 and 2020 recoveries; the 2005 to 2023 acquisition record (about 25 deals at about 6x EBITA, ROTIC falling from 22.7% in 2023 to 12.9% in H1 2026). The strengths to test: niche market leadership and high switching costs; cash conversion (118% in FY2025, 90% in H1 2026); acquisition price discipline; the new central team's operating intervention; against strengths it would need to acquire: a proven deal-sourcing capability without the founder, commercial-market sales reach, software and connectivity capability, and pricing power in academic channels. Focus: How often fixed-cost instrument makers with two-thirds academic exposure have recovered margins to within a few points of their prior peak within two years of an order-book turn without a large cost programme, and how often small serial acquirers with a progressive-dividend commitment and leverage near 2x have restarted acquisitions within 18 months of an earnings trough; and which of Judges' own past recoveries (2015 to 2016, 2020 to 2021) is the better precedent for 2027 given that acquisitions and coring were present then and are absent now.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Renishaw (RSW-GB) and Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB), UK instrument exporters with fixed sterling cost bases and published FX sensitivities (Oxford Instruments: about £0.6m of adjusted operating profit per cent on GBP/USD, Annual Report FY2026) whose margins have swung widely with volume; Halma (HLMA-GB) and Diploma (DPLM-GB), compounders whose forecast dispersion has been narrow; SDI Group (SDI-GB), Judges' closest listed analogue in scale and end-market; Bruker (BRKR-US) and Waters (WAT-US), for the dispersion of academic-exposed instrument makers' outcomes through the 2025 to 2026 US funding disruption; Jenoptik (JEN-DE) and Vaisala (VAIAS-FI), European precision-instrument peers whose earnings moved on semiconductor and data-centre demand rather than academic demand; Spectris (private since 2025), for a profit-improvement precedent; Fugro and other marine survey operators, for the dispersion of timing of government-funded offshore scientific expeditions comparable to Geotek's coring. Operating conditions: an order recovery that is partly deferred-order release (customers asked for delayed shipments, interim results 23 September 2026) and partly China administrative catch-up, against a US academic market still described as weak by 75% of laboratory managers (Jefferies survey, 2025 to 2026); a Q4 2026 shipment concentration that management called greater than usual; sterling moves beyond a 12-month economic hedge horizon; UK wage inflation of 4% to 9% in engineering (Jefferies, 2023 to 2026); a US FY2027 appropriations cycle with proposed research cuts; the timing of government-funded gas-hydrate coring expeditions (Judges: next expedition no earlier than 2028, after earlier expectations of 2026 and early 2027); private-market acquisition multiples for UK niche instrument businesses with private equity active above £1m of EBITDA; a thin sell-side (three brokers after Jefferies dropped coverage in April 2026). Inputs: The drivers and forecast differences to test: (1) the conversion of a 17.3-week order book (June 2026) and recovering intake into H2 2026 and FY2027 revenue, and how much of a Q4-weighted year typically slips across the year-end at instrument makers; (2) the incremental operating margin on recovering volume at a company with staff costs of 33% of revenue and raw materials of 31%, against what the sell-side embeds (FactSet consensus EBIT of £22.0m on revenue of £128m for FY2027 and £23.8m on £138m for FY2028, 22 October 2026); (3) the recurrence of the UK Patent Box tax benefit after an adjusted rate of 18% in H1 2026 against 21% in FY2024; (4) the timing and size of the next Geotek coring expedition (Zeus sensitivity of about 50p of EPS per expedition, September 2025) and the drivers of the customer's schedule; (5) the probability, timing and size of a first acquisition under the new deal team and the entry multiples now achievable; (6) sterling: the lag and extent of repricing of dollar and euro price lists at UK instrument exporters after a sustained sterling move; (7) a second leg of US academic funding cuts; (8) further impairments or losses at Fire Testing Technology, Armfield and Scientifica. For each: how diffuse or concentrated the drivers are, and the historical dispersion of outcomes when forecasts rested on order conversion and a single lumpy contract. Focus: How wide the range of 12-to-18-month EPS outcomes has been for small, fixed-cost, academically exposed instrument makers at the point where orders had turned but revenue had not, how skewed those outcomes were, and how often consensus that excluded lumpy contracts and acquisitions was subsequently beaten or missed; and specifically for Judges, how its own January trading updates after Q4-weighted years compared with the September guidance that preceded them.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Halma (HLMA-GB), which raised FY2026/27 margin guidance by about 100 basis points at its September 2026 trading update and reports half-year results on 19 November 2026; Renishaw (RSW-GB), whose FY2027 consensus EPS rose about 15% after its October 2026 first-quarter update; Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB), reporting interim results in November 2026 after a 44% order rise; SDI Group (SDI-GB), reporting interims in December 2026; Bruker (BRKR-US), for US academic demand commentary at Q3 and Q4 2026 results; Jenoptik (JEN-DE) and Vaisala (VAIAS-FI), for the pattern of guidance raises in the sector; Spectris, Diploma (DPLM-GB) and discoverIE (DSCV-GB), for UK acquisitive industrials' guidance behaviour; the three brokers covering Judges (Zeus and Shore Capital, both house or corporate brokers without formal ratings, and Investec at Buy) and Jefferies, which dropped coverage at Underperform in April 2026. Operating conditions: a company that rebased FY2026 adjusted EPS guidance in January 2026 to 200p to 250p from about 300p consensus, assuming no US recovery and no coring, and has since said it is trading in line with consensus of about 200p (July and September 2026); no FY2027 guidance until the FY2026 results on 24 March 2027; a new chief executive since February 2026 and a CFO who has resigned effective September 2027; a January 2027 pre-close trading update and a July 2027 trading update as the dated events; US FY2027 appropriations and NIH grant flow; Chinese tax-exemption resolution; sterling; the H1 2027 comparison against H1 2026 revenue of £55.7m (−21%) and adjusted EPS of 39.0p (−72%). Inputs: The dynamics to test: (1) the company's guidance record: three consecutive downgrades (November 2024, July 2025, January 2026), delivery against each rebased range, and whether the founder-era pattern of conservative guidance followed by beats has survived the change of chief executive; (2) where consensus sits against the company's run-rate: FactSet FY2026 median revenue £121m, EBIT £20.3m and EPS 199p with a broker range of 199p to 206p, and the H2 2026 margin brokers assume (Shore Capital: over 20%, 23 September 2026) against the revenue needed to deliver it; (3) the shape of a first FY2027 guidance under the new chief executive, and how new management teams at UK small caps have framed first guidance after a downturn; (4) what the January 2027 trading update will have to say about full-year order intake, order-book weeks and Q4 shipments for consensus to be held, raised or cut; (5) how the FY2027 consensus EPS (FactSet median 219p, mean 224p, range 212p to 243p) and the revision breadth (EPS −33% over twelve months, 0% over one month; buy share 100% across three brokers) are likely to move after each of the January 2027, March 2027, July 2027 and September 2027 events; (6) read-across from peers' latest results on US academic demand, China, semiconductor demand and sterling; (7) secondarily, how the 30% return on total invested capital ambition and any new mid-term framework from the new chief executive and incoming CFO might be set. Focus: Whether the January 2027 trading update is more likely to confirm FY2026 at about consensus with a clean Q4, or to report deferred shipments, and what each outcome would do to FY2027 consensus and to the three remaining brokers' estimates; and whether the FY2027 guidance set in March 2027 is more likely to bracket, sit above or sit below the current consensus given the company's stated assumptions and the new management's incentives.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Halma (HLMA-GB), Renishaw (RSW-GB), Oxford Instruments (OXIG-GB), SDI Group (SDI-GB), Spectris, Bruker (BRKR-US), Jenoptik (JEN-DE), Vaisala (VAIAS-FI), Diploma (DPLM-GB), discoverIE (DSCV-GB), Lifco (LIFCO-B-SE), Indutrade (INDT-SE), AddLife (ADDT-SE): for how the sell-side and investor commentary treat operating leverage at the turn, academic-funding exposure, serial-acquirer restarts after pauses, dividend-versus-deleveraging tension, FX transaction exposure at UK exporters and lumpy contract timing; the UK small-cap investor community (fund managers, AIM-focused commentators, Twitter/X and forum commentary on JDG). Operating conditions: a share price down about 40% over twelve months to 3,450p (21 October 2026), a 24-month-forward P/E of about 14.5x at the bottom of its five-year range, average daily trading of about $1m, three remaining brokers (Zeus, Shore Capital, Investec) and Jefferies' dropped coverage; the January 2027 and March 2027 results events; a sector in which semiconductor-exposed peers have re-rated and academically exposed names have de-rated; an open debate among brokers (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, 2026) about whether US academic instrument demand has reached its trough. Inputs: The dynamics to test for articulation and awareness: (1) the fixed-cost operating leverage of Judges' cost base (staff 33%, materials 31% of FY2025 revenue) and what it implies for incremental margins in a recovery; (2) the conversion of the 17.3-week order book and the Q3 2026 intake recovery into H2 2026 and H1 2027 revenue, and the Q4 2026 shipment risk; (3) the UK Patent Box's effect on the tax rate (18% in H1 2026) and whether any part is a one-off; (4) the Geotek coring expedition's slippage to no earlier than 2028 and its EPS sensitivity; (5) the tension between a progressive dividend raised 10% at 1.1x cover and the restart of acquisitions at 2.1x leverage; (6) the absence of acquisitions since 2024 and the new deal team; (7) the loss-making subsidiaries (Fire Testing Technology, Armfield, Scientifica) and the central team's intervention; (8) sterling transaction exposure with about 50% of revenue in dollars and 33% in euros against a sterling cost base and 12-month hedges; (9) the leadership transition (founder to Chair, new chief executive, CFO leaving September 2027, new auditor after Financial Reporting Council restatements); (10) structural margin changes from higher central costs, UK taxes and R&D since FY2023; (11) the comparison of H1 2027 against H1 2026 (revenue −21%, EPS −72%) as a narrative catalyst; (12) the possibility of a bid from private-equity or strategic buyers active in UK scientific instruments. Focus: Which of these dynamics the three remaining brokers and the investor commentary already discuss (and since when, and with what view), which are mentioned by only one source, and which are not discussed at all, with particular attention to whether sterling transaction exposure, the dividend-versus-acquisition tension, the recurrence of the Patent Box benefit and the arithmetic of the H1 2027 comparison appear anywhere in the commentary, so that the next Analyst can judge what a January 2027 update or an H1 2027 result would reveal that the market does not already expect.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what does the record of its January pre-close trading updates from 2014 to 2026 show about how fully a heavily Q4-weighted year converted the June order book into December revenue, how often shipments slipped across the year-end and by roughly how much, and how the January update compared with the guidance or consensus in place at the preceding September interims; and what do comparable UK instrument makers (Renishaw, Oxford Instruments, SDI Group, Spectris) show about the share of a Q4-weighted order book that typically ships before the year-end when customers have asked for deferred delivery?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what is known from HMRC statistics, tax advisers and the disclosures of UK-listed engineering and instrument companies (Renishaw, Oxford Instruments, Halma, Spectris, SDI Group, Judges itself) about how the effective tax rate behaves in the first years of an enhanced UK Patent Box claim, specifically whether first-year claims typically include prior-period catch-up relief, how the benefit evolves as qualifying patents age and new ones are added, and what adjusted effective tax rates comparable UK instrument exporters with Patent Box claims have sustained over three to five years; and what Judges' own disclosures (adjusted tax rate 21% in FY2024, 18% in H1 2026; Shore Capital's reference to a larger Patent Box benefit in H2 2026) say about the recurring level?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB) and its Geotek subsidiary, who is the customer for the next major gas-hydrate pressure-coring expedition that the company says will take place no earlier than 2028, what is the funding and approval status of that programme (for example the Japanese MH21 or other national gas-hydrate research programmes), how long the gap between contract signature and expedition has been for Geotek's previous expeditions (including the Japan expedition in Q1 2025), what determines vessel availability and scheduling for such expeditions in the current offshore survey market, and how often comparable government-funded marine scientific expeditions have slipped more than once before taking place?

Request: OWN-4

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB) and UK scientific-instrument exporters with sterling cost bases and dollar and euro revenue (Renishaw, Oxford Instruments, Spectris, Halma's UK-manufactured businesses, SDI Group), what happened to operating margins in past episodes of sustained sterling appreciation (2013 to 2015 and 2021 to 2022) once 12-to-24-month hedges rolled off, how quickly and by how much dollar and euro price lists were raised in response, and whether academic customers accepted those rises; and what the companies disclosed about the lag between a sterling move and its full effect on reported operating profit, by channel (direct sales, distributors, OEM supply contracts)?

Request: OWN-5

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB) and other small listed serial acquirers that paused acquisitions for 18 months or more and then completed a first deal (SDI Group, AddLife, Indutrade, discoverIE, Judges itself in 2016 and 2020 to 2021, and UK AIM-listed consolidators), how did sell-side estimates, the number of covering brokers, the valuation multiple and investor commentary respond in the six to twelve months after the first post-pause acquisition and after the first reported half of organic growth, and what distinguished the cases where the multiple re-rated from those where it did not?

Request: OWN-6

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what is the current evidence on the two external demand drivers for its 2027 revenue: (a) the US FY2027 appropriations outcome for the National Institutes of Health and National Science Foundation, the pace of NIH and NSF grant obligations and outlays in the fourth quarter of 2026, and the historical lag between restored grant flow and laboratory capital-equipment orders at academic-exposed instrument makers (Bruker, Oxford Instruments, Thermo Fisher, Waters, Agilent); and (b) the resolution of China's R&D tax-exemption procedures for imported scientific instruments, as reported by JEOL, Bruker, Oxford Instruments, Shimadzu and Chinese distributors, and how far deferred 2026 orders have begun shipping, against the longer-run effect of China's domestic-substitution rules on foreign instrument makers' China revenue?

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 11,842 words · written 25 October 2026, 07:51 to 08:12 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB): third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and the gap to guidance, consensus and narrative

Reading notes for the Consumer

  1. Basis and anchors. Every figure is on the company's adjusted basis, as in the second set. FY25: revenue £145.8m, adjusted operating profit £28.0m, adjusted EPS 275.3p. H1 26: revenue £55.7m, adjusted operating profit £4.8m (8.6%), adjusted EPS 39.0p, adjusted net debt £45.3m (2.1x), order book 17.3 weeks. Consensus (FactSet median, three brokers; two for FY28): FY26 revenue £121m, EBIT £20.3m, EPS 199p; FY27 £128m, £22.0m, 219p (mean 224p; range 212p Shore to 243p Investec); FY28 £138m, £23.8m, 248p. Price 3,425p to 3,450p; 24-month-forward P/E 14.5x on consensus, 12.3x on the model's EPS.
  2. The model summary flags no material gap between the second set's cross-check and the model's figures: EPS agrees to within 1p in every year (1.93/1.94, 2.34/2.34, 2.83/2.82, 3.94/3.93, 3.94/3.92) and free cash flow to within £0.3m (the FY26 difference of 10.1 against 9.8 is cash-tax or working-capital timing inside the model; the rest is rounding of shares and tax). There is nothing to explain and no input is wrong on that account. One difference the summary does not flag should go to the Financial Analyst: year-end net debt. The model shows £34.9m, £36.9m, £44.8m, £51.1m and £57.6m against the second set's £40.9m, £42.3m, £49.7m, £55.3m and £61.1m, a constant gap of about £5.5m to £6m in every year. A constant gap is an opening-balance or definition difference (the model appears to start from a December 2025 net debt about £6m below the company's adjusted £42.6m), not a forecast difference. It does not touch EPS, because interest is an input, but it flatters the model's leverage by about 0.2x (1.5x against 1.7x at December 2026). The memo should quote the company's adjusted definition until the two are reconciled.
  3. One forecast input changes as a result of these answers: the FY26 tax rate, from 17% to 18%, because the chief financial officer guided an 18% adjusted rate for the full year on the 23 September 2026 results call (reported in the Patent Box AlphaSense report). The effect is about 2p off FY26 EPS, to about 191p; FY27 growth becomes about 22% instead of 21%; and the non-recurring element inside FY26's tax rate shrinks to about 2 to 3 points (5p to 7p of EPS, the difference between 18% and the 20% to 21% recurring rate), not the 8p to 15p the second set assumed or the 20p to 30p the first set assumed. The change is recorded in inputs-changed.md. Where I quote "our" FY26 EPS below, I use 191p.
  4. How to read the AlphaSense reports. The four template reports are built mainly from Zeus and Shore Capital notes, the two corporate brokers, so their recurring "other side of the story" (the compounding model is intact, near-term noise does not matter) is the house-broker narrative, not independent evidence; I treat it as the narrative to be tested in I-03. Where the reports add independent evidence (HMRC and peer tax rates, NIH outlays, Chinese customs clearance, vessel markets, Fugro, Bruker and Oxford Instruments order books), it is consistent with the two sets and slightly more negative on the external drivers than the second set's inputs.
  5. Dates used throughout. About 22 January 2027: FY26 pre-close trading update. 24 March 2027: FY26 results and first FY27 guidance. May or June 2027: AGM statement. July 2027: H1 trading update. September 2027: interim results; 23 September 2027: the CFO leaves. January 2028: FY27 pre-close. Peers: Halma interims 19 November 2026; Oxford Instruments interims November 2026; Bruker third quarter early November 2026 and fourth quarter February 2027; SDI Group interims December 2026; JEOL second quarter November 2026; US FY2027 appropriations in the December 2026 lame-duck session.

The third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

❌ D477 questions
D-0166D-0258D-0338D-0442D-0540D-0646D-0758

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The predictability of Judges' financial forecasts over the next 18 months is low-to-moderate, and it is low for an unusual reason: not because the mechanisms are uncertain but because the two numbers that will be printed first depend on things no outsider can observe. The sets are congruent on the mechanisms (D-01) and the mechanisms rest on demonstrated strengths (D-02): orders already booked convert to revenue with a four-month lag, and a fixed cost base of £63m to £66m returns 40p to 65p of profit for every £1 regained. But the drivers of change are concentrated (D-03): one quarter's shipments decide FY26; one half-year and one country's customs procedure decide FY27's growth; and two unproven engines, a deal team without its founder and a coring customer without a contract, decide whether FY28 is 259p or 283p and whether FY29 is 292p or 394p. The cone is wide (D-04): FY27 EPS 185p to 285p, two to three times a Halma's, skewed down for the next print and up thereafter against consensus and against the multiple. Disclosure (D-05) gives the right two indicators but only twice in six months, and the trace from management's words to EPS (D-06) is clear in direction and unquantified in every link that matters (the deferred share of the book, the cost base, the exposures, currency). The share price and the multiple are company-driven (D-07), so the forecasts and the stock will be judged at the same dated events, which is the one feature that makes the case tractable: 22 January 2027 resolves most of FY26 and a third of FY27; 24 March 2027 sets the guide and the tax split; July 2027 shows the first half of growth.

For the memo's topic #9: the case is simple (one driver for 18 months), its fragilities are few and named (Q4 slippage, China clearance, US appropriations, sterling above 1.40), its risks are trackable at dated events and untrackable between them, and their materiality to the share price is high because the multiple sits at its own floor and responds to company news alone. Sector precedents (Judges 2014 to 2016; Renishaw 2025 to 2026; Oxford Instruments 2015 to 2017) say volume-led recoveries from this setup are usually delivered within two years; Judges' own precedent also says that Q4-weighted years have missed their September expectations in two of the last two attempts. The forecasts are more predictable in direction than any in the company's last three years, and less predictable in level than any Halma or Diploma forecast the Consumer will have modelled.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets?

C66

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement. The answers are highly congruent in direction and mechanism, and congruent in most figures; the differences between the sets are refinements that the second set documented and reconciled, not contradictions. Five points jar, and two of them matter for the memo: (i) the sets, and the evidence, do not agree on how much of the 2025 to 2026 order collapse was external (public research funding) and how much was company-specific (product gaps and share loss at three or four businesses, European OEM softness), and the recovery arithmetic assumes the lost volume returns, which is true only of the external part; (ii) the second set's base case for FY28 to FY30 contains an acquisition restart and a coring expedition that the first set said should not be assumed until evidenced, and the second set's own probabilities for coring (40% on 2028) are inconsistent with its placement of the expedition in FY29. The other three are small: the first set's leverage path is internally inconsistent (1.2x to 1.4x at end-FY27 in C-76 against 1.5x to 1.7x in C-19; the second set's 1.5x resolves it); the size of the FY26 Patent Box one-off (20p to 30p in C-54, 8p to 15p in P-08, about 5p to 7p on the CFO's guided 18%); and the model's year-end net debt sits £6m below the second set's in every year, a definitional gap (reading note 2).

Where the sets support each other: the spine of the case.

  • Trough and turn. FY26 is the trough, orders turned in Q3 2026 (−18%, flat, about +15%), revenue turns in Q4 2026 to H1 2027, earnings trough in FY26. Identical in both sets and in the model (revenue −14.1%, +8.1%, +11.3%).
  • Mechanism. Fixed-cost operating leverage is the dominant mechanic in both. The first set's 60% to 65% is the contribution margin on a marginal shipment; the second set's 40% to 45% is what reaches the operating line after semi-variable costs return and wage inflation nets against price. P-01 reconciles the two and the half-year decrementals (H1 26: −65%) support both readings.
  • The critique of consensus. Both find FY26 consensus internally inconsistent (£121m of revenue cannot produce £20.3m of EBIT without a 24% H2 margin) and both lean below consensus on FY26 profit and above on FY27 to FY28, for the same arithmetic reason.
  • The constraints. Both treat the dividend-and-leverage balancing loop as the brake on acquisitions, the political demand driver as the main swing factor, sterling as the largest unquantified sensitivity (first set "high single-digit £m" per 10%; second set about £7m) and management as an honest tracker deserving no execution premium.
  • The dated tests. Both name 22 January 2027 and 24 March 2027 as the resolution events and July 2027 as the first reported half of growth.
  • The model. No flagged gaps. The FY26 segment build (Vacuum £63.0m, Materials Sciences £62.2m) matches FactSet's segment consensus, which sums to £125.3m against the £121m group median; the second set used the internally consistent one of the two.

Where they jar, and what each jar means.

  1. External against company-specific decline. The first set attributes the FY24 to FY26 decline almost wholly to external forces (US funding, the China procedure, coring timing, offshore wind) and allows one point a year of share-loss drag. The AlphaSense evidence weakens that attribution at the margin. Bruker's book-to-bill stayed above 1.0 for four consecutive quarters through the same US academic disruption (Rothschild & Co Redburn, 5 August 2026). Oxford Instruments' group orders rose 44% in the half to September 2026 with US academic the only exception (Deutsche Bank, 13 October 2026). Judges' European orders fell 20% in H1 26 on "OEM and end-customer softness", which no funding story explains. Jefferies' January 2026 note referred to "individual business issues" the company did not name. My reading: of the roughly £17m of organic volume lost between FY25 and FY26 excluding coring, perhaps £3m to £5m is company-specific (share loss at Armfield, Fire Testing Technology's calorimeter delay, Scientifica's product exit, European OEM weakness) and will not return with funding. The second set's volume path (+4 points FY27, +3 FY28) already embeds a point a year of share loss and a flat US, so the base case does not rely on recovering that portion; but the Consumer should not read the "mechanical rebound" as a full retracement. FY27 revenue of £135m is £11m below FY25 and only £1m above FY23; it is a partial recovery consistent with a permanent loss of £3m to £5m of volume. This jar changes the interpretation, not the numbers.

  2. Acquisitions and coring inside the base case. The first set: exclude until a deal completes; resumption unlikely before H2 2027. The second set: £5m in H2 2027, £12m in FY28, £15m a year thereafter, and one coring expedition in FY29. The second set separates the contributions (organic EPS 229p, 259p, 292p, 320p against 234p, 283p, 394p, 394p), which is the right treatment and the one the memo should use. The jar is in the coring probabilities: P-10 assigns 40% to 2028, 30% to 2029, 15% to 2030 and 15% to none, yet places the expedition in FY29 "as the most probable single scenario"; on those probabilities 2028 is the mode. The Geotek AlphaSense report resolves the inconsistency in favour of FY29 or later: no contract has been signed; Geotek's past gap between signature and expedition has been five to twelve-plus months (Japan: signed August 2024, sailed January 2025; the 2023 expedition was on a 2022 contract); the schedule has already slipped three times (late 2024, early 2027, "no earlier than 2028"); specialised geotechnical vessels are being absorbed by oil, gas and offshore wind at record day rates; and government-funded marine programmes "almost always slip more than once". I restate the probabilities as 2028 about 25%, 2029 about 35%, 2030 about 20% and none inside the window about 20%. That makes FY29 the mode and leaves the input where the second set put it. The memo should describe FY29's £4.8m of coring EBIT and 55p of EPS as a roughly one-in-three single-year event and anchor on the organic FY29 figure of 292p.

  3. The leverage path. C-76 says 1.2x to 1.4x by end-FY27 absent acquisitions; C-19 says 1.5x to 1.7x. The second set's 1.5x (with a £5m deal) is built from a cash-flow statement and should be used. The model's £36.9m at December 2027 (1.3x) is £5.4m below the second set's £42.3m for the reason in reading note 2.

  4. The FY26 tax one-off. First set 20p to 30p; second set 8p to 15p. The Patent Box report finds peers' recurring adjusted rates of 21% to 24%, Judges' likely recurring range of 19% to 21%, and quotes the CFO guiding 18% for the full year. The non-recurring element is therefore 18% against 20% to 21%, two to three points, about 5p to 7p. Both sets overstated it, the first set materially. The FY27 growth hurdle from tax is about 6p, not 9p to 13p. I change the FY26 input to 18%.

  5. FY27 guidance expectations. The first set expects a March 2027 range "bracketing about 200p to 230p"; the second set expects "about 210p to 240p" and its own base is 234p, at the top of that range. Not a contradiction, but the Consumer should note that our base case sits where management's first guidance is likely to be capped. That is the shape of the "guide low, beat later" setup the fourth set relies on.

Congruence with the external evidence. Where the AlphaSense reports add independent evidence it is congruent with the sets and slightly more negative on the external drivers: NIH cash outlays fell 9% year on year in September 2026 and about 15% in the quarter (Jefferies, 19 October 2026); Shimadzu's president expects China to recover "very slowly" over three years (June 2026); Chinese distributors report customs clearance of more than six months and some cancellations (Morgan Stanley on JEOL, 18 October 2026). That argues for weighting the second set's downside tail a little more heavily (D-04), not for changing the base case.

For the Consumer. The case can be written from the two sets without further reconciliation beyond the points above. The one substantive thing to carry forward is that the recovery is a partial retracement from a trough whose depth was partly self-inflicted, and that the out-year EPS has two layers, organic and acquisitions-plus-coring, with the second layer resting on an unproven deal team and an unsigned contract.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has?

C58

Question asked To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

Judgement. Over the next 18 months the expected change rests almost entirely on strengths the business has demonstrated: converting an order book into shipments, a fixed cost base that returns 40p to 65p of profit for every £1 of revenue regained, cost discipline at the margin, nominal price pass-through, a Patent Box claim already in the tax rate, and a dividend culture. The record of delivery against those mechanics is good even though the record against expectations is poor: three downgrades in three years, but each rebased figure was then delivered (FY24 283p inside 270p to 300p; FY25 275p, 3% below its range; H1 26 at the midpoint of 38p to 42p), cash conversion stayed between 90% and 122% throughout, and the 2014 to 2016 recovery shows a volume-led margin rebuild (17.3% in 2015 from a sub-8-week book) without a cost programme. From FY28 the thesis adds two strengths the business would need to acquire or re-prove: deal sourcing without the founder (zero deals in 21 months; the team in place since July 2025) and the operational repair of three businesses it let drift for a decade. Those two account for about a quarter to a third of the FY26 to FY28 EPS uplift (acquisitions 24p of the 90p; underperformer repair about half of a further £1.6m of cost and repair) and for about half of the increase above the organic path by FY30. The commercial pivot and software capability, which the business lacks, are not relied on materially inside the window.

What is demonstrated, with the evidence.

  • Order conversion to revenue with about a four-month lag. 2014 to 2016: book 7.8 weeks in June 2014 to 14.7 weeks in December 2016, 2015 intake +12.7%, margin back to 17.3% within a year. December 2024: "a very healthy November and December" converting the book after the November reset (Jefferies, 20 January 2025). Counter: the January 2026 outturn was 6% below the September 2025 consensus and November 2024 saw about £4m (3% of revenue) slip. Conversion is demonstrated, but not every year.
  • Operating leverage both ways. H1 26 decremental about 65%; FY23 to FY24 steeper still; 2021 to 2023 incremental 35% to 40%. The 42% assumed for FY27 is inside the observed range.
  • Cost action. £1.8m to £2.0m taken out in H1 26 at underperformers with about half reinvested: modest but delivered, and management's refusal to run a Renishaw-style programme is consistent with the second set's fixed block rising to £66m in FY27.
  • Pricing. About 85% of businesses pass costs through (management); prices held through the 2025 to 2026 collapse; demonstrated as cost recovery only, which is exactly what the second set assumes (2.5% a year, nil margin effect).
  • Patent Box. 18% in H1 26 and guided for the year; two subsidiaries used it in FY24 and FY25 with more to follow; peers sustain 21% to 24%. Demonstrated, with the recurring level (20% to 21%) in the inputs.
  • Balance-sheet management and the dividend. Facility to 2030 with covenants unchanged; cash conversion of 90% in the trough half; 20 years of dividend rises of at least 10%. Demonstrated.

What is partly demonstrated.

  • Margin recovery without a cost programme. The question the plausibility report was asked. Judges' own 2015 recovery did it on volume; 2020 to 2022 did it on volume plus acquisitions plus coring; Renishaw's 15.7% to 18.7% needed £20m of cuts plus volume; Oxford Instruments needed a 20% headcount cut at one site and a commercial pivot. The second set does not assume a return to near peak: 18.0% in FY28 against 25.6% in FY23, 19.8% by FY30 without coring, and a return to within about one point of FY25's 19.2% by FY28. That is the 2015 precedent, not the 2023 one, and it is plausible on demonstrated strength.
  • Holding the fixed block. The centre added about £2m of cost in FY25 (a 75% rise) and Jefferies (10 September 2026) warned that "additional costs might be required to support long-term growth". The second set's fixed block rises about £2m a year, which allows for inflation but not for a second round of central build. Half demonstrated.

What would need to be acquired or proven.

  • Deal sourcing without the founder at 4x to 6x EBIT while private equity pays 10x to 12x EV/EBITDA for UK industrial assets above £1m of EBITDA (Morgan Stanley, 28 July 2026) and Chinese buyers compete. The SDI Group chief executive (expert call, 24 September 2026) says private-equity competition has surged. The deal team's first completion is the test; the second set puts 65% to 70% on H2 2027. I put 55% to 60%: the nine-to-twelve-month restart after a trough is the historical pattern (2016, 2020 to 2021), but the founder sourced those deals, and the dividend leaves only £3m to £5m a year of retained cash, so any deal above £5m needs debt while leverage is still about 1.5x.
  • Repair of Armfield, Fire Testing Technology and Scientifica. Judges has fixed businesses before (GDS EBIT 3.5x after purchase) and has failed to (Armfield's share from 40% to 15% over a decade; FTT's loss widening to £1.21m in FY25 while R&D was cut; the new calorimeter a year late). A Portfolio Chief Executive and new managing directors are in place; no numbers are disclosed. The second set credits half, which matches the Wisdom Library's turnaround base rate.
  • Commercial-market sales reach and software. Lacking (direct sales only in the US; no group platform). Relied on only for two to three points of mix drift a year; immaterial to the window.
  • Real pricing in academic channels. Not a strength and not assumed.

Plausibility by horizon.

  • H2 26 (revenue £69.5m, margin 20.1%, tax 18%): rests on converting a 17.3-week book plus Q3 intake. A demonstrated mechanic with a one-sided timing risk (D-04).
  • FY27 (organic +6.5%, EBIT £22.6m): rests on the H1 26 base effect (−21%), the China refill and 42% drop-through. All demonstrated mechanics; none requires new capability. Sterling is the strength-independent risk.
  • FY28 (EBIT £27.1m, of which £2.0m acquired and about £0.8m from repair): about 30% of the FY27 to FY28 EBIT increase needs capabilities not yet proven.
  • FY29 to FY30: about half of the EPS above the organic path needs the deal engine and the coring customer.

Where I differ from the second set. It describes the FY27 to FY28 lean above consensus as resting "on arithmetic, not on management". I agree, and add that the arithmetic has a precondition that is itself a demonstrated weakness: twice in two years the business failed to convert a Q4-weighted year to its September expectations. The strength that converts books into revenue is real; the strength that forecasts the conversion is not. That is why the FY26 lean below consensus is right, and why the FY27 lean should be removed rather than reduced if the January update shows more than about £5m of slippage.

D-03

How diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years' financials?

❌ D38

Question asked In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

Judgement. The drivers are concentrated, and more concentrated over the next 18 months than over four years. Over 18 months (FY26 to FY27) a single driver, the conversion of recovered order intake into shipments, carries essentially all of the change in operating profit; inside that driver, two items carry most of FY27's organic revenue increase: the H1 26 base effect and a China refill of £4m to £6m of shipments deferred from 2026, which together are roughly three-quarters of the £8.1m organic increase. Over four years, two drivers, organic volume on a fixed cost base and the acquisition restart, carry over 80% of the profit change (P-09: 54% volume and price net of inflation, 24% acquisitions, 19% cost actions and repair for FY26 to FY28), and a third, a single coring contract with a single state customer, moves whole years. Against history the concentration is new: the FY21 to FY23 expansion had three engines running together (organic about 7 points, acquisitions about 12 points, coring), so any one could disappoint without breaking the year; the FY27 to FY28 forecast has one engine and the FY29 to FY30 forecast adds two that are binary. The portfolio is diffuse (25 businesses, thousands of customers, none material), but diffuseness of the asset base does not make the drivers of change diffuse: about two-thirds of revenue follows public research budgets that move together, and the recovery assumption is that they stop falling at the same time.

The concentration, quantified.

FY26 to FY27 (EBIT £18.8m to £22.6m, +£3.8m): organic volume +£5.3m at 65% contribution; returning semi-variable cost −£1.0m; inflation −£2.8m; price +£3.3m; cost actions and repair +£1.3m; reinvestment −£0.5m; acquisitions +£0.4m. Price and inflation net to +£0.5m and the cost items to +£0.8m, so volume is roughly the whole net change. Within the volume, H1 27 revenue is +£7.2m (of which the China refill is £4m to £6m and the rest is the base effect against H1 26's −21%) and H2 27 is +£2.9m. FY27 is therefore a story of one half-year and one country's customs procedure. The second set's phasing confirms it: H1 27 EBIT of £8.6m against £4.8m is the whole year's increase; H2 27 is flat on H2 26 (£14.0m on £14.0m).

FY26 to FY28 (+£8.3m EBIT, +90p EPS): as P-09, two drivers above 80%.

FY28 to FY30 (+£10.6m EBIT): acquisitions £5.2m (49%), volume £4.0m (38%), price net of inflation £1.1m, repair £1.0m; FY29 alone carries +£4.8m of coring that FY30 loses again.

What each driver itself depends on.

  • Volume: public research budgets (about two-thirds of revenue; the US a quarter of revenue, half of that academic; China 10%), where the swing variable is political (FY2027 appropriations; NIH outlays −15% year on year in the September 2026 quarter; a proposed 55% NSF cut) plus one administrative event (China's once-in-four-years tax-exemption procedure). The commercial third (semiconductor, battery) grows but is sub-scale; it diversifies the base, not the change.
  • Margin: one cost base, 47% to 50% fixed, so the margin driver is the volume driver with a multiplier. There is no independent margin lever: price is cost recovery and cost actions net to about zero after inflation.
  • Acquisitions: one small team, one balance-sheet gate (leverage at or below about 1.5x) and one dividend policy that pre-empts the cash.
  • Coring: one customer (a state gas-hydrate programme, probably Japan's MH21-S or India's NGHP), one contract not yet signed, one vessel market.
  • Tax: one UK regime at two, rising to more, subsidiaries.
  • Currency: one rate pair against a twelve-month hedge.

Against the last four to six years. FY21 to FY23: revenue +19.5% a year from three engines; margin 21.9% to 25.6%; had organic stalled, acquisitions would still have delivered double-digit EPS growth. FY24 to FY26: all three went into reverse together, which is why an £11m revenue fall produced a £16m profit fall. FY27 to FY30 (base case): organic 5 to 6 points, acquisitions 5 points from FY28, coring once. The mix is less diversified than FY21 to FY23 because the acquisition engine runs at half speed and the coring is a single event. History therefore overstates the predictability of the forecasts: investors who remember a three-engine compounder will be judging a one-engine cyclical recovery for the next 18 months.

Where the drivers are more diffuse than they look. Within the organic driver, eight of 25 businesses grew in H1 26; Vacuum (half of revenue, 26% to 27% EBITDA margin) is steadier than Materials Sciences; the US is a quarter of revenue and Europe (28%) and the Rest of World (30%) have different drivers. A second US leg down (US academic orders −10% to −15%) costs about two points of group organic growth, not ten. That partial diffuseness is why the downside is bounded (FY27 EPS floor of about 185p to 190p in the second set's downside case) even though the upside drivers are concentrated.

For the memo's topic #9. State the forecast as one driver for 18 months (order conversion, concentrated in H1 2027 and in China), two drivers for four years (volume and the deal restart), and one binary event (coring). Concentration makes the case simple to monitor and fragile to a single disappointment.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

❌ D42

Judgement. The cone is wide and its skew depends on the reference point. Against our base case, the FY27 EPS cone is roughly 185p to 285p (about ±25%), two to three times the width that applies to Halma or Diploma, and roughly symmetric. Against FY26 the distribution is skewed down: mode about 191p, probability-weighted mean about 188p, a one-in-three chance of 165p to 185p. Against consensus (219p for FY27) and against the price (14.5x, a five-year-low multiple) the distribution is skewed up, because the downside cases are mostly timing (revenue moving between years) while the upside cases are level (revenue, margin and acquisitions that consensus does not contain). Predictability is therefore low for the next three months (the Q4 shipment outcome is unobservable before 22 January 2027), moderate for the direction of FY27 (orders already booked write most of H1 27) and low for its magnitude (sterling, US appropriations, the China refill). The case is predictable in shape, a trough, a mechanical rebound and a slow restart of compounding, and unpredictable in the two numbers the market will trade on first: FY26 EPS and the FY27 guide.

The cone by horizon, with my probabilities (the second set's in brackets where I differ).

  • FY26 adjusted EPS (base about 191p after the tax change). 185p to 200p, "in line": 50%. 165p to 184p: 32% (30%). Above 200p: 18% (20%). The downside is one-sided because every £1m of Q4 revenue that slips costs about 8p and nothing inside the year offsets it. The January-update record is one clean Q4-weighted conversion (December 2024) and two shortfalls against September (November 2024, about £4m; January 2026, 6% below consensus). Management has pre-signalled both a "greater than usual" Q4 and customers' requests for deferral, which is the setup that preceded the 2024 and 2025 shortfalls. Probability-weighted FY26 EPS about 188p, 5% to 6% below consensus.
  • FY27 adjusted EPS (base 234p). Below 200p: 30% (25%). 200p to 260p: 50%. Above 260p: 20% (25%). I shade the downside up because NIH cash outlays fell 9% in September 2026 and about 15% in the quarter, the lag from restored grant flow to instrument orders is two to three quarters (so a December 2026 stopgap helps H2 27 at the earliest), and Chinese distributors describe clearance of more than six months with some cancellations. Probability-weighted FY27 EPS about 226p, still 3% above consensus and 7% above Shore. What sets the width: Q4 26 slippage carried into FY27 (+10p to +25p if it recovers, −10p to −20p if the orders were cancelled rather than deferred); the China refill at £2m (−15p) or £6m (+10p); US academic orders −10% to −15% (−20p to −30p); sterling at 1.42 for a year from mid-2027 (−25p to −40p in FY27, up to −80p in FY28); a first acquisition of £10m (+8p at half-year effect); a coring contract signature (narrative, not FY27 EPS).
  • FY28 adjusted EPS (base 283p, organic 259p). 210p to 340p; the acquisition pace is the swing inside the range; coring in 2028 (my 25%) adds about 55p above it. Width about ±30%.
