Why the reference point decides the skew. Relative to the base case the FY27 cone is roughly symmetric because the second set's base already sits above consensus and includes a £4m to £6m refill. Relative to consensus and the multiple the skew is favourable: consensus FY27 EBIT of £22.0m implies an incremental margin of about 24% on £7m of revenue, which requires the company to add about £3m of cost that neither its actions nor its statements support; the share trades at 14.5x on that consensus and 12.3x on the model's two-year-forward EPS. The driver-dispersion AlphaSense report calls the outcomes "negatively skewed due to valuation risks". I disagree on the stock and agree on the next print: the negative skew is in FY26, the positive skew is in the four prints that follow.

What narrows the cone, and when. The 22 January 2027 update resolves most of the FY26 cone and about a third of the FY27 cone (full-year intake, December book weeks, "in line" or not, commentary on deferred shipments). The 24 March 2027 results resolve the tax split, set the FY27 guide and may name a CFO and a first deal. The July 2027 update gives the first reported half of growth and the regional intake pattern, after which the FY27 cone should narrow to about ±10%. Until January the forecasts carry the weight of a mechanism, not of a delivery.

Predictability against peers. Halma: an EPS cone of about ±5% to 8%, guidance raised twice a year. Renishaw: a wide cone (operating gearing about 37% in growth years, currency) but order books of under three months, so surprises show within a quarter. Oxford Instruments: wider than Halma, narrower than Judges, with a 12-to-18-month hedge. SDI Group: comparable to Judges in width. Judges' cone is the widest of the set because its fixed block is the largest share of revenue, its disclosure the sparsest and its hedge the shortest.

Where I differ from the second set. Only in the FY27 downside weight (30% against 25%), and in insisting that the FY26 base case be quoted as a mode, not a mean: the memo should say "about 190p, with a one-in-three chance of 165p to 185p", not "193p".

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and the key drivers to monitor the case over about the next six months?

❌ D40

Question asked How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

Judgement. Disclosure over the next six months is sparse, binary and of the right kind, which makes it moderately unhelpful. The two indicators the company gives, organic order intake growth (including by region) and order-book weeks, are exactly the lead indicators the thesis turns on, but they arrive only twice in the period (the trading update of about 22 January 2027 and the results of 24 March 2027), the book-weeks measure includes shipments customers have asked to defer, and nothing is disclosed between events. Of the seven things that decide the forecasts in the period, the company's disclosure monitors three well (intake, book weeks, full-year EPS), two partially (tax; net debt and working capital) and two not at all (subsidiary-level repair; the acquisition pipeline). External data fill part of the gap. Compared with peers, Judges discloses less than Halma, Renishaw and Oxford Instruments (segment orders, currency sensitivities and, at Renishaw, quarterly updates) and about the same as SDI Group.

What is disclosed, and what each item tells the Consumer.

  • Organic order intake, year to date and by region (US, China and Hong Kong, Europe, UK, Rest of World). The best indicator: it moved from −12% to −1% between June and late September, and the January update will give the full year. The test is FY26 intake within 2% of FY25, which implies a book-to-bill above 1.0 and means most of FY27's revenue is already written. Weakness: a group aggregate of 25 businesses with no split between academic and commercial and no value-against-count detail.
  • Order-book weeks at the period end. 17.3 at June; 15 to 17 at December is consistent with the base case, below 14 is not. Weaknesses: weeks are measured against budgeted forward sales, so 17 weeks of a lower budget is fewer pounds; and the book includes orders whose customers asked for later shipment, so a high reading can signal deferral rather than demand.
  • Full-year adjusted EPS "in line" or otherwise, in January; H2 revenue, margin, tax rate, segment EBIT and the final dividend only in March.
  • Net debt and working capital at December (March). The test of the seasonal unwind: the second set expects adjusted net debt of about £41m; above £44m signals receivables stuck in deferred shipments.
  • The tax note (March): the only place the recurring Patent Box rate becomes visible.
  • Breadth: "eight of 25 businesses grew" was given for H1 26; if repeated, it is the best proxy for whether the recovery is broad.
  • Governance items with model relevance: a CFO successor; any acquisition or Geotek contract (by RNS, though the company has historically disclosed coring timing inside trading updates).

What is not disclosed, and matters.

  • Anything about Q4 shipping progress between September and January.
  • Subsidiary performance: nothing for Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, Scientifica or Geotek; Companies House filings arrive about nine months after year-end (FTT's FY25 loss of £1.21m became public in September 2026), so FY26 subsidiary accounts will not appear until the second half of 2027.
  • The acquisition pipeline: "healthy" is the only word; no count, size or multiple.
  • Price, volume and mix: never quantified.
  • The cost run-rate: the £2m of actions is stated once; the fixed block is not disclosed.
  • Currency sensitivity: none published (Oxford Instruments publishes about £0.6m per cent on GBP/USD).

External monitors that substitute, with dates. Monthly NIH outlays (Jefferies and Bernstein trackers; September −9%, quarter −15%). The FY2027 appropriations outcome in the December 2026 lame-duck session (a stopgap is the base case; the House countered a proposed 14% NIH cut with a flat budget). Bruker's US academic and China commentary (early November 2026 and February 2027). Oxford Instruments' academic order commentary (November 2026). JEOL's second quarter (November 2026) for whether Chinese clearance has begun after its first-quarter China sales fell 50% on the same procedure. SDI Group's December interims for UK small-cap read-across. Sterling against 1.38 to 1.40. Fugro's order intake for the vessel market that gates coring. These are leading or coincident for the external drivers and silent on Judges' own conversion.

Where I add to the first set. C-60 and C-73 called communication a "black box" with order-book weeks as the main KPI and sparse, binary events. I add two points: the book-weeks measure is softer than it looks because it includes requested deferrals (management said so in September 2026), and the intake measure has already done its work for FY27 (revenue follows intake by about four months), so the January intake figure is more informative for the FY27 guide than for the FY26 outturn.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into operational drivers, revenue, cost and margin lines, and EPS; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to EPS?

❌ D46

Question asked How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

Judgement. The company's statements trace clearly in direction and poorly in quantity. Management says five things it will achieve: FY26 adjusted EPS in line with consensus (about 200p) with a heavier-than-usual Q4; about £2m of cost removed at underperformers; an increasing Patent Box benefit; a return to acquisitions at 3x to 7x EBIT when leverage allows; and a progressive dividend. The first traces to H2 EPS of about 160p, which traces to H2 revenue of £65m to £70m at a 20% to 24% margin, which traces to shipping the orders customers deferred; but the company gives no revenue, margin, tax or interest guidance, so each link is an outside construction and the brokers' constructions disagree (Shore: £121m of revenue and £21.3m of EBIT; Zeus £120m and £20.0m; Investec £130m). Below the operating line the trace is clean: interest is swapped to 2028, the share count drifts about 0.7% a year, and tax is the one uncertain item until the March 2027 note. The external transmission is clear in mechanism and well evidenced in lag, but the company gives no exposure numbers beyond "about two-thirds academic", "US about 25%, half academic" and the revenue-by-currency split, so the Consumer must apply peer-derived elasticities. The milestones that would make the trace observable (Q4 shipments by month, China clearances, the cost run-rate, Armfield and FTT turnaround metrics, the pipeline) are not disclosed.

The internal trace, link by link.

  1. Orders to revenue. Intake −1% year to date at late September; book 17.3 weeks; a four-month lag; management: a "greater-than-usual" Q4. Traceable in direction; the quantity (H2 £69.5m in our case, about £65m in consensus) is an estimate because the book's pound value and deferred share are unpublished.
  2. Revenue to adjusted operating profit. Fixed block about £63m annualised in H1 26; 65% contribution; H2 26 revenue of £69.5m gives about £14m (20%); each £1m of revenue is about £0.65m of EBIT. The best-evidenced link in the case (half-year decrementals of 65%; the 2015 and 2021 to 2023 incrementals) and the one the sell-side underweights: broker sensitivity tables of 4.2p per point of organic revenue imply 25% to 30% drop-through.
  3. EBIT to PBT. Net financial items about £1.6m a half (H1 26: PBT £3.2m on EBIT £4.8m); SONIA swapped at 3.2% to 3.7%; a leverage grid of 1.85% to 3.5%. Predictable to ±£0.2m.
  4. PBT to EPS. Tax 18% guided for FY26 (CFO, 23 September 2026), recurring 20% to 21%; 6.70m shares. The only unclear element is how much of the 18% is prior-period relief, visible only in the March 2027 tax note.
  5. EPS to dividend and leverage. 126p declared for FY26 (interim 36p), cover about 1.5x; adjusted net debt about £41m at December if Q4 ships (1.7x), £44m to £46m if not.

Put together, the H2 EPS of 160p that consensus needs decomposes as revenue of £65m to £70m at a 20% to 24% margin (EBIT £13m to £16.5m), less £1.6m of interest, taxed at 18%, over 6.70m shares: 140p to 180p. Consensus sits in the middle; our 152p sits below it because we put the margin at 20%, not 24%. That is the whole FY26 disagreement with consensus, and it is traceable.

The milestones management named, and whether they can be measured. The £2m of cost actions: traceable to about 1.4 points of H2 margin. "Positive operating leverage when demand recovers": the FY27 incremental margin, for which the company gives no number (we use 42%; consensus implies 24%). Patent Box: more subsidiaries will apply; no number. Underperformers: "operational issues addressed urgently"; no metric; the only public numbers are Companies House (FTT revenue £5.12m, loss £1.21m, FY25). ROTIC 30%: reaffirmed and untraceable to any near year (about 12% FY26, 14% to 15% FY27 to FY28 on our figures); its restatement by the new team is itself a milestone to watch.

The external transmission, mechanism by mechanism, with the lags the evidence supports.

  • US public research funding: appropriations to NIH and NSF obligations and outlays (one to two quarters) to grant awards to laboratory capital orders (two to three quarters: the 2013 sequester, Thermo Fisher's six-month rule, Bruker NMR at 9 to 12 months) to shipments (four months). So the FY2027 appropriations outcome reaches Judges' revenue in H2 2027 at the earliest; the US leg of FY27 is already set by 2026 flows, which were −15% in the latest quarter. Elasticity: US academic is about 12% of revenue, so a 10% move is about £1.5m of revenue, about £1m of EBIT, about 12p of EPS.
  • China: the tax-exemption procedure to customs clearance to shipment of deferred orders. The 2017 Bruker precedent was two quarters; distributors now report six months. China is 10% of revenue; the refill is £4m to £6m in H1 27 in our case; each £1m is about 8p.
  • Semiconductor and battery capex: to eight businesses, mainly Vacuum. Renishaw's first quarter at +47% and Oxford Instruments' Advanced Technologies orders at +125% show the driver is strong; Judges' exposure is perhaps 10% to 15% of revenue, so a 10% move is about £1.5m of revenue, 10p to 12p.
  • Sterling: hedged for about twelve months (the 2025 annual report lists options to sell $41.3m and €9.8m) to annual dollar and euro list-price revisions to margin. Lag two to four quarters. Elasticity: the second set's £7m of EBIT per sustained 10% move is at the high end; Oxford Instruments' published £0.6m per cent scaled to Judges' size is about £0.25m per cent, so the range is £4m to £7m per 10%. Not quantified by the company or any broker.
  • Offshore wind: to geotechnical testing (perhaps 5% of revenue) in Materials Sciences. Fugro's offshore wind survey revenue fell 24%; recovery "from H2 2027" (Jefferies, 6 August 2026). Lag a year or more.
  • Public gas-hydrate budgets (Japan METI/MH21-S, India NGHP, US DOE): to a vessel charter to Geotek coring. Lag from signature to expedition five to twelve months; no signature yet.
  • UK tax policy: Patent Box and corporation tax to the tax rate; employer National Insurance to the fixed block. Known and in the numbers.

Where the trace breaks. In three places: no quantity for the deferred-shipment share of the book, so the Q4 outcome cannot be traced before January; no segment or end-market exposure numbers, so the external elasticities above are peer-derived; no cost-base disclosure, so the incremental margin, the single largest difference between us and consensus, is an inference from half-year accounts. The first set's "black box" verdict stands. I add that the box is transparent on mechanisms and opaque on magnitudes, which is why analysts working from the same information differ by 15% on FY27 (Shore 212p, Investec 243p).

D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically, and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C58

Question asked To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

Judgement. The share price has moved almost entirely idiosyncratically for two years. Over the latest six-month window (28 April to 23 October 2026) the correlation of Judges' daily returns with the equal-weighted basket of its five dashboard peers (Halma, Jenoptik, Renishaw, Tomra, Vaisala) is −0.01, the beta −0.01 and the R-squared 0%; Judges fell 27.5% while the basket rose 7.5%. Over 24 month-end readings the rolling six-month R-squared never exceeded 12% (May 2025) and has been at or below 1% since November 2025. The multiple tells the same story from the other side: Judges' P/E sits at the 9th percentile of its own ten-year history (0th of five years) while Renishaw is at the 54th, Vaisala the 60th, Jenoptik the 34th and Halma the 20th; only Tomra (4th) has de-rated with it, for its own reasons. The multiple is company-driven: it compressed on Judges' three downgrades, its coring slippage, its leadership change and its loss of coverage, not on sector rotation, and it will re-rate, if it does, on Judges' own events. For predictability this nets positive: the stock will respond to the dated company events rather than to the semiconductor cycle, so the forecasts and the share price are tied to the same observables; but no sector tailwind will rescue the multiple if January disappoints, and the sector's own re-rating (test and measurement up about 25% over twelve months) has passed Judges by.

Three qualifications.

  1. Measurement. Daily correlations for a stock trading about $1m a day are biased towards zero by non-synchronous trading and bid-offer noise; a weekly or monthly measure would show higher, though still low, co-movement. The six-month return gap (−27.5% against +7.5%) is not an artefact; it is the de-rating itself.
  2. The peer set is not Judges' demand peer set. Renishaw, Jenoptik and Vaisala re-rated on semiconductor and data-centre capex to which Judges has little exposure; Halma is a regulated-markets compounder. Judges' true co-movers are academic-exposed instrument makers (Bruker, Oxford Instruments' academic side, SDI Group), none of which is in the basket. Even so, the first set's finding that Judges sits "on the wrong side" of a split sector is confirmed: the sector's winners did not pull Judges with them.
  3. History. In late 2024 the correlation was negative (−0.17 to −0.19); in mid-2025 it rose to 0.35 (R-squared 12%) as UK industrials re-rated together after the April 2025 tariff shock; since the November 2024 and July 2025 downgrades it has been near zero. Judges moves with peers only when nothing company-specific is happening, and something company-specific has been happening continuously since November 2024.

What this means for the multiple and the case.

  • The current 14.5x (24-month forward, consensus) or 12.3x (two-year forward on the model's EPS) is Judges' own trough, set by Judges' own news. The model's guideline fair multiple of 17.05x is a company-phase judgement; the peer P/Es (Halma 23x, Renishaw 23x, Vaisala 22x, Jenoptik 14x, Tomra 13x) are not the anchor the market uses for Judges and should not be the memo's anchor either.
  • The re-rating trigger is company-specific and dated: a clean January update and a bracketing March guide. A sector sell-off need not take Judges down further (it did not join the rise), but a UK small-cap liquidity event could, because at $1m a day the price is set by a handful of holders (the founder 9.3%, Odin Global 8.9%).
  • The revision cycle is also company-specific: the dashboard's revision-momentum score for Judges is −6 against +5 to +15 for Halma, Jenoptik, Renishaw and Vaisala.

For the memo's topics #10 and #11. A company-driven multiple supports placing the stock in a bottoming or turning-up phase whose fair multiple is set by its own historical range rather than by peers, and it means that topic #8's "momentum at key peers" carries little signal for Judges.

Back to contents

Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

The Investment Analyst's heading The fourth set: Assessing the extent to which our assessment is reflected in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative

The fourth set as a whole C59 written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Our assessment of the foundations and the forecasts is reflected in current guidance, consensus and narrative in its negatives and not in its positives. Guidance (I-01) is anchored to a 200p FY26 that we think will be missed by 4% to 5% in January 2027 and will then be set conservatively for FY27 in March at a range we expect the company to beat. Consensus (I-02) is cleaned of coring and acquisitions and embeds incremental margins of 18% to 24% and, at Shore, a 23% tax rate, against our 40% to 45% and 20% to 21%, so the first six months lean to small cuts and the following twelve to upgrades of 3% to 7% on FY27 and more on FY28. The narrative (I-03) articulates every structural worry (leadership, dividend cover, the deal pause, Geotek, Q4 risk, the margin ceiling, a bid) and none of the recovery arithmetic (book-to-bill, the incremental margin, the H1 27 EPS multiple, the year-end leverage unwind, sterling's lag), and there is no retrievable investor commentary at all. The gap is therefore shaped like the earnings path: unfavourable for one print, favourable for the next four, and widest for FY28 to FY30, where the market's frame has no acquisitions and no coring and ours has both. The condition on all of it is the 22 January 2027 update: a clean conversion makes the March guide a floor and the July update an upgrade; a slip of more than about £5m resets the sequence by six months and would, on this share's history, take the price down 15% to 25% before the arithmetic reasserts itself. For the memo's topics #4, #7 and #8: guidance is conservative in its inputs and demanding in its execution; proactivity in keeping expectations low is now built into the new team's incentives; the record of raising guidance is absent but the setup for the first raise since 2023 (July 2027) is in place; and momentum in guidance, estimates and price is currently negative-to-flat at Judges and positive at its semiconductor-exposed peers, which is the signature of a stock at the bottom of its own cycle rather than inside the sector's.

Guidance

1 question
I-0154
I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the earnings events over the next 18 months, and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any mid-term target or ambition to be raised, met or reduced?

C54

Question asked How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

Judgement. Over the next 18 months Judges will speak to guidance at five events: the FY26 pre-close update (about 22 January 2027), the FY26 results and first FY27 guidance (24 March 2027), the AGM statement (May or June 2027), the H1 trading update (July 2027) and the interim results (September 2027); the FY27 pre-close (January 2028) falls just inside the window. My probabilities. FY26 guidance (formally 200p to 250p, in practice "in line with market expectations" of about 200p) is met in January with 50%, reduced (an outturn described as "slightly below", around 185p to 194p) with 35%, and exceeded with 15%. The first FY27 guidance in March 2027 brackets the then consensus (about 215p to 220p) with 55%, sits below it with 30% and above it with 15%; the most likely form is a range of roughly 205p to 240p on the same two stated assumptions (no US recovery, no coring), set by a chief executive whose annual bonus is budget-EPS based and who received 5% of maximum for 2025, and by a departing CFO who will want to hand over a clean floor. From July 2027 the probability that FY27 guidance is raised or trading described as "ahead" is about 40%, held "in line" about 40%, reduced about 20%; a raise would come from the H1 27 comparison (revenue about +13%, EPS roughly 2.4 times H1 26's 39p on our numbers), not from a change in the environment. Secondarily, the only mid-term target, 30% ROTIC, will not be met inside the window (about 12% in FY26, 14% to 15% in FY27 to FY28 on our figures) and is more likely to be reframed than reaffirmed: I put 45% on a new mid-term framework (a margin or EPS-growth ambition, or a lower return target) within 18 months, most probably with the new CFO's first results in March 2028, 40% on the 30% simply being repeated and 15% on its being dropped without replacement. A reframing would read as a reduction in ambition and an increase in credibility. The dividend policy of at least 10% a year holds with about 80% probability; the 20% case is a hold, not a cut, triggered by FY26 EPS below about 180p and a flat FY27.

Event by event.

  1. About 22 January 2027, FY26 pre-close. "Met" needs H2 revenue of about £65m or more with the Q4 orders shipped, a December book of 15 to 17 weeks and full-year intake within 2% of FY25. The record is one clean conversion (December 2024) in the last two Q4-weighted years, and management has pre-signalled both the heavy Q4 and the deferral requests, the pattern that preceded the November 2024 and January 2026 shortfalls. "Reduced" means £3m to £6m of Q4 revenue slipping, which the company would most likely describe as "slightly below market expectations" with the revenue "secured for 2027", its 2024 to 2025 wording. "Exceeded" needs the China catch-up to land in December rather than January, or a full-year tax rate below 18%. Because the January update has disappointed against the September consensus in two of the last two years, I weight "reduced" above the second set's 30%. Our own 191p sits at the boundary between "met" and "reduced".

  2. 24 March 2027, FY26 results and FY27 guidance. The record: FY24 guidance set in March 2024 and cut in November; FY25 set in March 2025 and cut in July; FY26 set in January 2026 at 200p to 250p and held. The new chief executive has held a range once (H1 26 at the midpoint). Incentives (a bonus of up to 50% of salary, half on budget EPS; options on a 5% to 6% EPS compound hurdle from a rebased floor), the CFO handover, and the SDI Group and discoverIE precedents of new teams resetting low all point to a conservative bracket. Consensus by then will be 212p to 243p with a median near 219p; a 205p to 240p range would bracket it and cap at about our 234p. Probability the guide's midpoint is above today's consensus: 15% (it needs a strong December intake and a US turn). Probability the FY27 guide is accompanied by a first acquisition: 25%; by a CFO appointment: 40%.

  3. May or June 2027, AGM statement. Historically "in line"; a change in either direction is rare (about 10% each).

  4. July 2027, H1 trading update. The comparison is H1 26 revenue of £55.7m (−21%) and EPS of 39p; our H1 27 is £62.9m and about 95p. If orders held through H1 27, this is the first event since January 2023 at which the company could say "ahead of expectations". The record of July updates is poor (2024 trimmed, 2025 cut), but those followed weak H1 intake; the 2027 setup is the reverse, with intake recovered six to nine months earlier. Raised or "ahead": 40%; in line: 40%; cut: 20% (a second US funding leg through enacted FY2027 cuts, sterling above 1.40 from early 2027, or the China refill failing).

  5. September 2027 interims and the CFO's departure on 23 September. Confirmation of the July message; a new CFO's statement of priorities is more likely in March 2028.

  6. January 2028, FY27 pre-close. On our numbers FY27 EPS of 234p against a March guide of 205p to 240p would be at or near the top; the probability that the FY27 outturn exceeds the March 2027 guide's midpoint is about 60%, which is the "guide low, beat" pattern the first set thought an ex-Halma chief executive might adopt.

What drives each outcome, in order of weight. (i) Q4 2026 shipments: decides January and colours March. (ii) The December 2026 US appropriations outcome and NIH outlays: a stopgap is neutral; enacted cuts of even half the request are the main "reduce" driver for FY27 from July 2027. (iii) China clearance: the H1 27 refill. (iv) Sterling: neutral at 1.34; a sustained move above 1.40 from early 2027 would cost about 4% to 6% of FY27 EBIT after hedges and would most likely appear as caution in July rather than a cut. (v) The semiconductor and battery businesses: small upside. (vi) A first acquisition: about 1% to 2% of EPS per £10m deal in the year of purchase. (vii) A coring contract signature: changes the FY28 or FY29 outlook, not FY27 guidance.

Our assessment against guidance: the gap. FY26: our 191p against the "about 200p" anchor is unfavourable by 4% to 5%, and the market half expects it (the shares fell 20% after a results update that held guidance). FY27: our 234p against a probable March guide of 205p to 240p is favourable: we expect the guide to be beaten and raised in H2 2027. Mid-term: our 15% ROTIC in FY28 against the 30% ambition is unfavourable on paper, but the ambition is not believed (the first set scored it 30). Net: a small negative gap at the first event, a favourable gap at the third and fifth events, and a favourable gap in how the new team is likely to frame its first year.

Back to contents

Sell-side estimates

1 question
I-0258
I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events, and why? Secondarily, for 18 months to three years out?

C58

Question asked How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

Judgement. Consensus is three brokers (Zeus and Shore Capital, corporate brokers without ratings; Investec at Buy), so a "consensus" move is the move of two or three analysts and breadth is a fraction with a denominator of three. FY26 EPS consensus (199p median, range 199p to 206p) is more likely to be cut than raised at the January 2027 update (cut 35%, held 50%, raised 15%), by 3% to 8% in the cut case, with FY27 trimmed 2% to 5% alongside. The March 2027 results will compress the FY27 range towards the guide's midpoint (Investec's 243p down, Shore's 212p up), leaving the median roughly where it is; net breadth over the first six months is therefore more likely flat-to-negative than positive. From July 2027 the direction reverses: if H1 27 prints revenue growth of about 10% or more at a margin above 13%, the three brokers will raise FY27 by 3% to 7% and FY28 by a similar amount, and breadth turns positive for the first time since early 2023. Over the whole 18 months I put the probability that FY27 consensus EPS ends higher than today's 219p at 50%, lower at 30% and within 2% at 20%, with a probability-weighted level of about 225p; and the probability that FY28 consensus (248p, two brokers) is higher in 18 months at 55%, driven by operating-leverage upgrades, any first acquisition (about 15p per £10m at full-year effect) and, if signed, a coring contract (about 55p into the named year). Secondarily, for 18 months to three years out, consensus (FY28 248p; no FY29) is more likely to be upgraded than cut (60/25/15), because it contains neither acquisitions nor coring nor more than 18% to 24% incremental margins, all three of which the company's history and stated intent say will appear.

Why the first six months lean down and the following twelve lean up.

  • The FY26 line items are inconsistent and must be rebased whatever January says: £121m of revenue with £20.3m of EBIT needs an H2 margin of about 24%; the consistent reading is revenue slightly above £121m and EBIT nearer £19m to £20m. Even a "met" January will see FY26 EBIT estimates nudged down while EPS is held through the tax line, and the revision data will record a negative EBIT revision. Revision breadth is 0% over one month and −8% (EPS) and −5% (EBITDA) over three; the direction of travel is still slightly down.
  • The brokers have cut 33% in twelve months and sit at the bottom of the company's range with FY27 to FY28 cleaned of coring and acquisitions; the clearing conditions the first set identified (C-74) are largely met, so the January cut case is bounded at 5% to 10%, not another 25% to 33%.
  • Investec's 243p for FY27 is 11% above the median and will be the first number to move either way. Shore's 212p embeds a 23% tax rate against the CFO's guided 18% and a recurring 20% to 21%; that alone is worth 8p to 10p of upgrade when Shore updates its tax assumption, most likely once the March tax note is published.
  • The upgrade mechanics from July 2027 are arithmetic: consensus FY27 EBIT of £22.0m on £128m implies an incremental margin of about 24%; H1 27 revenue of £62.9m (+13%) at a 13.7% margin gives EBIT of £8.6m against £4.8m, which with a normal H2 points to £22.5m to £23.5m for the year; brokers would move FY27 EBIT to about £23m and EPS to 225p to 235p. The guidance-and-consensus AlphaSense report, citing Zeus (23 September 2026), notes that revenue 5% above £128m would push EPS to the top of the range: the house broker has pre-written the upgrade.
  • Coverage count: the serial-acquirer AlphaSense report finds that broker initiations lag until organic growth is reported, so a fourth broker (or Jefferies' return) is plausible after September 2027, not before. The "100% buy share" is a composition artefact (one rated broker) and the +67-point three-month change is the dropping of Jefferies' Underperform, not conviction.

Targets. Mean 64.50 and median 65.00 (Zeus 70, Investec 72) against a price of 34.25. Targets 90% to 110% above the price have a record of being cut (−10% over three months) while estimates hold; expect further cuts of 10% to 20% in the "reduced" January case and holds otherwise, with no rises until estimates rise from July 2027.

Our assessment against consensus: the gap. FY26: ours 191p against 199p, unfavourable by 4%. FY27: ours 234p against 219p, favourable by 7% (10% against Shore). FY28: ours 283p (259p organic) against 248p, favourable by 14% (4% organic). FY29 to FY30: ours 394p and 394p (292p and 320p organic) against no consensus. The gap is unfavourable at the first print and favourable thereafter, and the favourable part is mostly margin (an incremental 42% against 24%) and tax (20% to 21% against Shore's 23%), both of which the brokers can be expected to adopt once the first half of growth is reported. Net breadth should therefore trace a shallow U: slightly negative to March 2027, positive from July 2027.

Stock market narrative

1 question
I-0365
I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of sell-side commentary and investor commentary, and how well known is each?

C65

Question asked How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

Judgement. The negative dynamics are fully articulated and the positive arithmetic is not. Of the twelve dynamics tested in the narrative AlphaSense report, the sell-side (effectively Zeus and Shore, with Jefferies' notes to April 2026 as the only independent record) articulates nine clearly and has done so since late 2025 or earlier: the leadership transition and CFO departure; the dividend-cover-against-leverage tension (Zeus: "not sustainable"); the acquisition pause and the new deal team; the Geotek slippage (excluded from all models); the Q4 2026 shipment risk and the 160p H2 hurdle; the structural margin changes since FY23 (Jefferies, July 2025: no return to FY23 margins before FY27); the H1 2027 comparison as a sentiment inflection (Zeus, September 2026); the private-equity bid possibility (Shore, October 2026; Rothschild & Co Redburn on eight UK industrial take-outs at a 53% average premium); and the structure of sterling exposure (the 50% dollar, 33% euro split). Three are known only in outline or not at all. Operating leverage is described qualitatively ("very high incremental margins", Shore) but no broker quantifies it, and the models embed 18% to 24% incrementals, the opposite of what the words say. The Patent Box is treated as recurring by all three, with Shore at 23% and Zeus at 20%, and nobody separates a catch-up element or models the longevity beyond FY27. Sterling's transaction lag is quantified by no broker (only the structure is stated), against Oxford Instruments' published £0.6m per cent. Investor commentary (fund managers, AIM commentators, Twitter/X, forums) could not be found by the report in any substantive form; the only direct evidence of the investor narrative is price action (−40.5% over twelve months, relative strength at the 4th percentile of the universe, a 200-day average falling for twelve months, a 20% fall after a results update that held guidance) and the register (the founder 9.3%, Odin Global 8.9%), which together describe capitulation by generalist UK small-cap holders and a narrative of "former compounder turned academic-funding casualty with management change". Relative to our assessment the gap is favourable: the market knows everything that is wrong and does not appear to have done the arithmetic of what happens when orders already booked ship onto a fixed cost base.

Dynamic by dynamic: who articulates it, since when, and the gap to our assessment.

Dynamic Articulated by Since Gap to our assessment
1 Operating leverage and incremental margins Shore qualitatively (March 2026); Zeus "a small revenue change becomes a larger profit change" (January 2025); Jefferies warned extra cost may be needed (September 2026) 2025 Large: the words say high, the models say 18% to 24%; we say 40% to 45%. The least-priced positive
2 Order-book conversion and Q4 risk Zeus, Shore and the company; the 160p H2 hurdle widely quoted July 2026 Small: fully known; the market half expects a slip
3 Patent Box recurrence All three treat it as recurring; Shore 23%, Zeus 20%; none separates a catch-up July 2026 Moderate: our 20% to 21% recurring is above Zeus and below Shore; the catch-up (about 5p to 7p) is unknown to all; Shore's 23% is 8p to 10p of latent upgrade
4 Geotek slippage and the 50p sensitivity Zeus, Shore, Jefferies 2024 None on FY26 to FY27; our FY29 placement, and the "slips again" risk, are in no model
5 Dividend against deleveraging Zeus ("not sustainable"), Shore, the company September 2026 Small on the fact; the quantification (the dividend takes 70% to 78% of free cash flow; a twelve-month delay to the flywheel) is ours alone
6 Acquisition pause and the new team Jefferies (June 2025), Shore (July 2025), Zeus 2025 Small on the fact; nobody models a restart; our £47m is unpriced
7 Loss-making subsidiaries Jefferies (March 2025), the company's impairments, Companies House for FTT 2025 Moderate: known as narrative, unsized by anyone; our 25p to 35p of repair potential is in no model
8 Sterling transaction exposure The structure, from the annual report; no broker quantifies the lag Always Large: our £4m to £7m per 10% is in no model and no note
9 Leadership, CFO, auditor All, extensively November 2025 None
10 Structural margin ceiling Jefferies (July 2025); Shore implicitly (FY28 margin 17.6%) 2025 Small, and in our favour: the sell-side sits below us (17.6% against 18% to 20%), so the ceiling is over-articulated
11 The H1 2027 comparison Zeus (September 2026: a "sharp EPS recovery" should re-rate the stock) September 2026 Moderate: the direction is known; the arithmetic (EPS 39p to about 95p, revenue +13%) is published by nobody
12 A private-equity or strategic bid Shore (October 2026), Rothschild & Co Redburn (September 2026), an SDI expert call 2026 None; arguably over-articulated for a company with a 9.3% founder-chair

Two dynamics the report did not test and nobody articulates: the book-to-bill arithmetic (FY26 intake roughly flat while FY26 revenue is −14%, so the FY27 revenue base is already largely written); and the year-end working-capital unwind (June leverage of 2.1x overstates December by about 0.4x), which means the "2.1x" that dominates the narrative is a seasonal high.

Investor commentary. Absent from the sources: no fund-manager letters, forum threads or Twitter/X commentary were retrievable, and the only named outside view is the former SDI Group chief executive's praise of the model (expert call, 24 September 2026), which is a peer's view, not a holder's. What can be inferred: three years of downgrades, coverage dropped at Underperform, a 40% fall and the lowest relative strength in the universe describe a holder base that has given up on the compounder narrative; Odin Global's 8.9% and the founder's 9.3% describe a concentrated, patient core. The absence of commentary is itself informative for a £230m AIM stock: nobody is telling the story, which is consistent with a multiple at its own ten-year floor while the sector re-rates.

How well known are the dynamics 18 months to three years out. The sell-side's FY28 (two brokers, 248p) is an extrapolation with no acquisitions, no coring and a 17.6% margin; the narrative for 2028 to 2029 does not exist beyond "the model is intact" (Shore) and "a bid is possible". Our organic FY29 of 292p and the two-layer view (organic against deals-plus-coring) are not in the market's frame. The gap for the longer horizon is wide and favourable, but it will not close before the first acquisition and the first coring signature make the second layer visible.

Where I differ from the first set. C-22 expected the H1 2027 recovery to be "highly novel"; the evidence shows Zeus has already written that the H1 27 comparison should re-rate the stock, so the direction is known to the few who read the house broker. The novelty will be the size (EPS more than doubling) and the incremental margin it reveals. The gap is in the arithmetic, not in the idea.

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB, AIM: JDG): a UK buy-and-build group of about 25 autonomous niche scientific-instrument manufacturers selling to academic, research-institute and industrial laboratories worldwide, reported in two segments, Materials Sciences (including Geotek coring and core logging, Armfield, Fire Testing Technology, GDS) and Vacuum (including UHV Design, Oxford Cryosystems, Sircal, Scientifica, Korvus, CoolLED), with about 85% of revenue exported, about 50% of revenue in US dollars and 33% in euros against a largely sterling cost base, and a 20-year record of acquisitions at 3x to 7x EBIT.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what do the individual statutory accounts filed at Companies House for the financial year to 31 December 2025 (filed from mid-2026) by its UK operating subsidiaries (including Geotek Ltd, Armfield Ltd, Fire Testing Technology Ltd, Scientifica Ltd, GDS Instruments Ltd, UHV Design Ltd, Oxford Cryosystems Ltd, Sircal Instruments Ltd, Korvus Technology Ltd, CoolLED Ltd, Deben UK Ltd, Quorum Technologies Ltd, Thermal Hazard Technology Ltd and Henniker Scientific Ltd) show about each subsidiary's revenue, operating profit or loss, headcount, research and development spending and dividends paid to the parent in 2025 against 2024; which subsidiaries grew and which declined; how concentrated group profit is in Geotek and the three or four largest contributors; how large in revenue and in operating losses the loss-making or sub-scale businesses are as a share of the group; and what the directors' reports say about 2026 trading, order intake, cost actions and new products at each? Separately, what do the directors' reports and accounts of the named education-equipment competitors TecQuipment Ltd and GUNT Gerätebau GmbH, and of Fire Testing Technology's competitors, show about 2025 revenue and margin trends in those niches?

Request: OWN-2

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what have the companies that report between October 2026 and February 2027 and share its end markets said about the fourth quarter of 2026 and the outlook for 2027, specifically: Oxford Instruments' interim results (November 2026), Halma's interim results (19 November 2026), SDI Group's interim results (December 2026), Renishaw's first-half results (February 2027), Bruker's third-quarter results (November 2026) and fourth-quarter results (February 2027), JEOL's second-quarter results (November 2026), Shimadzu, Thermo Fisher, Waters and Agilent, on (a) US academic and government instrument demand, National Institutes of Health grant notices and Treasury outlays for October to December 2026, and the outcome of the FY2027 appropriations process for the National Institutes of Health and National Science Foundation; (b) whether Chinese orders deferred during the 2026 change to the research and development import tax-exemption procedure have begun shipping, how long customs clearance is taking, and whether deferred orders have been cancelled; (c) European academic and OEM instrument demand; (d) offshore-wind geotechnical survey activity (Fugro's fourth-quarter 2026 statements); and (e) the effect of the sterling exchange rate on UK exporters' reported margins and any published sensitivities? What do these statements imply for the shipment of a Q4-weighted order book by a UK niche instrument exporter in December 2026?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Judges Scientific plc (JDG-GB), what is the current investor narrative outside the sell-side: who are the largest shareholders (from the company's disclosures and TR-1 notifications since January 2026, including David Cicurel's holding of about 9.3% and Odin Global's of about 8.9% disclosed in September 2026), which holders have added or sold during 2026, what have fund managers, investment trusts and UK small-cap commentators (including Investors' Chronicle, Shares Magazine, Citywire, Stockopedia and fund manager letters or interviews) written about the company since the January 2026 trading update, what is being said on investor forums and Twitter/X, is there any disclosed short interest, and which of the following do these sources discuss or ignore: the fixed-cost operating leverage of the group's cost base and the incremental margin on recovering volume; the conversion of the order book and the risk of fourth-quarter 2026 shipments slipping; the UK Patent Box's effect on the tax rate; the Geotek coring expedition's deferral to no earlier than 2028; the progressive dividend against the restart of acquisitions; the loss-making subsidiaries; sterling transaction exposure; the leadership and CFO transition; the first-half 2027 comparison against first-half 2026 revenue of £55.7m and adjusted EPS of 39.0p; and the possibility of a bid? How does this compare with investor commentary on SDI Group, Oxford Instruments and Renishaw over the same period?
